# Supplement 2 – Human Faiths

This supplement contains a list of many of the human faiths practised across the Flanaess and beyond. Each entry briefly describes the deity or faith and, where applicable, provides the rules for its specialty priests, as well as notes concerning its shamans and paladins. Guidelines are also provided for clerics who serve the various human pantheons.

Other character classes may choose to become adherents of a specific deity, faith, or pantheon, but gain no mechanical benefits or hindrances from doing so. Such affiliations are instead another tool for developing and portraying the character.

Each human culture is associated with its own council of gods, but these pantheons are constructs of human society rather than reflections of how the gods themselves organize or interact. A deity may therefore be claimed as a member of several pantheons, perhaps under different names or aspects, while another may attract worshippers among a people whose pantheon does not recognize that deity at all. For their part, most human deities are largely indifferent to the culture, ancestry, or nationality of their worshippers, concerning themselves instead with the nature and sincerity of their devotion.

These human-constructed associations may also be shaped by political power. A kingdom, empire, or theocracy may promote a particular group of gods as the recognized pantheon of its people, elevate worship of one deity above the others, suppress the worship of rival powers, or deliberately alter how the relationships between the gods are understood. Such official interpretations may be deeply influential within the region without necessarily being accepted elsewhere, and they should not be assumed to reflect the actual relationships, alliances, or rivalries of the gods themselves.

This supplement also describes the **Old Faith**, which does not worship individual deities, though it may recognize the existence or influence of gods belonging to the various pantheons. The Old Faith is followed by the great majority of druids and is widely practised in rural communities throughout the Flanaess.

The **Rhennee** are not represented by a formal pantheon in this supplement, as their culture generally has no organized religion and claims no gods as its own. Most Rhennee nevertheless revere the elemental force of water, though their relationship with it is not normally expressed as a formal faith. The few Rhennee who do adopt an established religion are often drawn to deities associated with fate, travel, or luck.  

> **The following tables present the deities** **traditionally** **recognized as members of each cultural pantheon. They do not represent the complete range of gods worshipped by that people, nor do they imply that the listed gods recognize one another as members of a single divine hierarchy.**

# Chapter 1: The Pantheons and their Clerics

## The Baklunish Pantheon

The Baklunish gods embody the principles by which the Baklunish understand the world: fate and uncertainty, faith and duty, mastery of the self, harmony with the world, the necessities of life, and the exchanges that bind communities together. They are not a perfectly harmonious family, nor do they represent a single moral philosophy. Each god teaches a particular way of understanding the relationship between mortals and the forces that shape their lives.

Baklunish religion consequently places considerable emphasis upon **knowing one's place within a greater order**. Istus represents the web of fate; Al'Akbar the obligations created by faith and duty; Xan Yae and Zuoken the mastery of mind, body, and spirit; Geshtai the sustaining waters of the world; Mouqol the reciprocal exchanges through which people obtain what they need; and the other gods govern further aspects of nature, society, and the human experience.

Baklunish people do not necessarily devote themselves exclusively to one deity. A farmer may honor Geshtai for water, Mouqol when selling a harvest, Al'Akbar when swearing an oath, and Istus when facing an uncertain future. Particular gods may receive greater personal devotion, but recognition of several divine powers is ordinary religious practice.

The gods are understood to be real and powerful, but they are not expected to solve every mortal problem. Their worshippers are expected to fulfill their own obligations, exercise discipline and judgment, and make use of the gifts and opportunities the gods provide. **Faith is demonstrated through conduct.**

### Clerics of the Baklunish Gods

Clerics of the Baklunish pantheon serve the pantheon as a whole rather than devoting themselves exclusively to a single god. They preserve the rites and traditions of the faith, maintain temples and shrines, teach the principles shared by the gods, and determine which deity should be honored in a particular circumstance.

A generalist cleric is not expected to treat every god as equally important in every matter. Instead, the cleric understands the different concerns of the pantheon and knows which god's favor, guidance, or protection is appropriate. A priest might invoke Geshtai before a journey through the desert, Mouqol when witnessing a contract, Al'Akbar when administering an oath, Istus when seeking guidance concerning an uncertain future, or Xan Yae when teaching discipline and self-mastery.

The priesthood also serves as an important bridge between the traditions of different Baklunish communities. Clerics preserve religious knowledge, settle disputes concerning custom and ritual, and ensure that local practices remain compatible with the broader faith.

#### Dogma

The gods are real, and their domains shape the world in which mortals live. Know them, respect them, and understand what each demands.

Do not expect the gods to accomplish what mortals are capable of accomplishing themselves. Faith requires action. Fulfill your obligations, master yourself, respect the natural world, honor legitimate agreements, and accept that some things lie beyond mortal control.

Seek harmony rather than needless conflict. When opposing interests can be reconciled, seek reconciliation. When duty requires resistance, resist with discipline. When fate cannot be changed, meet it with dignity; when a better outcome can be achieved through mortal action, do not surrender merely because the future is uncertain.

Preserve the knowledge and traditions of the faith, but distinguish true principle from empty custom. The gods provide guidance and opportunity; mortals remain responsible for what they do with them.

#### Duties and Obligations

A generalist cleric of the Baklunish pantheon is expected to:

- Maintain temples, shrines, holy places, and the rites associated with the gods.
    
- Teach the nature, portfolios, and proper observances of the Baklunish gods.
    
- Determine which deity should be honored when circumstances call for divine aid or recognition.
    
- Preserve religious traditions, sacred histories, genealogies, and rituals.
    
- Maintain the sanctity of oaths, contracts, and other legitimate obligations.
    
- Provide religious counsel during births, marriages, deaths, journeys, disputes, and other important transitions.
    
- Protect sacred places and objects from desecration, theft, or misuse.
    
- Encourage discipline, self-mastery, and responsible conduct among the faithful.
    
- Protect communities and aid those whose survival depends upon water, food, safe travel, or legitimate trade.
    
- Resolve religious disputes when different customs or interpretations come into conflict.
    
- Warn communities when their actions risk offending a deity or violating an important religious obligation.
    
- Offer appropriate prayers and sacrifices when divine intervention is genuinely required.
    
- Never claim that personal desires or political interests are divine commands.
    

#### Adventuring Clerics

Baklunish clerics may adventure when circumstances carry them beyond the settled community or require them to act where ordinary priests cannot. Some accompany caravans, expeditions, or pilgrims; others seek lost temples, recover sacred objects, investigate supernatural disturbances, or travel to places where the traditions of the faith have been forgotten.

An adventuring cleric may be called upon to negotiate between communities, protect travelers, recover knowledge, investigate an omen, locate a source of water, settle a dispute, or confront a supernatural threat. Such priests are expected to represent the faith wherever they travel and to recognize the appropriate deity in unfamiliar circumstances.

The wandering cleric also encounters peoples whose customs differ from those of the Baklunish. Such encounters are opportunities to learn as well as to teach. The priest should distinguish genuine violations of divine principle from practices that are merely foreign or unfamiliar.

Above all, an adventuring cleric carries the responsibilities of the priesthood beyond the temple. The gods may provide guidance, power, and opportunity, but **the priest is still responsible for choosing how those gifts are used.**

## **The** **Flan Pantheon**

The Flan pantheon is one of the oldest religious traditions of the Flanaess. Its gods encompass the land and its seasons, birth and death, community and justice, love and conflict, fortune and misfortune, and the many other forces that have shaped Flan life since antiquity. Unlike the more centralized religions of many later peoples, Flan worship has traditionally been local, practical, and closely connected to the land.

The Flan gods do not form a single organized divine hierarchy. Some are closely associated with particular communities or traditions, while others are known throughout the Flanaess. Their worship varies considerably from one region to another, and a god important to one Flan community may receive little attention in another. Local customs, ancestral traditions, and the needs of the community often determine which gods receive the greatest honour.

Many Flan traditions are also closely associated with the **Old Faith**, the ancient religious tradition that recognizes the natural world and its spirits as sacred without necessarily identifying those powers with particular gods. The Old Faith and the worship of the Flan gods often coexist within the same community, and the distinction between the two is not always important to ordinary worshippers. Druids in particular generally belong to the Old Faith rather than serving a single deity.

The Flan gods are not uniformly benevolent or even morally compatible. Pelor and Mayaheine may be invoked for protection and healing, while Nerull and Incabulos represent death and calamity. Ralishaz embodies the unpredictable nature of fortune, and Iuz represents a profoundly destructive power despite his place within the traditional Flan religious heritage. The existence of a god within the pantheon does not mean that the god is loved, trusted, or imitated.

Flan religion consequently places considerable emphasis upon **living in proper relationship with the world rather than imposing a single doctrine upon it**. The land must be respected, traditions preserved, communities protected, the dead remembered, and the natural cycles of life accepted. The wise worshipper learns the customs of the community, understands the powers that surround it, and does not assume that every problem can be solved through force or command.

#### Worship of the Gods

Most Flan do not devote themselves exclusively to a single deity. Particular gods receive attention according to circumstance, occupation, community, and personal need. A farmer may honour Beory or Berei, a hunter may honour Obad-Hai or Ehlonna, a mourner may seek the guidance of Nerull or the comfort of Pelor, and a person seeking reconciliation may pray to Rao or Zodal.

Such worship does not necessarily exclude the other gods. A household may maintain several traditional observances, while a community may call upon different gods at different stages of the agricultural year or during different ceremonies. Local traditions often preserve rites whose origins are so ancient that their original meanings are no longer certain.

The Flan also recognize that the spirits of particular places, ancestors, and natural features may deserve respect even when they are not identified with one of the great gods. A sacred spring, ancient grove, mountain, or burial ground may have its own traditions and taboos. Ignoring such customs merely because a greater god is being honoured is considered foolish.

The result is a religious tradition that is **accumulative rather than exclusive**. New gods, local spirits, ancient customs, and regional interpretations may exist alongside one another without requiring the worshipper to abandon everything that came before.

The gods are honoured, the spirits are respected, and the land is preserved. The oldest lesson of Flan religion is that **mortals are part of the world, not masters of it**.

### Clerics of the Flan Gods

Most Flan clerics serve the pantheon as a whole rather than devoting themselves exclusively to a single deity. They maintain the traditional rites of their communities, preserve the customs handed down by their ancestors, and act as intermediaries between mortals and the divine powers that influence their lives.

Flan religion is ancient and decentralized, and no single temple hierarchy speaks for all Flan. A cleric's responsibilities are therefore shaped as much by local tradition as by the particular god whose aid is being sought. One community may place great importance upon Beory and the spirits of the land, another upon Rao or Allitur, while another may maintain ancient observances dedicated to gods that have little importance elsewhere.

A cleric is not expected to approve of every god in the pantheon. Some gods are benevolent, some indifferent, some dangerous, and some actively opposed to mortal well-being. The presence of a god within the traditional pantheon does not require the cleric to admire or imitate that god. Nor does honouring a god during an appropriate rite necessarily constitute personal devotion.

The central responsibility of the pantheon priesthood is therefore **to preserve the proper relationship between the community, the gods, the spirits, and the land**. The cleric knows which traditions apply to a particular circumstance, performs the appropriate rites, seeks divine assistance when it is needed, and warns the community when an ancient custom or sacred place is being neglected.

The Flan priesthood exists alongside the Old Faith rather than replacing it. Clerics serve the gods and preserve the religious traditions associated with them. Druids of the Old Faith serve the natural order and the spirits of the land. The two traditions frequently overlap in practice, particularly in communities where the distinction between divine and natural powers is ancient or poorly defined, but they remain distinct religious traditions.

#### Dogma

Remember what the ancestors knew.

The land sustains those who respect it and punishes those who abuse it. Take what is needed, give back where you can, and do not destroy what you do not understand.

Know the gods, but do not assume that every god is your friend. Honour each according to its nature and the traditions of the community. Seek the aid of those willing to help, appease those who can be appeased, and resist those who threaten the people.

Respect the spirits of places and the customs associated with them. An ancient grove, burial ground, spring, or standing stone may carry obligations older than any living person remembers.

Do not discard an old tradition merely because its origin has been forgotten. The wisdom of the ancestors is often preserved in customs whose purpose is no longer obvious.

At the same time, do not confuse tradition with wisdom merely because it is old. A cleric must understand the purpose of a custom before demanding that others follow it blindly.

Above all, remember that **mortals are part of the land, not masters of it**.

#### Duties and Obligations

A cleric of the Flan gods is expected to:

- maintain the traditional rites of the local community and pantheon;
    
- preserve and teach the customs, myths, and religious knowledge handed down by the ancestors;
    
- determine which gods, spirits, or traditional observances are appropriate to particular circumstances;
    
- perform prayers, offerings, sacrifices, blessings, funerary rites, and other customary ceremonies;
    
- preserve sacred places and warn the community against violating ancient taboos;
    
- respect the spirits and powers associated with particular natural features and places;
    
- protect the community from supernatural threats;
    
- seek the aid of benevolent gods when their intervention would benefit the people;
    
- appease or ward off hostile or dangerous powers when necessary;
    
- preserve the traditions associated with births, marriages, deaths, harvests, hunting, and other important events;
    
- cooperate with druids and other keepers of the Old Faith when their responsibilities overlap;
    
- settle disputes concerning religious customs and obligations when possible;
    
- discourage needless destruction of the natural world;
    
- preserve the religious traditions of the Flan without requiring every community to observe them in exactly the same manner.
    

A cleric is not required to worship every god equally, nor to approve of every god. **Recognition is not devotion.** Nor is the cleric required to treat every local tradition as universally binding. Flan religion is rooted in particular communities and places, and wise priests understand which customs belong to their people and which have broader significance.

#### Adventuring Clerics

A cleric of the Flan gods may leave the community when circumstances require it. Some seek lost religious knowledge or sacred objects; others investigate ancient ruins, recover relics, protect isolated settlements, or deal with supernatural threats that cannot be handled locally.

Others adventure because the old traditions have led them beyond the boundaries of their own community. An ancient shrine may have been disturbed, a sacred site threatened, a forgotten spirit angered, or a relic of the ancestors uncovered. The cleric may be called upon to determine what has happened and what obligations have been awakened by it.

Flan clerics are also well suited to journeys into places where the natural and supernatural worlds overlap. They may seek the counsel of spirits, consult ancient traditions, or cooperate with druids and shamans when confronting dangers that cannot be understood through ordinary religious knowledge alone.

**A cleric travelling with adventurers may therefore carry several kinds of offerings, know rites dedicated to many different gods, and maintain customs whose importance is not immediately apparent to companions from younger cultures. Such practices are not considered contradictory. The wise cleric preserves what was handed down, understands why it matters, and knows when the old ways must be brought to bear.**

## **The** **Oeridian Pantheon**

The Oeridian pantheon is a broad and diverse collection of gods whose interests encompass nearly every aspect of Oeridian life. Its present form reflects the history of a conquering people whose migrations carried them across the Flanaess, absorbing other peoples and their traditions along the way. The Oeridians brought their own gods with them, but as their influence spread, they also adopted deities worshipped by the peoples they encountered, conquered, or settled among. Over generations, many of these gods became part of the wider Oeridian religious tradition.

The result is a pantheon with no single greater god to call its own. No deity stands above the others as the undisputed patron of the Oeridian people, and no universal divine authority defines what it means to be Oeridian. The pantheon instead encompasses gods with differing interests, temperaments, and moral outlooks. Some uphold law, justice, and legitimate authority; others champion freedom, individual achievement, or the pursuit of pleasure. Some protect communities and sustain the natural world, while others embody tyranny, destruction, or the exploitation of the weak.

The Oeridian gods are not united by a common morality or purpose. Their relationships reflect their own interests and personalities, encompassing alliances, rivalries, friendships, and enduring enmities. Their followers may find common cause in particular circumstances, but shared membership in the pantheon does not guarantee agreement. A cleric who serves Heironeous may have little in common with one who serves Hextor, despite both being priests of Oeridian gods.

The pantheon is consequently not a single faith with a uniform doctrine. **Oeridian religion is united more by its shared history and accumulated traditions than by any common creed.** The gods provide patrons for nearly every undertaking, but their followers must determine which deity's teachings apply to their own lives and obligations.

#### Worship of the Gods

Most Oeridians direct their principal devotion towards one or more particular deities, according to their occupation, circumstances, convictions, and community traditions. A farmer may honour the Velaeri for favourable weather and a good harvest; a merchant may seek Zilchus's favour; a soldier may pray to Heironeous or Hextor; and an artist may look to Olidammara or another deity whose interests accord with their craft.

Honouring one god does not necessarily mean rejecting the others. Households and communities commonly observe customary rites associated with several deities, particularly at important occasions. A wedding, funeral, planting, harvest, voyage, or military departure may call for different religious observances, even where the community has a principal patron. Such practices need not represent equal devotion to every god involved. They reflect the recognition that different powers have different interests.

The diversity of the pantheon is also evident in its origins. Some deities belong to the older Oeridian religious tradition, while others were adopted from neighbouring or conquered peoples. Their inclusion does not mean that every distinction between traditions has disappeared. Some gods retain customs, congregations, and religious identities that differ from those of other Oeridian faiths. In regions where several peoples have lived together for generations, worship may reflect a mixture of inherited traditions rather than a single, uniform practice.

The absence of a supreme Oeridian god has important consequences. No single church can claim universal authority over the pantheon, and no one deity's priesthood can speak for all Oeridians. Religious disputes must be settled through the authority of the individual faiths involved, customary practice, local institutions, or the judgement of those concerned. The pantheon provides a shared religious inheritance, not a unified religious government.

### Clerics of the Oeridian Gods

Oeridian clerics generally serve a particular deity and uphold that god's teachings, rites, and obligations. Their responsibilities vary according to the nature of their patron. A priest of Heironeous may defend the innocent and uphold just authority, while a priest of Zilchus may oversee commercial agreements and promote honest dealings. A priest of Zagyg may pursue occult knowledge through methods that other clergy regard as bizarre, while a priest of Hextor may serve authority through conquest and coercion.

A cleric is not expected to approve of every god in the pantheon or to regard every other priesthood as an ally. The Oeridian tradition accommodates conflicting ideals, and the obligations of one faith may directly oppose those of another. A cleric's primary religious responsibility is to their own deity, not to an imagined common doctrine shared by all Oeridian gods.

The broader pantheon nevertheless provides opportunities for cooperation. Clerics may work together when their gods share interests, when their communities face a common threat, or when customary observances require several priesthoods to participate. Such cooperation does not erase doctrinal differences, nor does it require a cleric to abandon the obligations of their own faith.

**The central responsibility of an Oeridian cleric is to uphold the relationship between their deity and the faithful, while recognizing that other gods and their priesthoods have their own claims.** A cleric may acknowledge another deity's authority over a particular matter without accepting that deity's teachings as their own. Recognition of another faith is not the same as submission to it.

#### Dogma

The gods are many, their purposes diverse, and their teachings not always in agreement. Know the deity you serve and understand the obligations that follow from that choice.

Preserve the rites and traditions handed down by your faith. Teach the faithful what their god requires, and do not substitute personal preference for divine instruction. Honour the customs appropriate to important occasions, and recognize that other gods may have legitimate claims upon those who participate in them.

Do not mistake the pantheon for a single church. The gods have different interests, and their followers are not obliged to pretend otherwise. Cooperate with other faithful when your duties permit it, but do not neglect your own obligations merely to preserve appearances of harmony.

Remember that the Oeridian gods come from many traditions. A deity's origins do not invalidate its place in the pantheon, nor does inclusion in the pantheon mean that every community must worship it in the same manner. Preserve the traditions of your own faith while recognizing the religious inheritance shared by the wider Oeridian world.

Above all, **serve your deity faithfully, but do not presume that your god speaks for all the others.**

#### Duties and Obligations

A cleric of the Oeridian gods is expected to:

- maintain the traditional rites and observances of their own faith;
    
- preserve and teach the customs, myths, and obligations associated with their deity;
    
- instruct the faithful in the principles and duties of their religion;
    
- perform appropriate prayers, offerings, and ceremonies at important occasions;
    
- preserve temples, shrines, sacred objects, and religious records;
    
- uphold the responsibilities their deity places upon them, whether these concern justice, authority, trade, warfare, agriculture, knowledge, or other matters;
    
- recognize the religious customs of other Oeridian communities where circumstances require it;
    
- cooperate with other priesthoods when their duties and their deity's interests permit;
    
- settle disputes concerning the rites and obligations of their own faith, and seek appropriate counsel when disputes involve other priesthoods;
    
- warn the faithful when their actions risk violating the obligations of their religion;
    
- protect their community from supernatural threats in accordance with their deity's teachings;
    
- avoid claiming that personal desires or political decisions are divine commands without good reason;
    
- preserve their faith's traditions without requiring the faithful to adopt the teachings or imitate the nature of every other god in the pantheon.
    

A cleric is not required to worship every god equally, nor to approve of every deity recognized within the Oeridian tradition. **Membership in the pantheon is not a universal profession of faith.** A priest may respect another god's established claims while opposing that god's purposes, provided they do not neglect the obligations of their own deity.

#### Adventuring Clerics

An Oeridian cleric may leave their community to spread their faith, recover sacred objects, investigate supernatural disturbances, protect the faithful, or fulfil a command given by their deity or church. Others travel as chaplains, healers, scholars, emissaries, or agents of the institutions their priesthood supports.

The pantheon's breadth means that an adventuring cleric may encounter unfamiliar customs and religious traditions even in lands where Oeridian gods are widely worshipped. A deity may have a different standing in another region, a local congregation may preserve older rites, or a god adopted from another people may be more prominent there than among the cleric's own community. The cleric should not assume that familiar names imply identical practices.

Adventures may also bring clerics into contact with the interests of other gods. A shrine may have been desecrated, an ancient obligation forgotten, or a conflict between rival priesthoods may threaten the surrounding community. The cleric must determine how such matters affect their own faith and what response their deity's teachings require. There is no universal Oeridian authority to resolve every dispute, and no guarantee that the gods involved will agree upon a satisfactory outcome.

**An adventuring cleric may cooperate with priests of other Oeridian gods without sharing their beliefs, just as they may oppose another priesthood without denying that its deity belongs to the pantheon.** Such decisions are matters of religious duty, circumstance, and personal judgement rather than adherence to a common Oeridian creed.

#### Clerics of the Pantheon

Some clerics serve the Oeridian pantheon collectively rather than devoting themselves exclusively to a single deity. These generalist clerics maintain shared religious traditions, oversee customary observances involving several gods, and help their communities navigate the pantheon's many overlapping claims.

Their responsibilities are often practical. A village cleric may maintain several shrines, conduct rites for planting and harvest, arrange appropriate ceremonies for births and funerals, and recognize when a matter requires the attention of a deity's own priesthood. In larger settlements, generalist clerics may preserve religious records, advise civic authorities on customary observances, or coordinate ceremonies involving several congregations.

Such a cleric is not a supreme representative of the Oeridian gods, nor an authority empowered to overrule their individual priesthoods. Their role is to maintain the community's relationship with the pantheon as a whole, not to impose a uniform doctrine upon it. They must understand the broad interests and customary observances of the gods, while recognizing the limits of their own authority.

A generalist cleric may be especially valuable in communities shaped by the Oeridians' long history of migration and conquest. They can help preserve a shared religious tradition while accommodating local practices and deities whose worship predates Oeridian settlement. This work requires an understanding that incorporation into the pantheon does not necessarily erase a god's origins or the distinct identity of its followers.

The generalist's task is therefore one of **maintaining religious continuity amid diversity**. They preserve the common inheritance of the Oeridian pantheon without pretending that its gods are united in purpose or that their followers must be united in belief.

## **The** **Olman Pantheon**

The Olman pantheon is an ancient and dangerous collection of gods whose powers encompass nearly every aspect of Olman life. Its members are not united by a common morality or purpose. Some provide rain, sunlight, fertility, knowledge, or protection; others embody death, war, fear, disease, darkness, or destruction. Even the gods who aid mortals may demand terrible sacrifices or strict obedience in return.

The gods are not distant ideals. They are powers that can intervene directly in the mortal world, and Olman religion is consequently concerned less with deciding which gods are good than with understanding which god must be honoured, appeased, or resisted in a given circumstance. A farmer needs rain regardless of whether the god who provides it is kind. A warrior cannot simply ignore the god of death because he finds that god abhorrent. A community may honour one god while taking precautions against another.

The gods themselves are divided by rivalries and ancient conflicts. Quetzalcoatl and Tezcatlipoca are particularly bitter rivals, and their servants carry that rivalry into mortal affairs. Other gods pursue their own interests with little concern for the welfare of mortals. Divine conflicts may therefore become political, military, or religious conflicts among Olman communities.

There is little expectation that the gods should agree about what constitutes proper behaviour. A quality praised by one god may be condemned by another. Quetzalcoatl values order, administration, and creation, while Tezcatlipoca teaches that wealth is power and rewards cunning and betrayal. Tlaloc demands sacrifice for rain; Mictlantecuhtli claims authority over the dead; Camazotz embodies both the useful bats that protect crops and the horrors that emerge from darkness. The Olman religious tradition accepts these contradictions rather than attempting to resolve them.

#### Worship of the Gods

Most Olman do not devote themselves exclusively to a single god. Different gods are honoured according to circumstance. Farmers make offerings to Tlaloc for rain and to the appropriate powers for healthy crops. Warriors honour the gods of battle and death. Artisans and metalworkers may invoke Quetzalcoatl. Funerary rites acknowledge Mictlantecuhtli. Other gods may be invoked when their particular powers or interests become relevant.

This does not mean that all gods are equally beloved. A person may honour a god because the god is powerful, necessary, or dangerous without admiring that god's nature. Sacrifice may be an expression of devotion, but it may also be an attempt to secure favour or prevent divine anger. **Recognition is not devotion.**

The demands of the darker gods are nevertheless real. A community that refuses to acknowledge Tlaloc may suffer drought; those who defy Mictlantecuhtli may find death itself opposed to them; those who offend Tezcatlipoca may discover that political enemies have acquired unexpected divine assistance. The Olman therefore maintain rites not only for gods they love, but also for powers they fear.

Quetzalcoatl represents an important exception. He is the most openly constructive of the major Olman gods and the principal divine patron of order, administration, art, and craft. His rivalry with Tezcatlipoca nevertheless demonstrates that even the most benevolent divine power exists within a pantheon defined by conflict.

Olman religion is therefore fundamentally concerned with maintaining the proper relationship between mortals and the supernatural powers that govern their world. The gods need not be good for their worship to be necessary. Mortals survive by knowing which powers sustain them, which demand payment, which can be bargained with, and which must never be allowed to gain an advantage.

## Clerics of the Olman Gods

Most Olman clerics serve the pantheon as a whole rather than dedicating themselves exclusively to one god. They preserve the rites of the gods, conduct sacrifices and ceremonies, maintain temples and sacred places, and act as intermediaries between mortal communities and the divine powers that govern their lives.

An Olman cleric is not expected to approve of every god in the pantheon. The gods are too numerous, too powerful, and too different for such an expectation to be practical. A cleric may revere Quetzalcoatl while fearing Tezcatlipoca, depend upon Tlaloc while despising his demands, or honour Mictlantecuhtli without welcoming death. **Recognition is not devotion.** Acknowledging a god's power does not require admiration or imitation.

The central responsibility of an Olman cleric is to maintain the proper relationship between the community and the gods. This includes knowing which god should be honoured for a particular purpose, what sacrifices or rites are required, and what behaviour might provoke divine displeasure. A cleric may appeal to a benevolent god for aid, bargain with an indifferent one, appease a hostile power, or warn the community against attracting the attention of a dangerous god.

The cleric's duty is not simply to make the gods pleased with mortals. Some gods cannot be pleased without terrible cost, and some actively seek suffering, conflict, or death. In such cases, the cleric must determine how much of the god's demand must be satisfied, how its consequences can be contained, and how the community can survive its attention.

#### Dogma

The gods are real, powerful, and divided. Know them.

Give each god the honours that are properly due, whether that honour is offered out of love, gratitude, fear, or necessity. Do not mistake a god's power for goodness, nor assume that a god who aids mortals will always do so without demanding payment.

Learn the rites and obligations handed down by previous generations. Do not neglect a sacrifice because its purpose is no longer remembered. At the same time, do not assume that every ancient custom is wise simply because it is ancient.

When the gods oppose one another, protect mortal lives and preserve the community wherever possible. Divine quarrels are not automatically mortal commands.

#### Duties and Obligations

An Olman cleric is expected to:

- Maintain the traditional rites, sacrifices, prayers, and observances of the pantheon.
    
- Know which gods are properly invoked for particular circumstances.
    
- Preserve the myths, histories, and religious traditions of the Olman people.
    
- Maintain temples, shrines, sacred objects, and places of worship.
    
- Conduct rites surrounding births, marriages, funerals, warfare, agriculture, and other important events.
    
- Ensure that sacrifices and offerings are made according to established tradition.
    
- Warn communities when their actions risk provoking a dangerous deity.
    
- Appease, bargain with, or distract hostile gods when necessary.
    
- Seek the favour of gods whose powers are necessary to the community.
    
- Protect the community from supernatural threats and the consequences of divine conflict.
    
- Prevent the demands of one god from unnecessarily destroying the people who depend upon another.
    
- Avoid claiming personal desires or political ambitions as divine commands.
    
- Preserve religious traditions without requiring mortals to imitate every quality of the gods they honour.
    

#### Adventuring Clerics

Olman clerics adventure for many of the same reasons that they serve their communities: to recover sacred objects, rediscover lost rites, investigate ancient temples, protect sacred places, and confront supernatural threats. Some travel as emissaries carrying offerings or messages between communities; others accompany warriors or expeditions to ensure that the appropriate gods are honoured.

An adventuring cleric may also be sent to investigate a divine disturbance. A drought, plague, series of unexplained deaths, unnatural storms, or outbreak of undead may indicate that a god's influence has been disturbed—or that another power is deliberately interfering. The cleric's task is not necessarily to destroy the offending power. Often it is to discover what has happened, determine which gods are involved, and restore a tolerable balance before mortal communities suffer the consequences.

**Olman clerics therefore carry more than holy symbols when they travel. They carry offerings, ritual knowledge, and the obligations owed to powers that may be benevolent, indifferent, or terrifying. Their greatest skill is knowing which god must be addressed, and how, before it is too late.**

## **The** **Suel Pantheon**

The Suel pantheon is an old and fractious collection of gods whose interests encompass nearly every aspect of Suel life. Its members are not united by a common morality or purpose. Some protect mortals and the natural world, some demand order and obedience, some embody freedom and risk, and others delight in cruelty, destruction, or betrayal.

The Suel therefore do not generally regard their gods as uniformly benevolent patrons. Even a favourable god may demand strict observance of ancient obligations, while an indifferent or hostile god cannot simply be ignored. Much of traditional Suel religion is concerned with **knowing which gods have an interest in a particular matter and acting so as not to offend them unnecessarily**.

This is particularly important because the gods themselves are neither harmonious nor distant from one another. They have friendships, rivalries, alliances, grudges, and occasional affairs. Their conflicts sometimes intrude upon the mortal world, and their followers may find themselves drawn into disputes whose origins lie far beyond mortal concerns.

The pantheon consequently contains few universally applicable religious virtues. A quality praised by one god may be condemned by another. Courage may please Kord but caution may be more appropriate when dealing with Syrul; wealth may be celebrated by Xerbo while indifference to material possessions is more fitting for another god. The wise worshipper learns the expectations of the gods rather than assuming that one moral principle governs them all.

#### Worship of the Gods

Most Suel do not devote themselves exclusively to a single deity. Particular gods receive special attention according to circumstance: Phyton may be honoured for the harvest, Osprem before a voyage, Kord before a contest, Lydia when seeking knowledge, or Wee Jas at a funeral.

This does not make the other gods irrelevant. Traditional rites often acknowledge whichever powers might reasonably take an interest in an event. A family might honour Phyton for its crops while also making an offering intended to keep Beltar's attention away from the household. A merchant may ask Xerbo for success while taking care not to offend Norebo. A sailor may invoke Osprem for protection while acknowledging Xerbo's claim over the sea.

Such practices are not necessarily expressions of devotion. **A prayer may be an appeal, an offering may be respect, and a ritual may simply be an attempt to avoid attracting divine attention.**

The Scarlet Brotherhood represents an unusual development of Suel religion. Its insistence upon the superiority of the Suel and its close identification with the ancient Suel gods should not be taken as representative of all Suel worship. Outside the Brotherhood, the pantheon remains a part of the religious heritage of a people who have otherwise become diverse in culture and outlook.

### Clerics of the Suel Gods

Most Suel clerics serve the pantheon rather than a single deity. They maintain the rites of the gods collectively and act as intermediaries between their communities and the divine powers that may affect them.

A cleric is not expected to approve of every god in the pantheon. Indeed, a cleric may personally dislike or fear particular gods while still recognizing their power and observing the rites necessary to avoid their displeasure. The gods are powers to be understood and dealt with, not a moral example that every worshipper must imitate.

The central responsibility of the pantheon priesthood is therefore **to preserve the proper relationship between the community and the gods**. When a benevolent god can be persuaded to help, the cleric makes the appropriate appeal. When a dangerous god has been offended, the cleric seeks to appease it, distract it, or find another divine power willing to protect the community.

A cleric who serves the pantheon may consequently invoke different gods for different purposes without being considered fickle or unfaithful. What matters is that the proper god is honoured in the proper circumstances and that the ancient rites are observed.

#### Dogma

The gods are real, powerful, and divided. Know them and respect them.

Do not demand kindness from a god whose nature is cruel, nor assume that a benevolent god will protect those who disregard their obligations. Learn which powers have an interest in your actions and give each its proper respect.

Preserve the rites handed down by those who came before. Do not provoke the gods needlessly, and do not mistake fear of a god for devotion to that god.

When the gods conflict, protect mortal lives from the consequences whenever possible. Seek the aid of those willing to help, appease those who can be appeased, and turn the attention of hostile powers elsewhere.

Above all, remember that the gods are not responsible for preserving mortal communities. **That responsibility belongs to mortals.**

#### Duties and Obligations

A cleric of the Suel gods is expected to:

- maintain the traditional rites of the pantheon;
    
- preserve and teach the customs, myths, and obligations associated with the gods;
    
- determine which gods should be honoured in matters affecting the community;
    
- perform appropriate prayers, offerings, and ceremonies at important occasions;
    
- appease or distract hostile gods when their attention threatens the community;
    
- seek the aid of benevolent gods when their intervention would protect the people;
    
- preserve temples, shrines, sacred objects, and religious records;
    
- settle disputes concerning religious obligations and customary observances;
    
- warn the community when its actions risk offending a god unnecessarily;
    
- protect the community from supernatural threats, including those arising from divine displeasure;
    
- avoid claiming that personal desires or political decisions are the commands of the gods without good reason;
    
- preserve the religious traditions of the Suel without requiring the faithful to imitate the nature of every god they acknowledge.
    

A cleric is not required to worship every god equally, nor to approve of every god. **Recognition is not devotion.** A priest may honour a dangerous god solely because failing to do so would endanger the community.

#### Adventuring Clerics

A cleric of the Suel gods may leave the community when circumstances require it. Some seek lost religious knowledge or sacred objects; others investigate ancient ruins, recover relics, protect isolated communities, or deal with supernatural threats that cannot be handled locally.

Others adventure because the divine world has intruded upon mortal affairs. An ancient shrine may have been disturbed, a sacred obligation may have been forgotten, or two rival powers may be using mortals as instruments in a divine conflict. In such circumstances, the cleric's task is not simply to destroy the immediate threat but to determine **which divine interests are involved and how the consequences can be contained**.

**A cleric travelling with adventurers may therefore know rituals for gods whom they do not personally favour and carry offerings intended for powers whose attention they would ordinarily avoid. Such practices are considered prudent rather than hypocritical.**

## The Touv Pantheon

The Touv pantheon is a divine family whose members embody the forces that sustain, shape, and threaten Touv life. Uvot, the creator and leader of the pantheon, represents prosperity and the bounty of the land, while his children and other divine relations embody the sun, weather, nature, metal and beauty, time and decay, passion, defence, and the many dangers that afflict mortal communities.

The gods are not uniformly benevolent, nor do they share a single moral purpose. Some nurture life and prosperity, some preserve the order of things, and others embody forces that mortals must endure or resist. Meyanok and his children in particular represent dangers such as poison, discord, fear, vermin, and darkness. Their place within the pantheon does not make their purposes good, but neither does it make their existence something that can simply be denied.

Touv religion consequently places great importance upon **understanding each god's proper place and giving each its due**. The gods have different interests and responsibilities, and a wise worshipper knows which power should be honoured in a particular circumstance. The rain belongs to Vogan, the bounty of the land to Uvot, the sun to Nola, metal and beauty to Xanag, and the passage of life into death to Katay. The same event may involve several gods, each of whom may have a legitimate interest in what occurs.

The relationships between the gods are also important. They are a family, and their relationships include affection, rivalry, resentment, obedience, and conflict. The actions of one god may affect another, and divine disputes can have consequences for mortal communities. Touv priests therefore learn not only the individual nature of the gods but also their relationships with one another.

The pantheon does not provide a single set of virtues that all Touv are expected to follow. The qualities valued by one god may be irrelevant or even contrary to another's nature. Vogan's wandering nature, Xanag's appreciation of beauty, Katay's concern for order and inevitability, and Kundo's loud protectiveness all have their proper places. The Touv religious tradition instead teaches that **each thing should be understood according to its nature and given its proper place in the world**.

#### Worship of the Gods

Most Touv do not devote themselves exclusively to a single deity. Particular gods receive special attention according to circumstance: Uvot may be honoured for prosperity and a successful harvest, Vogan for rain and protection from storms, Nola for health and growth, Xanag for craftsmanship and marriage, Katay at a funeral, or Kundo when a community must be defended.

Honouring one god does not make the others irrelevant. Traditional rites often acknowledge whichever powers might reasonably take an interest in an event. A farmer may honour Uvot for a good harvest while also praying to Vogan for rain. A family may seek Nola's blessing upon a child while observing Katay's rites when an elder dies. A metalsmith may honour Xanag for a fine piece of work while seeking Kundo's protection for the workshop in which it is made.

The darker gods are treated differently but are not simply forgotten. A community may take precautions against Meyanok, Vara, or Damaran without considering such acts worship. Their powers are real, and their interests can have consequences even when the community opposes them. In some circumstances, ritual observance may be intended to prevent a dangerous god from taking notice rather than to seek its favour.

Such practices are not necessarily expressions of devotion. **A prayer may be an appeal, an offering may be respect, and a ritual may simply be recognition of a power that must be reckoned with.**

Touv religious life is therefore concerned less with choosing a single god than with maintaining proper relationships among mortals, the divine family, and the forces represented by the gods. To honour the gods is to understand their gifts, respect their boundaries, fulfil the obligations placed upon mortals, and use what the world provides without forgetting the powers that made those gifts possible.

### Clerics of the Touv Gods

Most Touv clerics serve the pantheon rather than a single deity. They maintain the rites of the gods collectively and act as intermediaries between their communities and the divine powers that govern the world around them.

The Touv gods are a divine family, and each has a proper place within the order of creation. Uvot stands at the head of the pantheon, while his descendants and other divine beings embody the forces upon which Touv life depends or against which it must struggle. Rain may nourish the fields or become a destructive storm; the earth provides both food and metal; the sun gives life; decay eventually claims all things; and fear, poison, vermin, and discord remain dangers that cannot simply be wished away.

A cleric is therefore not expected to approve of every god in the pantheon. Some gods are benevolent, some indifferent, and some actively hostile to the Touv people. A cleric may honour a dangerous god when circumstances require it without embracing that god's nature or purposes. The gods are powers to be understood, respected, and dealt with according to their proper place.

The central responsibility of the pantheon priesthood is **to maintain the proper relationship between the Touv community and the gods**. The cleric determines which deity should be honoured for a particular need, performs the appropriate rites, seeks divine assistance when it is available, and warns the community when its actions risk provoking divine displeasure.

A cleric who serves the pantheon may consequently invoke different gods for different purposes without being considered fickle or unfaithful. A prayer for rain belongs to Vogan, a prayer for prosperity to Uvot, a prayer concerning metal and beauty to Xanag, and a funeral rite to Katay. The gods have different responsibilities, and wise worshippers acknowledge those distinctions.

#### Dogma

The gods are part of the world. Know them, honour them, and understand what each demands.

Give thanks for the gifts upon which life depends. Protect the sources of water, respect the land, preserve the community, honour the dead, celebrate marriages and births, and pass the knowledge of the ancestors to those who come after.

Do not mistake a god's power for goodness. Some divine powers bring prosperity and protection; others embody dangers that must be endured or opposed. A god's place in the pantheon does not make that god worthy of imitation.

Respect the relationships between the gods. Their rivalries, alliances, and family ties can affect mortals, and the wise cleric avoids drawing divine conflicts unnecessarily into the affairs of the community.

When a god can aid the people, seek its favour. When a god has been offended, make proper amends. When a hostile power threatens the community, seek the protection of those gods willing to oppose it.

Above all, remember that the gods provide gifts and guidance, but **mortals remain responsible for using those gifts wisely**. Prosperity must be cultivated, communities must be defended, and the world entrusted to the Touv must be cared for.

#### Duties and Obligations

A cleric of the Touv gods is expected to:

- maintain the traditional rites of the pantheon;
    
- preserve and teach the customs, myths, and obligations associated with the gods;
    
- determine which gods should be honoured in matters affecting the community;
    
- perform appropriate prayers, offerings, and ceremonies at important occasions;
    
- give thanks for the gifts of the gods and encourage their proper use;
    
- preserve sources of food, water, shelter, and other necessities whenever possible;
    
- appease or otherwise address gods whose displeasure threatens the community;
    
- seek the aid of benevolent gods when their intervention would protect or benefit the people;
    
- preserve temples, shrines, sacred objects, and religious records;
    
- settle disputes concerning religious obligations and customary observances;
    
- preserve the proper rites for births, marriages, funerals, and other important transitions;
    
- warn the community when its actions risk offending a god unnecessarily;
    
- protect the community from supernatural threats, including those arising from hostile divine powers;
    
- avoid claiming that personal desires or political decisions are the commands of the gods without good reason;
    
- respect the proper place of every member of the pantheon without requiring the faithful to imitate the nature of every god they acknowledge.
    

A cleric is not required to worship every god equally, nor to approve of every god. **Recognition is not devotion.** A priest may honour a dangerous or unpleasant god because that god has a legitimate place in the divine order, without accepting that god's purposes as their own.

#### Adventuring Clerics

A cleric of the Touv gods may leave the community when circumstances require it. Some seek lost religious knowledge or sacred objects; others investigate ancient ruins, recover relics, protect isolated communities, seek new sources of food or water, or deal with supernatural threats that cannot be handled locally.

Others adventure because the divine world has intruded upon mortal affairs. A sacred place may have been disturbed, an important obligation may have been forgotten, or the actions of one god may have placed a community in danger from another. In such circumstances, the cleric's task is not simply to destroy the immediate threat but to determine **which divine interests are involved and how the consequences can be contained**.

A cleric travelling with adventurers may therefore carry offerings for several different gods, know the rites appropriate to different circumstances, and call upon powers whose concerns have little to do with the cleric's personal devotion. A Vogan priest may still perform the funeral rites of Katay; a devotee of Xanag may pray to Nola for healing; and a cleric who normally seeks Uvot's favour may appeal to Vogan when a drought threatens the community.

**Such practices are not considered contradictory. The wise cleric gives each god its proper due.**  
  

# Chapter 2:The Deities and their Priesthoods

## Al'Akbar

**The Exalted, Keeper of the Faith, Guardian of the Faithful**

**Alignment:** LG, LN

**Portfolio:** Guardianship, faithfulness, dignity, duty

**Symbol:** A golden cup or vessel held between two raised hands

Al'Akbar is the exemplar of faithful service and honorable duty. He teaches that faith is demonstrated through conduct rather than words: a promise must be kept, a charge must be fulfilled, and those entrusted to one's protection must not be abandoned. He is invoked by guardians, judges, householders, soldiers, officials, and all who bear responsibility for others.

Al'Akbar's followers place great importance upon dignity. A person's station carries obligations as well as privileges, and those who accept authority are expected to exercise it responsibly. Likewise, those who swear loyalty or accept another's protection are expected to remain faithful to that bond. Al'Akbar is not concerned merely with obedience, however; duty must be honorable, and faithfulness does not excuse injustice.

#### Dogma

Honor what you have sworn to honor. Fulfill the duties entrusted to you, even when they are difficult. Protect those placed in your care, preserve what has been entrusted to you, and do not abandon your responsibilities for convenience or personal gain. Conduct yourself with dignity, for your actions reflect upon those whose trust you bear. Those who hold authority must be worthy of it, and those who accept protection must respect the obligations that accompany it.

### The Priesthood

The priests of Al'Akbar serve as guardians, judges, advisers, and keepers of sacred tradition. Temples commonly stand in prominent positions within settlements and serve not only as places of worship but as repositories of records, oaths, sacred objects, and communal wealth. Priests are expected to be trustworthy and composed, and their word is relied upon in matters of obligation and dispute.

The priesthood recognizes two traditional forms of devotion. The **True Faith** emphasizes humility, steadfastness, and service, while the **Exalted Faith** places greater emphasis upon dignity, ceremony, and the responsibilities of rank. These are traditions of emphasis rather than separate religions, and priests of either tradition recognize the same god and obligations.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Al'Akbar is expected to:

- Keep all oaths and promises unless fulfilling them would directly violate a greater duty.
    
- Protect people, places, objects, and knowledge entrusted to their care.
    
- Fulfill the responsibilities associated with their position rather than seeking its privileges alone.
    
- Conduct themselves with dignity and restraint, particularly when acting as representatives of the faith.
    
- Preserve sacred traditions, records, and objects.
    
- Provide food and other necessities to those legitimately under their protection.
    
- Encourage reconciliation and lawful settlement when disputes threaten the community.
    
- Hold other guardians and authorities accountable when they abuse the trust placed in them.
    
- Never knowingly betray a person who has legitimately placed themselves under the priest's protection.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Priests of Al'Akbar may adventure when duty requires them to recover something entrusted to their protection, defend a community or individual, recover sacred property, settle a dispute, or fulfill an oath. Some undertake journeys to establish shrines, carry important messages, or seek knowledge necessary to preserve their faith and people. An adventuring priest is expected to regard companions as people under their protection once a bond of trust has been established.

### Priest of Al'Akbar

**Ability Requirements:** WIS12

**Alignment:** LG or LN

**Weapons:** Falchion, scimitar, dagger, knife, spear, mace

**Armor:** Any up to scale mail

**Major Spheres:** All, Combat, Divination, Guardian, Healing, Law, Protection, Sun

**Minor Spheres:** —

**Prohibited Spheres:** —

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Language of the Faithful:** The priest gains Ancient Baklunish as a bonus proficiency.

**3rd Level — Know Alignment:** The priest may cast _know alignment_ once per day without expending a spell slot.

**5th Level — Create Food & Water:** The priest may cast _create food & water_ once per day without expending a spell slot.

**7th Level — Divination:** The priest may cast _divination_ once per week without requiring its material components.

**10th Level — Atonement:** The priest may cast _atonement_ once per day without expending a spell slot or requiring its material components.

**Turn Undead:** Priests of Al'Akbar turn undead as though they were three levels lower than their actual level.

## Al-Zarad

Baklunish alias of Boccob

## Allitur

**Titles:** The Law-Giver, Truth-Sayer, Hammer of Truth, Guide of Decision

**Alignment:** LG (LN)

**Portfolio:** Ethics, Propriety, Law, Tradition, Honesty

**Symbol:** A pair of clasped hands

Allitur is the Flan Power of ethics, propriety, and the laws and traditions that bind communities across generations. Often considered the younger brother of Rao, he maintains close ties with the other Flan Powers and sometimes acts as their intermediary with other pantheons. He is credited with teaching the Flan the principles of law and punishment, and many customs concerning the home, family, and community are attributed to his guidance. He is said to ride an untiring horse named Keph.

Allitur is widely respected among the Flan, even by those who do not worship him directly. His name is invoked at trials, diplomatic meetings, formal agreements, and other occasions when fairness and good faith are expected. His priests serve as judges, arbiters, legal advisers, and teachers, preserving the traditions that give communities their identity and the laws that allow disputes to be settled without endless retaliation.

#### Dogma

A people that forgets its traditions risks forgetting what holds it together. Customs and laws preserve the accumulated wisdom of generations, define obligations between individuals and communities, and provide means to settle disputes. They must be remembered, understood, and properly observed.

Law is not merely the power to punish. It establishes obligations, protects agreements, and gives those in dispute a means to seek judgment. Those who exercise authority must act fairly and accept the responsibilities imposed upon them. Oaths and agreements are binding, and those who invoke the law must be willing to abide by its judgments.

Honesty is essential to justice, but truth cannot be established merely by demanding it. The faithful must learn to distinguish credible testimony from deception, examine evidence carefully, and recognize the limits of their own judgment. A fair decision requires patience and impartiality, not simply confidence in one's own righteousness.

Tradition deserves respect, but its proper observance requires knowledge and understanding. The faithful must teach customs to the young, preserve records for future generations, and ensure that rites are performed correctly rather than allowed to decay into empty gestures.

### The Priesthood

Allitur's priests serve as judges, arbiters, legal advisers, diplomats, scribes, and teachers of Flan tradition. They travel between communities to preserve old customs and instruct those unfamiliar with them, including children and people whose communities have forgotten traditional practices.

His priests are expected to cultivate honesty and impartiality in their own conduct. They study the laws and customs of the communities they serve, learn to assess testimony, and develop the patience needed to investigate disputes without prematurely condemning either party. Their authority rests upon their knowledge, reputation, and demonstrated fairness rather than an assumption that their judgments are infallible.

Temples of Allitur are commonly simple in their exterior construction, decorated with bas-reliefs depicting important Flan rites and scenes of Allitur giving the law to his people. Within, their walls are inscribed with Flan laws. Temples maintain archives of legal proceedings, agreements, genealogies, and cultural and religious teachings. In smaller settlements, a priest may serve as a _keeper_, recording local proceedings, arbitrating disputes, and overseeing customary rites.

Allitur's priests may enforce judgments and punishments, particularly in cases involving criminal acts, broken oaths, or violations of important taboos. They are also expected to challenge injustice, even when it has been sanctioned by local custom or the powerful interests of a community. Respect for law does not excuse corruption, nor does tradition justify every practice merely because it is old.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Allitur is expected to:

- Preserve and teach Flan laws, customs, traditions, and religious rites.
    
- Arbitrate disputes and render fair judgments when entrusted with that responsibility.
    
- Advise individuals and communities on legal obligations and customary practice.
    
- Record agreements, judgments, marriages, and other important proceedings.
    
- Carry messages and mediate negotiations between communities, tribes, and nations.
    
- Uphold lawful judgments and ensure that penalties are properly administered.
    
- Honour oaths and agreements, and hold oathbreakers accountable.
    
- Cultivate personal honesty and avoid deliberate deception, corruption, or partiality in the exercise of their duties.
    
- Challenge unjust treatment and abuses of authority, particularly when the vulnerable lack the means to defend themselves.
    
- Preserve records and teach traditions so that future generations need not depend upon unreliable recollection.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Priests of Allitur travel to settle disputes, negotiate agreements, recover lost records, or teach traditional practices in communities where they have fallen into neglect. Their duties may bring them into hostile lands, particularly when they confront oathbreakers, expose corruption, or challenge practices they consider unjust. They may also accompany expeditions as legal advisers, chroniclers, and impartial witnesses to agreements.

Many are sought out by ordinary people who cannot afford formal legal assistance or who lack the standing to make their grievances heard. Others serve rulers and communities as advisers, but are expected to speak honestly even when their conclusions are unwelcome.

### Priest of Allitur

**Ability Requirements:** Wisdom 13

**Alignment:** Lawful Good or Lawful Neutral

**Weapons:** As clerics, plus spear

**Armour:** Any

**Major Spheres:** All, Charm, Combat, Creation, Divination, Guardian, Healing, Law, Protection, Thought

**Minor Spheres:** Necromantic, Sun

**Granted Powers**

**3rd Level — Resistance to Coercion:** The priest gains a +2 bonus to saving throws against _ESP_, _charm person_, _suggestion_, _feeblemind_, and similar mind-affecting magic that could compromise ethical judgment or decision-making. If such magic coerces the priest into acting against their ethos, the priest immediately receives an additional saving throw, with a +2 bonus, to break the effect.

**5th Level — Zone of Truth:** Once per day, the priest may create a _zone of truth_ as the spell of the same name.

**9th Level — Unyielding Mind:** The priest's bonus to saving throws against qualifying mind-affecting magic increases to +4.

**11th Level — Attunement to Truth:** The priest becomes attuned to truth in a manner similar to a _ring of truth_. The priest can recognize when another creature knowingly speaks a lie, subject to the normal limitations of the equivalent magical effect. In return, the priest cannot knowingly tell a lie.

**Loss of Powers:** A priest who knowingly violates an applicable law or obligation that falls within the traditions of Flan law, or who commits a serious breach of ethical conduct, loses access to spells and granted powers until completing an appropriate act of atonement. The DM should distinguish deliberate misconduct from honest mistakes, reasonable differences of interpretation, or actions taken under genuine duress.

**Turn Undead:** Turn at –2 levels.

## Atar

Baklunish alias of Pelor

## Atroa

**Titles:** The Wind-Daughter, Provider, Shy One, First Sister

**Alignment:** Neutral Good

**Pantheon:** Oeridian

**Portfolio:** Spring, East Wind, Renewal

**Symbol:** A _kara_ tree laden with ripe, red fruit

Atroa is the youngest daughter of Velnius and the first of the seasonal sisters to return after Telchur's winter reign. She brings warmth back to the land, coaxes crops from the earth, and sends the eastern winds that herald the coming of spring. She represents planting, growth, renewal, and the continuation of life from one generation to the next.

Atroa is a shy and distant goddess who rarely communicates directly with mortals. Her followers understand the cycle she represents: birth, growth, decline, death, and rebirth. Winter may come, and individual lives may end, but life endures and spring returns.

#### Dogma

Life is a gift, but every gift brings responsibilities. Plant what must grow, nurture what has been born, and preserve what has been entrusted to your care. Renewal is not merely the arrival of something new; it is the continuation of life through patience, labour, and responsibility.

Welcome the return of spring and give thanks for the warmth, rain, and favourable winds that sustain the living. Work the land diligently, care for animals, protect children, and provide for those who cannot provide for themselves. A seed planted and abandoned is a promise broken. A child brought into the world and neglected is a life betrayed.

The seasons remind mortals that hardship does not last forever, but neither can prosperity be taken for granted. Prepare for the lean months, tend what you have planted, and remember that what flourishes today must be cared for if it is to flourish tomorrow.

### The Priesthood

Atroa's church is widespread throughout the lands settled by the Oeridian migrations. Her worship is particularly strong in Ahlissa, the Kingdom of Keoland, Bissel, the Gran March, the Ulek States, the County of Urnst, and the lands of the Wolf and Tiger Nomads. Smaller shrines are found elsewhere, especially in farming communities and among those who depend upon favourable winds.

Her temples are generally modest wooden chapels or shrines with large windows, often facing east. Most have a tower with a bell and a substantial garden. Larger temples may also maintain extensive grounds for cultivation and charitable work.

The church contains two principal traditions. The **Planterings** emphasize spring, agriculture, animal husbandry, and the renewal of the land. Their priests work alongside farmers and labourers, helping prepare fields, plant crops, maintain irrigation, and improve the land's fertility. Their busiest season is spring, though their work continues through the growing season and into preparations for the following year.

The **Bearers** place greater emphasis on children, families, and the continuation of life. They are predominantly women and frequently serve as midwives, caregivers, and keepers of orphanages. They regard every child as deserving of nourishment, shelter, affection, and the opportunity to grow. Male Bearers are uncommon but respected.

The church traditionally encourages marriage and family life, and many of its priests regard the bearing and nurturing of children as sacred responsibilities. Pregnancy is honoured, but the underlying obligation is not simply to produce new life: it is to care for that life. Neglect, whether of a child, a crop, or an animal, is considered a grave failing.

The clergy generally dress in practical clothing suitable for their work. Green hats, green cloaks sometimes decorated with red spots, and leather gloves are common. Formal vestments consist of green, knee-length robes with short sleeves and hoods. Priests who wear armour favour plain leather, avoiding ornament that interferes with their duties.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Atroa is expected to:

- participate in planting, cultivation, harvesting, and other seasonal agricultural work;
    
- teach and encourage sound agricultural practices, including soil care, irrigation, and the responsible use of resources;
    
- care for children, support families, and provide assistance to orphans and those unable to support themselves;
    
- serve as a midwife or assist with childbirth when trained to do so;
    
- bless newborn children, marriages, newly established households, and other beginnings;
    
- nurture what has been planted or created rather than abandoning it when the initial work is complete;
    
- preserve food and prepare communities for the hardships of winter;
    
- offer thanks for favourable weather and pray for relief when cold, drought, or other conditions threaten the growing season;
    
- honour the dead while teaching that death is part of the greater cycle of life;
    
- encourage cooperation among farmers, families, and communities, particularly during planting and harvest;
    
- oppose needless destruction, cruelty toward children, and the neglect of those dependent upon others.
    

#### Holy Days and Ceremonies

Daily prayers give thanks to Atroa for the return of the wind, the passage of the seasons, and the opportunity to sustain life. Agricultural labour itself is regarded as a form of prayer, and her followers commonly recite blessings over the ground as they work.

The **Spring Feast**, held on the fourth day of Growfest at the spring equinox, is the church's principal holy day. It is devoted to prayer, rest, reflection upon the previous year, and hope for the year ahead.

The wider festival of Growfest is also an important period for Atroa's clergy. Priests and worshippers work together to prepare and plant fields, with communal meals, songs, prayers, and celebrations following the day's labour. Godsday is reserved for rest.

The church also blesses newborn children and newly formed families, and its priests frequently preside over funerals. Such rites acknowledge grief without treating death as the end of the greater cycle of existence.

#### Adventuring Priests

Atroa's priests are not ordinarily eager to abandon their communities, but they may travel when their duties require it. Some seek new crops, agricultural knowledge, or remedies for blighted fields. Others carry seeds and practical knowledge to distant settlements, help communities recover after disaster, or seek to restore land rendered barren by war or neglect.

Bearers may travel to establish or support orphanages, protect vulnerable families, or find children separated from their communities. Other priests accompany adventurers because a threat to a settlement, its food supply, or its children cannot be resolved by local means alone.

An adventuring priest of Atroa understands that renewal requires more than destroying what threatens life. The work of rebuilding, planting, healing, and caring for survivors is equally important.

### Priest of Atroa

**Ability Requirements:** Constitution 11

**Prime Requisites:** Constitution, Wisdom

**Alignment:** Neutral Good, Chaotic Good, or Lawful Good

**Weapons:** Any nonmetal weapon

**Armour:** Any nonmetal armour

**Major Spheres:** All, Animal, Creation, Healing, Plant, Sun, Weather

**Minor Spheres:** Divination, Time

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Resistance to Withering:** Green Daughters and Green Sons gain a +2 bonus to saving throws against magical effects that cause aging, withering, or rotting. If such an effect normally allows no saving throw, the priest may attempt one without this bonus.

**3rd Level — Plant Growth:** The priest may cast _plant growth_ once per day for every three levels of ability, as described in the source rules.

**6th Level — Regeneration:** The priest regenerates 1 hit point per turn, even if killed. The application of this power after death must follow the reboot's rules for death and recovery.

**12th Level — Reincarnation:** The priest may cast _reincarnation_ once per day.

**Turn Undead:** As a cleric.

**Races:** Halflings may become Green Daughters or Green Sons, though they are uncommon among Atroa's specialty priests.

## Beltar

**The Mother of Monsters, Lady of Pits, The Hag Below**

**Alignment:** Chaotic Evil

**Pantheon:** Suel

**Portfolio:** Malice, caves, pits, monsters

**Symbol:** A snarling monstrous face emerging from a dark pit

Beltar is a cruel and hateful goddess who delights in the suffering of the weak and defenseless. She rules over the dark places beneath the earth and the savage creatures that make their homes there. Her worship is uncommon among civilized peoples, but she is revered by certain humanoid tribes, subterranean monsters, and those who embrace hatred and predation as virtues.

Beltar's true form is that of a hideous old hag, but she is a goddess of many shapes. She may appear as a beholder, a red dragon, or a marilith, and tales of a Suel serpent-goddess may have arisen from her use of this last form. Beltar is notoriously fickle even toward those who serve her. She takes mates freely, often in monstrous form, but commonly devours both her offspring and their father afterward.

#### Dogma

The strong have the right to prey upon the weak. Mercy is a weakness to be exploited, while fear and hatred are tools by which the powerful maintain their dominance. The defenseless exist to be tormented, the frightened to be hunted, and those who cannot protect themselves have no claim upon the protection of others.

Beltar's followers are taught to embrace the darkness rather than fear it. Caves, pits, ruins, and other places where sunlight rarely reaches are sacred. Monsters are not abominations to be destroyed, but powers to be feared, placated, or mastered. Those who serve Beltar must never mistake kindness for strength.

### The Priesthood
