**The Brawler, The Strongest Man, Lord of Athletics**

**Alignment:** Chaotic Good

**Portfolio:** Athletics, sport, brawling, strength, courage

**Symbol:** A four-pointed star formed from four spears

Kord is one of the most powerful of the Suel gods, surpassed among them only by his grandfather Lendor. Once perhaps the most widely worshipped of the Suel powers, his following has declined somewhat since the Greyhawk Wars, as many have turned toward gods who offer more direct protection in times of war and hardship. Kord offers no such promise. He teaches his followers to become strong enough to face danger themselves.

Kord appears as a hugely muscular man with long red hair and a long red beard. He wears dragon-hide gauntlets, boots, and a fighting girdle in white, blue, and red. He is famously fond of attractive mortals and has fathered numerous children, or at least accepted claims of divine descent. Humans and elves are his favored companions, although he has been known to pursue relationships with other demihumans and even giants.

Kord is generally jovial and competitive, but severe injury can drive him into a terrible blood rage in which friend and foe become equally vulnerable to his violence. Only Lendor is said to possess the authority to restore him to his senses before the rage ends. This fearsome aspect of Kord is particularly admired by barbarians and other warriors who regard uncontrolled ferocity as a natural expression of strength.

#### Dogma

Strength is meant to be tested. Courage is not the absence of fear, but the willingness to act despite it. Physical excellence should be cultivated through training, competition, hardship, and honest effort.

Kord's followers are encouraged to compete with one another without needless malice. Victory is worthy of celebration, but so is the achievement of an opponent who has fought well. Cowardice, deliberate weakness, and dependence upon magic when one's own strength could suffice are regarded with contempt.

A Kordite does not seek an easy life. Challenges exist to reveal what a person is capable of becoming.

### The Priesthood

Kord's priests emphasize physical prowess over magical power. They train as athletes, wrestlers, boxers, warriors, climbers, runners, and other practitioners of physical disciplines. Their spells are generally used to strengthen themselves and their companions rather than to destroy opponents directly.

Priests of Kord are expected to serve as leaders. They organize athletic contests, military exercises, expeditions, tournaments, and other tests of endurance and courage. They wear white, blue, and red, but deliberately avoid combining the colours in the same arrangement traditionally attributed to Kord.

Dragon-hide armour is considered blasphemous. Kord's priests may wear dragon-hide gauntlets and similar items associated with the god, but armour made from dragon hide is forbidden unless the wearer is genuinely descended from Kord.

Kord accepts no excuse for cowardice among his clergy. A priest who deliberately abandons companions or demonstrates true cowardice is disowned by the god and immediately loses the ability to cast priest spells. Such a priest retains their physical training and becomes a fighter of equivalent level.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Kord is expected to:

- maintain a rigorous regimen of physical training;
    
- regularly test their strength, endurance, courage, and skill;
    
- encourage honest competition;
    
- face danger rather than abandon companions through cowardice;
    
- strengthen and encourage those who fight alongside them;
    
- lead by example rather than relying upon magical power alone;
    
- use magic primarily to enhance physical ability and aid companions;
    
- respect worthy opponents and acknowledge genuine achievement;
    
- organize contests, games, trials, or other tests of physical excellence.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Kord's priests are enthusiastic adventurers. They seek difficult challenges, dangerous opponents, lost treasures, unexplored wilderness, and opportunities to test themselves against formidable creatures.

A Kordite adventurer is likely to volunteer for the most physically demanding task, accept a difficult contest rather than an easy victory, and encourage companions to overcome challenges through their own abilities. Such priests may also serve as expedition leaders, military instructors, arena champions, or trainers.

### Priest of Kord

**Ability Requirements:** Strength 13, Constitution 13

**Alignment:** Chaotic Good

**Weapons:** Any

**Armor:** Any

**Major Spheres:** All, Chaos, Combat, Creation, Divination, Healing, Protection, Travelers

**Minor Spheres:** None

**Prohibited Spheres:** None

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Courage:** The priest gains a +1 bonus to all saving throws against fear effects. The priest may also cast _enlarge_ as a granted power.

**3rd Level — Greater Courage:** The priest's bonus to saving throws against fear increases to +2. The priest may cast _strength_ as a granted power.

**5th Level — Battle Prayer:** The priest may cast either _prayer_ or _protection from normal missiles_ as a granted power. The choice may be made each time the power is used.

**7th Level — Greater Courage:** The priest's bonus to saving throws against fear increases to +3. The priest may cast _cure serious wounds_ as a granted power.

**10th Level — Kord's Strength:** The priest's Strength increases by 1, to a maximum of 18/00. The priest's bonus to saving throws against fear increases to +4.

**13th Level — Greater Courage:** The priest's bonus to saving throws against fear increases to +5. The priest may cast _heal_ as a granted power.

**Warrior Training:** A priest of Kord may select nonweapon proficiencies from the Warrior proficiency group without the normal penalty for selecting a proficiency outside the priest's group.

**Cowardice:** A priest who knowingly abandons companions through cowardice or otherwise demonstrates behavior fundamentally opposed to Kord's ideals is disowned by the god. The priest immediately loses all priestly spellcasting ability and becomes a fighter of the same level. The loss cannot be reversed merely by changing behaviour; restoration requires appropriate divine intervention and atonement at the DM's discretion.

**Opposition to Law:** Kord's priests may cast _detect lawful_ and _protection from lawful_ as variants of the corresponding _detect evil_ and _protection from evil_ spells.

## Kundo

**Titles:** The Builder, The Shield, The Thunderous Guardian

**Alignment:** Lawful Good

**Portfolio:** Building, Defense, Music, Noise

**Symbol:** A shield crossed by a hammer and sapling

Kundo is the union of storm and metal: thunderous noise joined with the strength and protection of shelter. He is a guardian god, loud and boisterous, who delights in building, construction, music, and the communal work by which people make themselves safe.

Kundo is depicted as a laughing Touv man carrying a great shield upon one arm and a cluster of young saplings beneath the other. His laughter and songs are said to be as loud as thunder.

#### Dogma

Build what protects. A shelter is more than walls and a roof; it is a promise that those within it will not be abandoned. Kundo teaches his followers to build strongly, repair what is damaged, and protect those who cannot protect themselves.

Work should be communal and celebrated. Songs, rhythmic labor, drums, hammers, and other loud sounds remind people that they are part of something greater than themselves. Kundo's followers should be proud of honest labor and should never allow pride, poverty, or social status to determine who deserves protection.

The strong have a duty to shield the weak. When danger threatens, a follower of Kundo should stand between the danger and those who cannot withstand it. Retreat is acceptable when it preserves lives and allows people to defend themselves another day, but abandoning those already in danger is a grave failure.

### The Priesthood

Kundo's priests and shamans are builders, defenders, teachers, and community leaders. They construct shelters for the poor, repair homes and defensive works, teach traditional songs, and organize communal labor. They are often found at the center of settlements, where their temples may also serve as meeting halls, workshops, shelters, or places of refuge.

Kundo's priests are expected to be conspicuous rather than subtle. Their voices, songs, hammers, drums, and shields announce their presence. In times of danger, however, their reputation for noise gives way to disciplined action. A Kundo priest will go to great lengths to rescue someone in danger, even when doing so places the priest at considerable risk.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Kundo is expected to:

- Build and maintain safe shelters for the community, especially for the poor and vulnerable.
    
- Protect those who cannot adequately defend themselves.
    
- Repair homes, roads, bridges, walls, and other useful structures when able.
    
- Teach and preserve traditional songs and communal music.
    
- Encourage cooperation and communal labor.
    
- Train others in practical methods of defense and emergency shelter.
    
- Never knowingly abandon a person who can reasonably be rescued.
    
- Defend the community against threats from without.
    
- Ensure that protection is not reserved solely for the wealthy or powerful.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Kundo's priests adventure when the work of protection requires them to leave the safety of their communities. They may escort refugees, protect builders and settlers, recover materials needed to repair a settlement, or investigate threats to a community's defenses.

They are particularly likely to undertake rescue missions. A Kundo priest does not need to be the strongest member of an adventuring party to insist that someone be rescued; refusing to help a person in immediate danger is difficult for such a priest to accept.

### Priest of Kundo

**Ability Requirements:** Constitution 15

**Alignment:** Lawful Good or Neutral Good

**Weapons:** Atlatl, chakram, short bow, short sword, staff

**Armor:** Any; a shield is required

**Major Spheres:** All, Charm, Combat, Divination, Elemental (Earth), Guardian, Healing, Necromantic, Protection, Wards

**Minor Spheres:** Charm, Combat, Divination, Necromantic

**Prohibited Spheres:**

**Magical Items:** A priest of Kundo must carry an ornate but functional shield or wear a similarly decorated breastplate bearing Kundo's symbol.

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Shield of Kundo:** The priest gains a **+2 bonus to Armor Class** while wielding a shield.

**3rd Level — Barkskin:** The priest may cast _barkskin_ once per day.

**5th Level — Protective Works:** The priest may cast either _magical vestment_ or _protection from normal missiles_ once per day.

**7th Level — Guardian's Ward:** The priest may cast _protection from evil 10' radius_ once per day.

**10th Level — Secure Shelter:** The priest may cast _Leomund's secure shelter_ once per day.

**13th Level — Wall of Force:** The priest may cast _wall of force_ once per day.

**16th Level — Blade Barrier:** The priest may cast _blade barrier_ once per day.

**Turn Undead:** A priest of Kundo may turn undead normally.

## Kurell

**Titles:** The Green-Eyed God, the Avenger, the Watcher Who Never Sleeps, the Vengeful Lord, Black Wolf of the North, Lord of Thieves

**Alignment:** Chaotic Neutral

**Pantheon:** Oeridian

**Portfolio:** Jealousy, Revenge, Thievery

**Symbol:** A single green eye

Kurell is the power of petty jealousy and vengeance, a paranoid and resentful god who believes that others are conspiring against him or enjoying advantages he has been denied. He hates those who have broken his trust, those who seem to possess what he lacks, and even those who are simply more successful or admired than himself. He is also the god of thieves, though his followers know better than to rely too comfortably on his favour.

Kurell is most often depicted as a tall, handsome man with bright green eyes, dressed in fine dark clothing and a black cloak. His apparent attractiveness conceals a bitter and insecure nature. He is associated with the black wolf, a form in which he sometimes manifests, and with the dagger as an instrument of revenge. The god is rarely portrayed as content: even his successes offer only temporary satisfaction before envy and suspicion return.

#### Dogma

You have a right to what you desire, and no one should be permitted to take from you what you consider yours. Those who wrong you must be made to pay, and justice is most satisfying when exacted by your own hand. Do not depend upon courts, rulers, or other authorities to avenge you; they may be indifferent, corrupt, or more concerned with their own interests than yours.

Guard your possessions, your reputation, and your secrets jealously. Trust no one completely, for even a friend may become a rival. Take what you can, protect what you have, and never forget an injury. Yet beware of inviting vengeance upon yourself: those who betray others must expect betrayal in return. A successful theft or act of revenge honours Kurell, but those who receive his aid should remember to give thanks lest the Vengeful Lord turn his attention towards them.

### The Priesthood

Kurell's clergy is small, secretive, and protective of its own influence. Its members are masters of vengeance, and some specialise in assassination, which they regard as the most professional expression of retribution. Such priests may be hired by the church to carry out carefully chosen acts of vengeance, though the faith has no unified hierarchy or clearly defined internal factions.

The clergy is composed exclusively of men, who jealously guard their authority against outsiders. Some observers believe that the exclusion of women is intended to provoke envy, though the church itself offers no definitive explanation. Priests tend to distrust one another, yet their fear of retaliation can make them surprisingly dependable when agreements are explicit and the consequences of betrayal are clear.

Kurell has followers among the Wolf Nomads and the Flan-descended peoples of the former lands of the Rovers of the Barrens. His worship is also found among thieves throughout the Flanaess, many of whom make offerings less from devotion than from fear of attracting the god's wrath. In the Great Kingdom, the city of Atirr has a particularly strong following, associated with the College of Endings and Beginnings, a guild that trains assassins and arranges contracts for their services.

Temples are rare. Shrines may be established in secluded natural sites or places where a notable act of revenge was carried out. Any place where vengeance is seriously contemplated or exacted may be regarded as sacred. The best-known sanctuaries include Black Wolf's Lair, a cavern in the Burneal Forest, and a small, richly decorated chapel within Atirr's College of Endings and Beginnings.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Kurell is expected to:

- Remember injuries and pursue vengeance against those responsible.
    
- Guard possessions, secrets, and personal interests against theft or betrayal.
    
- Help worshippers exact retribution when doing so serves the faith.
    
- Cultivate the skills of stealth, concealment, and theft.
    
- Honour agreements carefully, recognising that betrayal invites retaliation.
    
- Make nightly prayers, often naming those against whom vengeance is sought.
    
- Offer thanks to Kurell after a successful theft or act of revenge.
    
- Observe the Winter Solstice and Kurell's Night with appropriate rites.
    
- Support the church's assassins and other agents when their services are required.
    
- Avoid drawing unnecessary attention to the faith's shrines and activities.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Kurell's priests adventure to pursue grudges, recover stolen property, punish betrayal, and acquire wealth or influence. Some act as thieves, informants, or assassins; others travel in search of those who have wronged them or their allies. They may be useful companions when their interests coincide with those of the party, particularly when stealth and careful planning are needed. However, jealousy can make them suspicious of a companion's success, and perceived slights may become lasting grievances. Their word may be dependable when a bargain is clear, but they rarely forget who holds the advantage.

### Priest of Kurell

Kurell's clergy includes clerics, specialty priests known as **Quickfingars**, shamans, and thieves. The Quickfingars are the most distinctive priestly order, combining the worship of vengeance with the stealth and dexterity of professional thieves.

**Ability Requirements:** Dexterity 15

**Prime Requisites:** Dexterity, Wisdom

**Alignment:** Chaotic neutral, chaotic evil, or chaotic good (the latter uncommon)

**Weapons:** As thief

**Armour:** As thief

**Major Spheres:** All, Chaos, Combat, Guardian, Necromantic, Protection, Wards

**Minor Spheres:** Charm, Divination, Sun (reversed)

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Thief Skills:** The Quickfingar gains the thief abilities _Move Silently_ and _Hide in Shadows_, each beginning at 25%. Each time the priest gains a level, both abilities improve by 5%.

**5th Level — Invisibility:** The Quickfingar can cast _invisibility_ once per day.

**7th Level — Wraithform:** The Quickfingar can cast _wraithform_ once per day.

**9th Level — Poison:** The Quickfingar can cast _poison_ once per day, using the reversed effect of _neutralize poison_.

**Turn Undead:** Cannot turn undead. Evil priests may command undead, according to the general rules for Kurell's clergy.

## Lendor

**Prince of Time and Tedium, Creator of the Suel Powers**

**Alignment:** Lawful Neutral

**Symbol:** A black circle containing a crescent moon, surrounded by fourteen stars

Lendor is among the oldest of the Suel powers and, according to Suel tradition, the creator of the other gods of the pantheon. He is the Prince of Time and Tedium, concerned with the inexorable passage of ages and the unfolding of events throughout the multiverse. Unlike many gods who take an active interest in the affairs of mortals, Lendor is distant and aloof. He rarely intervenes directly, and those who seek his attention may find that centuries pass before their prayers receive an answer.

Lendor's worship was once more prominent among the ancient Suel, but has declined almost to extinction. His remaining worshippers tend to be sages, historians, astrologers, elderly people, and others whose interests extend beyond the immediate concerns of mortal life. His temples are often quiet and sparsely attended, and his priests are expected to devote themselves to contemplation, study, and the preservation of ancient ritual.

#### Dogma

Time is the great measure of existence. All things have their appointed place within it, and no mortal can hasten what must come or prevent what must pass. Patience, discipline, and acceptance of the inevitable are virtues. Hasty action is a failure of understanding; promises and decisions should be made with consideration for their consequences not merely tomorrow, but years or generations hence.

The past should be remembered, for those who forget what has already occurred are condemned to repeat it. The present should be observed carefully, for the smallest event may have consequences that become apparent only with time. The future should neither be feared nor presumed upon, for it belongs to time and cannot be possessed by mortals.

### The Priesthood

The priesthood of Lendor is small, formal, and deeply conservative. Priests devote considerable time to ritual, recitation, contemplation, historical study, and the preservation of religious traditions. Services are deliberately lengthy, with prayers and passages of scripture repeated according to ancient forms. Innovation in ritual is strongly discouraged; an established rite is presumed to have endured because it has served its purpose.

Lendor's priests are rarely eager recruiters. They are more likely to instruct a patient student who demonstrates an interest in history, philosophy, astronomy, or the nature of time than to seek converts among the general population.

The priesthood has little centralized political influence, but individual priests may be respected as scholars, counselors, keepers of records, or advisers to those who must make decisions whose consequences will extend far beyond their own lifetimes.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Lendor is expected to:

- preserve and transmit historical and religious knowledge;
    
- observe the proper rites and traditions of the faith;
    
- avoid needless haste and impulsive action;
    
- contemplate the consequences of significant actions before undertaking them;
    
- maintain accurate records where practical;
    
- respect the passage of time and the natural conclusion of mortal affairs;
    
- counsel patience when circumstances permit patience;
    
- resist attempts to manipulate or unnaturally circumvent the proper course of time.
    

A priest is not forbidden from acting decisively when circumstances demand it. Lendor's teachings concern _thoughtless haste_, not inaction. A deliberate action taken after proper consideration is preferable to either reckless urgency or pointless delay.

#### Adventuring Priests

Lendor's priests rarely seek adventure for wealth or glory. Those who leave their temples commonly do so to recover lost knowledge, preserve records, investigate disturbances in the natural course of events, seek artifacts associated with the ancient Suel, or ensure that events of historical significance are properly witnessed and remembered.

Some priests also regard adventuring as an opportunity to observe the unfolding of events firsthand. Such a priest may be remarkably patient in circumstances that would leave other adventurers frustrated, preferring to understand what is happening before deciding what should be done.

### Priest of Lendor

**Ability Requirements:** Intelligence 14

**Alignment:** Lawful Neutral

**Weapons:** Any non-edged weapon

**Armor:** Leather or chain armour

**Major Spheres:** Astral, Divination, Guardian, Law, Numbers, Thought, Time

**Minor Spheres:** Elemental (Air)

**Prohibited Spheres:** None

**Granted Powers**

**3rd Level — Slow:** The priest may cast _slow_ once per day as a granted power, using the priest's normal casting rules. The reverse of this power, _haste_, is not available.

**7th Level — Temporal Resistance:** The priest becomes immune to spells and magical effects that directly alter the passage of time or impose magical temporal conditions, including _haste_, _slow_, _time stop_, and _temporal stasis_.

**12th Level — Time Stop:** The priest may cast _time stop_ once per day as a granted power.

**Holy Vestments:** Priests of Lendor traditionally wear silver robes bearing the symbol of the faith: a black circle containing a crescent moon surrounded by fourteen stars.

## Lirr

**Titles:** Lady Poet, the Lorekeeper

**Alignment:** CG

**Pantheon:** Oeridian

**Portfolio:** Prose, Poetry, Literature, Art

**Symbol:** An illustrated book

Lirr is the Oeridian goddess of literature and the arts, patron of writers, poets, artists, scholars, and those who preserve the works of civilization. She is commonly depicted as a fresh-faced Oeridian woman with dark blue eyes and long black hair, carrying a tome capable of displaying any prose, poetry, spell, or artwork ever created.

Lirr values knowledge that is shared rather than hoarded. She cooperates with those who respect learning and artistic expression, but opposes those who destroy works of art, suppress knowledge, or conceal writings from those who would benefit from them. She is allied with Delleb, though her willingness to travel, engage with the world, and challenge conservative traditions distinguishes her from the more reclusive god of reason.

#### Dogma

The written word is a foundation of civilization. Knowledge preserved only in memory may perish with its keeper, and a story left untold may be lost forever. Record the deeds of the present, preserve the wisdom of the past, and ensure that poetry, prose, and art survive for future generations.

Art is not merely ornament, nor is literature merely entertainment. Both preserve the thoughts, experiences, and aspirations of peoples. The faithful must create, collect, copy, teach, and protect these works. Oral traditions should be recorded whenever possible, and knowledge should be made accessible to those who seek it.

Those who destroy works out of ignorance or malice, or who suppress knowledge to maintain power, oppose Lirr's purpose. Her faithful must be prepared to recover endangered works and carry them beyond the reach of those who would destroy them.

### The Priesthood

Lirr's clergy include priests, scholars, scribes, tutors, poets, artists, and wandering collectors of lore. Her temples and shrines are most common in lands with substantial Oeridian populations or cultural influence. Some are attached to libraries, schools, or collections of art; others occupy modest rooms where manuscripts are copied and travellers exchange news and stories.

Lirr's priests wander throughout Oerth in search of knowledge, historical accounts, poetry, magical writings, and works of art. They sometimes enter oppressive or dangerous territories to rescue items threatened with destruction. Their discoveries are copied, catalogued, and shared so that the loss of a single library, archive, or collection cannot erase its contents entirely.

Many priests train as bards, though their interests extend beyond music and performance. They may compose poetry, write histories, create paintings, teach languages, or preserve oral traditions. Others find employment as scribes, tutors, and artists. Novices commonly spend months copying their temple's books, scrolls, and artwork, producing copies that can be distributed more widely.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Lirr is expected to:

- Preserve written works, works of art, historical records, and significant oral traditions.
    
- Copy important writings and ensure that knowledge survives the destruction or loss of its original source.
    
- Create or cultivate artistic works, whether through poetry, prose, illustration, or another art.
    
- Teach others to read, write, appreciate art, or understand the knowledge entrusted to the priest.
    
- Seek out lost lore, neglected histories, unusual works of art, and endangered collections.
    
- Oppose the deliberate destruction of knowledge and the suppression of literature or artistic expression.
    
- Share discoveries with others rather than hoarding them for personal prestige.
    
- Respect the distinction between preserving a work and endorsing everything it says; even troubling or mistaken writings may possess historical or scholarly value.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Adventuring priests of Lirr often travel as scholars, scribes, artists, tutors, or wandering collectors. They accompany expeditions to recover lost manuscripts, investigate ancient ruins, document unfamiliar cultures, and retrieve works threatened by war, conquest, or deliberate destruction.

Some specialize in magical writings and the lore of wizards; others seek histories, poetry, sculpture, painting, or examples of artistic traditions unknown in their homeland. Priests of Lirr are particularly valuable to expeditions whose discoveries might otherwise be lost, misunderstood, or destroyed.

A known shrine to Lirr is maintained at the Guildhall of Performing Artistes in the City of Greyhawk.

### Priest of Lirr

**Ability Requirements:** Intelligence 13, Wisdom 12

**Prime Requisites:** Intelligence, Wisdom

**Alignment:** Chaotic Good

**Weapons:** Dagger, staff, short spear, rapier

**Armour:** Any; light armour is preferred

**Major Spheres:** All, Creation, Divination, Thought

**Minor Spheres:** Charm, Healing, Protection, Travellers

**Granted Powers**

**Preservation of Knowledge:** Lorekeepers receive a bonus proficiency slot at 1st level, which must be spent on a proficiency related to creating, interpreting, or preserving knowledge or art. Appropriate choices include Ancient History, Artistic Ability, Languages (Ancient), Local History, Poetry, or an additional Reading/Writing proficiency. The proficiency must be one the character qualifies for under the reboot's normal rules.

**3rd Level — Comprehend Languages:** The Lorekeeper can cast _comprehend languages_ as a 1st-level wizard spell once per day. This ability represents Lirr's concern with recovering and understanding works written in unfamiliar languages.

**5th Level — Read Magic:** The Lorekeeper can cast _read magic_ as a 1st-level wizard spell once per day.

**7th Level — Illusory Script:** The Lorekeeper can cast _illusory script_ as a 3rd-level wizard spell once per day. This ability is useful for protecting sensitive writings from casual readers, though it does not replace the duty to preserve and transmit knowledge.

**10th Level — Legend Lore:** The Lorekeeper can cast _legend lore_ as a 6th-level wizard spell once per tenday. The ability allows the priest to investigate the history of a significant person, place, object, or work of art.

**Turn Undead:** Lorekeepers cannot turn or command undead.

## Llerg

**Great Bear, Animal Fang, Strongest Serpent, God of Force**

**Alignment:** Chaotic Neutral

**Portfolio:** Beasts, physical strength, endurance

**Symbol:** The head of a bear, alligator, or giant snake

Llerg is the Suel god of beasts and physical strength. He is usually depicted as a great bear, enormous alligator, giant serpent, or a short, heavily built man clad in furs and wearing a great fighting girdle. Whatever his form, he conveys an overwhelming impression of physical power.

Llerg is worshipped by those who admire strength of body and the fierce creatures of the natural world. Warriors, hunters, barbarians, and berserkers call upon him for strength and courage, while those who live in harsh wilderness regions look to him as a protector and teacher. Llerg is less concerned with civilization than with the direct lessons provided by survival, struggle, and the natural world.

Llerg regards most civilized gods with indifference, considering them too far removed from the realities of strength and survival. He maintains a friendly rivalry with Kord, whose strength is expressed through contests and sport, while Llerg represents the strength of the wild. He has a particular hatred for Telchur, whose followers once imprisoned the Suel god of winter.

#### Dogma

Watch and learn from the beasts. Emulate their strength, endurance, and adaptability. The predator is your teacher and your kin; respect it, but do not fear it. Weakness should be overcome through effort and experience.

Strength is not merely the ability to kill. Know when to fight, when to endure, and when to show restraint. A warrior who can defeat a dangerous opponent without destroying it has demonstrated greater mastery than one who relies only upon the easiest path to victory.

### The Priesthood

Llerg's priests, known as **beastwalkers**, serve as warriors, hunters, animal handlers, and advisers to their communities. They train warriors, lead hunts when food is scarce, and intervene when dangerous animals threaten settlements. Those who devote themselves to the protection of animals may also serve as priests of Llerg, particularly in regions where hunting for sport or trophies threatens the natural balance.

Temples are simple stone or wooden structures, usually built near wilderness frequented by animals. Their interiors are decorated with carvings, paintings, and preserved hides depicting Llerg's sacred beasts, with an open fire generally occupying the center of the principal chamber.

Every beastwalker chooses one of Llerg's three principal forms—the bear, alligator, or serpent—as a personal totem. Senior priests may incorporate the name of their chosen beast into their title.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Llerg is expected to:

- cultivate physical strength, endurance, and practical competence;
    
- study and respect the behavior of wild animals;
    
- protect communities from dangerous beasts without needless destruction;
    
- hunt when necessary to sustain their people;
    
- train others to defend themselves and their communities;
    
- oppose cruelty to animals committed for sport, vanity, or trophies;
    
- test themselves against worthy challenges rather than avoiding hardship.
    

At 5th level, every priest of Llerg must undertake the **Trial of the Beast**: alone and without assistance, the priest must face a bear in combat and defeat it without killing it. A priest who refuses the trial or flees from it is considered to have rejected Llerg's teachings and forfeits the favor of the god.

#### Adventuring Priests

Llerg's priests commonly adventure as hunters, wilderness guides, warriors, or protectors of isolated communities. They are particularly comfortable in harsh environments and often accept challenges that others would regard as unnecessarily dangerous. Their knowledge of animals can make them valuable companions when food is scarce or when an expedition must travel through wilderness inhabited by dangerous predators.

### Priest of Llerg

**Ability Requirements:** Strength 14, Wisdom 9

**Alignment:** Chaotic Neutral

**Weapons:** Battle axe, club, dagger, hand axe, short bow, spear

**Armor:** Any armor up to scale

**Major Spheres:** All, Animal, Combat, Healing, Protection

**Minor Spheres:** Creation, Divination, Elemental

**Prohibited Spheres:** —

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Surge of Strength:** Once per day, the priest may increase their Strength by 1d6 points, to a maximum of 18, for 1 round per level.

**3rd Level — Strengthened Form:** The priest's Strength permanently increases by 1 point, to a maximum of 18.

**5th Level — Trial of the Beast:** A priest who survives the Trial of the Beast gains the ability to track as a ranger of the same level.

**7th Level — Call of the Beast:** Once per day, the priest may summon a carnivorous animal appropriate to the surrounding terrain. The creature is normally a cave bear, alligator, or giant constrictor snake and arrives in 1d10 rounds. It remains for no more than 1 turn per level and obeys the priest's commands during that time.

**10th Level — Berserk Strength:** Once per day, the priest may enter a berserk state for 1 round per level. Their Strength becomes 18, they gain 2 temporary hit points per level, and they gain a +2 bonus to melee attack rolls. They may make two unarmed attacks per round, each inflicting 1d8 damage. Damage is deducted from the temporary hit points first. The priest may use only melee attacks while berserk. The state may be ended early by a successful saving throw vs. paralyzation.

**13th Level — Beast Form:** Once per day, the priest may assume the form of a cave bear, alligator, or giant constrictor snake. The priest retains their mind, hit points, and attack ability, but otherwise possesses the physical characteristics and abilities of the chosen creature. The transformation lasts for up to 1 turn per level. Upon returning to their normal form, the priest regains 1d12 hit points.

## Lydia

**The Scholar of Light, Lady of Song, Keeper of Knowledge**

**Alignment:** Neutral Good

**Portfolio:** Music, knowledge, daylight, education

**Symbol:** A golden lyre before a rising sun

Lydia is a wise goddess of music, knowledge, and daylight. She is favored by musicians, bards, sages, and those who seek to learn and share what they have learned. Her worship is particularly strong among women who seek education, travel, and opportunities to distinguish themselves through their own accomplishments.

Lydia commands her followers to spread knowledge through song and story, preferring accurate accounts of history and experience to deliberate fabrication. She believes that knowledge should illuminate rather than control. In this respect she stands in philosophical opposition to Pholtus: where he emphasizes the authority of the light, Lydia teaches that people must be free to see and understand for themselves.

Lydia appears as a dynamic older woman with long white hair and clear blue eyes. She wears a flowing white robe trimmed with gold and silver. Though elderly in appearance, she is depicted as energetic and alert, often carrying a book, scroll, or musical instrument.

#### Dogma

Knowledge is meant to be shared. What is learned should not be hidden merely to preserve the advantage of those who already possess it. Song and story can carry knowledge farther than books, reaching people who might otherwise never have the opportunity to learn.

Truth should be preferred to fabrication, especially when recounting history. A story may entertain, but a false account presented as truth can mislead generations. Education liberates people by giving them the ability to understand the world and make their own decisions.

Lydia's followers are taught to seek the light without becoming blinded by it. Knowledge should illuminate the mind, not replace it.

### The Priesthood

Lydia's priests are teachers, messengers, musicians, scholars, and collectors of information. They travel widely, gathering news and knowledge and carrying it between isolated communities. Many accompany priests of Fharlanghn, whose own wandering traditions make them natural companions for Lydia's messengers.

The education of women is a particular responsibility of Lydia's priesthood. Her priests are expected to assist women who seek learning and to spend time teaching reading and other useful knowledge to women and children in communities where such opportunities are limited.

Lydia's priests also maintain a tradition of recording what they learn. A priest is expected to provide an account of their discoveries when praying for spells. These reports are part of the priest's communion with the goddess and require an additional thirty minutes during spell preparation.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Lydia is expected to:

- seek useful knowledge wherever they travel;
    
- preserve accurate accounts of important events;
    
- spread knowledge through teaching, song, and story;
    
- assist women who seek education;
    
- teach literacy to women and children where practical;
    
- carry news and messages between isolated communities;
    
- distinguish historical truth from deliberate fabrication;
    
- make knowledge available to those capable of using it responsibly;
    
- record and report significant discoveries;
    
- encourage people to think and judge for themselves.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Lydia's priests frequently adventure as scholars, messengers, teachers, and chroniclers. They may accompany expeditions to record discoveries, seek lost knowledge, carry messages across dangerous territory, or investigate historical mysteries.

A Lydiaite adventurer may spend as much time speaking with villagers and recording local traditions as exploring ruins. Such priests are particularly interested in preserving knowledge that might otherwise disappear and in bringing useful learning to isolated communities.

### Priest of Lydia

**Ability Requirements:** Intelligence 11, Charisma 12

**Alignment:** Neutral Good

**Weapons:** As bard

**Armor:** As bard

**Major Spheres:** All, Astral, Charm, Divination, Healing, Protection, Summoning, Travelers

**Minor Spheres:** Elemental (All)

**Prohibited Spheres:** None

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Languages and Magic:** The priest may cast either _detect magic_ or _comprehend languages_ as a granted power. The choice may be made each time the power is used.

**3rd Level — Portents and Color:** The priest may cast either _augury_ or _color spray_ as a granted power. The choice may be made each time the power is used.

**5th Level — Illumination and Discovery:** The priest may cast either _locate object_ or _continual light_ as a granted power. The choice may be made each time the power is used.

**7th Level — Divine Knowledge:** The priest may cast either _divination_ or _rainbow pattern_ as a granted power. The choice may be made each time the power is used.

**10th Level — Rainbow:** The priest may cast _rainbow_ as a granted power.

**13th Level — Sight and Truth:** The priest may cast either _commune_ or _true seeing_ as a granted power. The choice may be made each time the power is used.

**Divine Answers:** Once per week, a priest of Lydia may use _commune_ to ask three questions of the goddess. Lydia answers completely and accurately, though her answers may be brief and she may withhold knowledge that she considers dangerous or inappropriate for the questioner to possess. This use of _commune_ is independent of the priest's normal spellcasting.

**Reporting:** When praying for spells, the priest must report significant knowledge and discoveries acquired since the previous preparation. This requires thirty additional minutes beyond the normal time required to prepare spells.

## Mayaheine

**Titles:** The Shield Maiden, Defender of the Ramparts

**Alignment:** LG

**Portfolio:** Protection, Survival, Defence, Courage

**Symbol:** A silver shield

Mayaheine is a demipower of protection and survival who rose from mortal ranks as an epic hero and paladin of Pelor. She came to Oerth from an unknown alternate world on the Prime Material Plane, travelling with Pelor's aid to defend the people of the Flanaess. Her arrival has been taken by many as a sign of hope and potential salvation, particularly in lands threatened by war and evil.

Mayaheine is portrayed as a strikingly tall woman with tanned skin, blue eyes, and auburn-gold hair. She wears silvered plate armour and carries a magical bastard sword. She is a formidable warrior, but her defining role is that of protector. She is depicted defending beleaguered communities, seeking protective magical items for those in need, and standing between the vulnerable and those who would destroy them. Her magical shield is said to be capable of melding into stone and protecting city walls from fire, frost, and magical assault, while her longbow can loose arrows almost to the horizon.

#### Dogma

Protection is both a duty and an act of courage. Those who are strong must use their strength to defend those who cannot defend themselves, and those who possess knowledge or resources that can preserve a community are obligated to share them when lives are at stake.

Mayaheine's faithful do not wait for danger to reach the walls. Defences should be built before they are needed, weapons maintained before battle begins, and communities prepared to endure hardship before it arrives. The poor and downtrodden deserve protection no less than the wealthy and powerful.

Defence does not require passivity. When threats cannot be deterred, Mayaheine's followers are expected to meet them openly and stand their ground.

### The Priesthood

Mayaheine's priesthood is young and remains relatively small. Many of its early priests received assistance from the established priesthood of Pelor, and Mayaheine's priests continue to hold Pelor's servants in high regard.

Her priests train for combat and self-defence, aid threatened communities, and travel to frontier settlements where their skills are most needed. They are often practical rather than ceremonial, helping communities construct fortifications, organize defences, and prepare for attack.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Mayaheine is expected to:

- Defend the innocent, particularly the poor and downtrodden.
    
- Aid communities threatened by war, raiders, monsters, or other dangers.
    
- Help construct and maintain practical defences.
    
- Train others in self-defence and the use of defensive equipment.
    
- Consecrate weapons, armour, and shields used in the defence of others.
    
- Seek out protective magic and place it in the hands of those who need it.
    
- Provide material aid to those whose homes or livelihoods have been threatened or destroyed.
    

### Adventuring Priests

Mayaheine's priests often adventure in response to threats against vulnerable communities. They seek magical weapons and defensive relics, investigate dangers along threatened borders, and confront those responsible for attacks upon the innocent. A priest may also undertake an expedition specifically to recover a protective magical item for a community that cannot otherwise obtain it.

### Priest of Mayaheine

**Ability Requirements:** Strength 14 or Charisma 13

**Alignment:** Lawful Good

**Weapons:** Bastard sword, flail, lance, longbow and arrows, longsword, mace, staff

**Armour:** Chain or plate

**Major Spheres:** Combat, Guardian, Healing, Law, Protection, Sun, Wards

**Minor Spheres:** Charm, Necromantic

**Granted Powers**

**3rd Level — Protective Aura:** The priest may cast _protection from evil 10-foot radius_ with double its normal duration.

**5th Level — Cloak of Bravery:** The priest may cast _cloak of bravery_.

**9th Level — Greater Protection:** The priest may use 6th-level spells from the Protection and Wards spheres.

**Turn Undead:** Turn

## Meyanok

**Titles:** The Serpent, Father of Darkness, The Whisperer

**Alignment:** Neutral Evil

**Portfolio:** Serpents, Poison, Discord, Darkness, Famine

Meyanok was born of darkness and pain and is the progenitor of the other evil gods of the Touv pantheon. He seeks to corrupt the rest of his divine family and control or destroy their servants. Outnumbered by the other gods, Meyanok prefers subterfuge to open confrontation, working through deception, poison, hunger, and agents who may not even realize that they serve him.

Meyanok is always depicted as a serpent coiled around a skull.

#### Dogma

Trust is weakness. Cooperation creates strength, but it also creates opportunities for betrayal. Meyanok teaches his followers to exploit those opportunities. A community that can be divided can be conquered, and a people deprived of food or hope will turn upon one another without needing an enemy to attack them.

The faithful should conceal their intentions, cultivate useful relationships, and allow others to believe that they serve their interests. Poison is preferable to the blade, hunger preferable to battle, and a corrupted servant preferable to a slain enemy.

Meyanok's followers are not expected to seek destruction for its own sake. They are expected to undermine whatever stands against them. The servants of other gods should be deceived, compromised, turned against one another, or destroyed when they can no longer be useful.

### The Priesthood

The priests and shamans of Meyanok are secretive and reclusive. They rarely deal openly with strangers and often maintain the appearance of ordinary members of the communities in which they live. Much of their work is performed through agents, servants, criminals, desperate individuals, and those whose wills have been broken or enchanted.

Meyanok's priests seek to disrupt communities, undermine rival faiths, and harm those who oppose their god. They cultivate discord between families, leaders, settlements, and religious groups. Some use poison, while others engineer shortages, sabotage food stores, or manipulate existing conflicts until they become violent.

The most devoted priests are willing to make sacrifices to Meyanok, including human sacrifices. Such rituals are normally concealed from the wider community, since discovery would bring the wrath of both the authorities and rival faiths.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Meyanok is expected to:

- Conceal the true nature of the faith from outsiders whenever possible.
    
- Undermine the servants and worshippers of rival gods.
    
- Encourage discord among communities, families, and leaders who might oppose Meyanok.
    
- Use deception, poison, hunger, and manipulation rather than open confrontation whenever practical.
    
- Recruit agents and cultivate useful servants.
    
- Maintain secret shrines and places of sacrifice.
    
- Corrupt or compromise the servants of rival faiths.
    
- Destroy food, supplies, and other necessities when doing so weakens an enemy.
    
- Make sacrifices to Meyanok when commanded by the priesthood.
    
- Never reveal the plans or identities of fellow servants without compelling reason.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Meyanok's priests adventure primarily as infiltrators, manipulators, and agents. They may travel under false identities, establish contacts in distant communities, gather intelligence, sabotage supplies, or seek objects and knowledge that can be used against rival faiths.

Some operate as the hidden hand behind apparently unrelated events, arranging conflicts between other groups while remaining far from the resulting violence. Others accompany adventuring parties precisely because such groups provide access to places and people that a secret cult could not easily reach.

### Priest of Meyanok

**Ability Requirements:** Wisdom 13

**Alignment:** Neutral Evil or Chaotic Evil

**Weapons:** Atlatl, dagger, hand axe, javelin, short bow, short sword

**Armor:** Any

**Major Spheres:** All, Animal, Charm, Combat, Divination, Elemental, Guardian, Healing, Protection, Summoning

**Minor Spheres:** Animal, Creation, Elemental, Guardian, Plant, Sun

**Prohibited Spheres:**

**Magical Items:** A priest of Meyanok must wear a snakeskin headdress or cloak. The snakeskin is normally taken from a venomous serpent and treated as a sacred relic.

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Serpent's Servant:** The priest may charm normal snakes as though using _snake charm_. The priest is also completely immune to the venom of normal and giant snakes.

**3rd Level — Darkness or Snakeskin:** The priest may cast either _darkness_ or _snakeskin_ once per day. _Darkness_ is the reverse of _light_. _Snakeskin_ is a variant of _barkskin_ that manifests as a protective layer of overlapping scales.

**5th Level — Snakes to Sticks:** The priest may cast _snakes to sticks_ once per day. This is the reverse of _sticks to snakes_ and transforms snakes into ordinary sticks.

**7th Level — Poison:** The priest may cast _poison_ once per day. This is the reverse of _neutralize poison_.

**10th Level — Confusion:** The priest may cast _confusion_ once per day.

**13th Level — Defoliate:** The priest may cast _defoliate_ once per day.

**Turn Undead:** A priest of Meyanok commands undead rather than turning them.

## Mictlantecuhtli

**Titles:** Lord of the Dead, Lord of Mictlan

**Alignment:** LE

**Pantheon:** Olman

**Portfolio:** Death, Unlife, War

**Symbol:** A red or jade rod

Mictlantecuhtli is the greater god of death and the power of unlife. He is normally depicted as a tall, lich-like being, though he is also represented as a skeletal canine. Death is not merely an end in his eyes, but a source of power and servitude. The dead belong to him, and those who die in his service may continue to serve him as undead.

Mictlantecuhtli's worship is deliberately costly. His followers traditionally seek his attention by sacrificing fifty members of their own community during the dark of the moons. Those sacrificed are thereafter said to serve the god as undead. He is also a warlike deity who encourages his followers to send their enemies to him as slaves in the afterlife.

#### Dogma

Death is inevitable, and those who die belong to Mictlantecuhtli. The dead should not be allowed to escape their appointed fate without divine permission. Enemies defeated in life are merely future servants of the god, and mercy toward a captured foe is considered a denial of Mictlantecuhtli's authority.

The god's priests are expected to accept death without fear or sentimentality, and to ensure that the dead are properly delivered into his keeping.

### The Priesthood

Mictlantecuhtli's priests are grim, cold, and uncompromising. They show no mercy to captured enemies and sacrifice them at the first opportunity. They oversee the internment of those who die in battle as well as those who die naturally, ensuring that the dead are properly accounted for and their remains placed under the god's authority.

A priest of Mictlantecuhtli must receive the god's permission before using _raise dead_ or _resurrection_. Such magic is not considered a normal right of the priesthood, but a temporary release from the god's claim over the dead. A priest who has not received permission cannot cast either spell.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Mictlantecuhtli is expected to:

- Conduct funerary rites and oversee the internment of the dead.
    
- Ensure that those slain in battle are properly accounted for and delivered to the god.
    
- Show no mercy to captured enemies, who are potential servants of Mictlantecuhtli.
    
- Maintain the rites and sacrifices by which the god is honoured.
    
- Observe the appointed sacrifices during the dark of the moons.
    
- Seek the god's permission before returning the dead to life.
    
- Treat undeath as a sacred manifestation of the god's power rather than an abomination.
    
- Preserve the graves and resting places placed under the priesthood's care.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Mictlantecuhtli's priests adventure to claim the dead of fallen enemies, recover remains, punish those who have escaped death through forbidden means, and further the god's influence through war. They may accompany armies as priests and funerary authorities, ensuring that the dead are properly consecrated—or claimed.

### Priest of Mictlantecuhtli

**Ability Requirements:** Strength 12, Constitution 15

**Alignment:** NE, N

**Weapons:** Club, dagger, javelin, staff

**Armour:** Any

**Major Spheres:** All, Astral, Combat, Elemental, Necromantic, War

**Minor Spheres:** Charm, Divination, Healing, Law

**Prohibited Spheres:**

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Death's Touch:** The priest may cast _chill touch_ or _invisibility to undead_ once per day.

**3rd Level — Servant of Death:** The priest may cast _spectral hand_ or _ray of enfeeblement_ once per day.

**5th Level — Voice of the Dead:** The priest may cast _cause disease_ (the reverse of _cure disease_) or _speak with dead_ once per day.

**7th Level — Enervation:** The priest may cast _enervation_ once per day.

**10th Level — Slay Living:** The priest may cast _slay living_ (the reverse of _raise dead_) once per day.

**13th Level — Withering Touch:** The priest may cast _harm_ (the reverse of _heal_) once per day.

**15th Level — Unholy Word:** The priest may cast _unholy word_ (the reverse of _holy word_) once per day.

**Turn Undead:** Command

## Mouqol

**The Merchant, Master of Bargains, Keeper of the Market**

**Alignment:** N

**Pantheon:** Baklunish

**Portfolio:** Trade, negotiation, ventures, appraisal, reciprocity

**Symbol:** Scales and merchant's weights

Mouqol is the god of commerce and exchange, but he is not a god of wealth for its own sake. To Mouqol, the value of a thing is demonstrated by what another person is willing to give for it. He teaches that every bargain is an exchange of interests, and that the skilled merchant seeks an agreement from which both parties benefit.

Mouqol is associated with merchants, traders, caravaneers, moneychangers, negotiators, and those who seek rare or difficult-to-obtain goods. He is equally comfortable dealing with mortals, genies, and other supernatural beings. He is said to understand what people truly desire, even when they themselves cannot express it, and to be able to arrange exchanges that would otherwise seem impossible.

#### Dogma

Know the value of what you possess, and learn the desires of those with whom you deal. Trade fairly, but do not confuse fairness with equality; each party must decide what something is worth to them. Take risks when the reward justifies them, but do not mistake recklessness for courage. Wealth is a tool, not an end. Fraud, theft, and coercion corrupt exchange, while a freely accepted bargain creates obligations between its participants. Seek opportunities where others see only disagreement, and remember that the greatest bargain is one in which every party believes it has gained.

### The Priesthood

The priests of Mouqol serve as merchants, brokers, appraisers, moneychangers, caravan advisers, and arbiters of commercial disputes. Many temples stand within marketplaces and serve simultaneously as shrines, counting houses, warehouses, and places where contracts are witnessed. Priests are frequently consulted when the value or authenticity of an unusual object is uncertain, or when two parties cannot reach an agreement.

Mouqol's clergy are not expected to accumulate great personal wealth. A priest who hoards wealth without purpose is considered to have misunderstood the god. Successful priests instead use their resources to facilitate further trade, support caravans and communities, and ensure that valuable goods reach those who can make proper use of them.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Mouqol is expected to:

- Deal honestly in matters of trade and negotiation.
    
- Learn the value, origin, and quality of goods offered in exchange.
    
- Expose fraud, counterfeit goods, and magical deception.
    
- Honor freely accepted bargains and contracts.
    
- Distinguish legitimate risk from reckless gambling.
    
- Seek mutually beneficial solutions to disputes.
    
- Protect merchants, caravans, markets, and legitimate trade.
    
- Share knowledge of markets, routes, prices, and opportunities with the faithful.
    
- Give aid to those whose legitimate trade has been ruined by fraud, theft, or disaster.
    
- Remember that wealth is valuable because it can be exchanged, not because it can be hoarded.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Priests of Mouqol commonly adventure as caravan leaders, merchant representatives, appraisers, negotiators, and seekers of rare goods. A priest may undertake an expedition to recover a lost commodity, establish a new trade route, broker peace between competing interests, or obtain an object that cannot be found through ordinary commerce. Mouqol's servants are particularly willing to deal with unusual peoples and supernatural beings when a mutually acceptable bargain can be found.

### Priest of Mouqol

**Ability Requirements:** INT12 WIS11

**Alignment:** N

**Weapons:** Dagger, knife, light crossbow, mace, spear, and other weapons commonly used by merchants and travelers

**Armor:** Any up to chain mail

**Minor Spheres:** Charm, Combat, Creation, Law, Summoning

**Prohibited Spheres:** —

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Appraiser:** A priest of Mouqol can determine the approximate market value, quality, and authenticity of an ordinary object after examining it for one turn. Magical deception or extraordinarily rare objects may require a proficiency check or appropriate spell.

**3rd Level — Discern Deception:** The priest can detect deliberate falsehood or fraudulent representation in a commercial negotiation. This does not reveal the truth, but alerts the priest when a speaker knowingly misrepresents a material fact. The power may be used once per day.

**5th Level — Create Food & Water:** The priest may cast _create food & water_ once per day without expending a spell slot.

**7th Level — Divination:** The priest may cast _divination_ once per week without requiring its material components. Questions concerning trade, travel, valuable objects, or negotiations are particularly favored.

**10th Level — Master of Bargains:** Once per day, the priest may invoke Mouqol's favor when negotiating an agreement. For one negotiation, the priest gains an intuitive understanding of what each participant most desires from the bargain. This does not compel anyone to accept an agreement, nor does it reveal private thoughts unrelated to the negotiation.

**13th Level — Find the Desired:** Once per week, the priest may petition Mouqol to identify the location or most likely means of obtaining a specific nonunique object or commodity known to the priest. The answer provides a direction, person, place, or opportunity through which the object may reasonably be obtained, but the priest must still complete the necessary journey or bargain.

## Myhriss

**Titles:** The Thrice-Kissed, Maid of Light and Dark

**Alignment:** Neutral Good

**Pantheon:** Flan

**Portfolio:** Love, Romance, Beauty

**Symbol:** A lovebird or a garland of flowers.

Myhriss is the goddess of love, romance, and beauty. She is commonly depicted as a young Flan woman wearing a garland of flowers, though her appearance varies between two principal aspects. One is a dark-haired, provocative beauty whose bearing suggests passion and determination; the other is a sun-blonde, tanned woman whose approachable prettiness suggests warmth and gentleness. The former is associated with the whip, the latter with the shortbow.

Myhriss teaches that love can mend divisions that reason and force cannot, and that beauty may inspire hope even in those who have abandoned it. Her concerns encompass romantic love, affection between friends and family, artistic expression, and the beauty of the natural world. She does not demand that beauty conform to a single ideal, for its forms are many, and even things that inspire fear may possess terrible splendour.

#### Dogma

Love can cure many of the world's ills. Where rivals quarrel and nations make war, affection may accomplish what threats and arguments cannot. Do not assume that hatred is permanent, or that a heart hardened by suffering is beyond reach. Offer kindness, foster understanding, and preserve the possibility of reconciliation even when success seems unlikely.

Beauty is precious, but it is not confined to youth, symmetry, or pleasing appearances. It may be found in a wild landscape, a work of art, a courageous deed, a familiar face, or the fierce majesty of a dangerous creature. Seek beauty wherever it exists, and help others recognise what they might otherwise overlook.

Love is not merely a feeling to be celebrated but a bond to be cultivated and protected. Help lovers overcome needless obstacles, whether those arise from prejudice, family pressure, social convention, or fear. Encourage those whose relationships have faltered to rediscover affection where it remains, but do not confuse reconciliation with an obligation to endure cruelty. Love cannot excuse every wrong, and no one should be compelled to offer affection against their will.

### The Priesthood

The priesthood of Myhriss is welcoming, sociable, and widely dispersed. Its members are often romantic in temperament, attentive to the affections and aspirations of those around them, and eager to discover beauty in unfamiliar people and places. They may serve as artists, musicians, matchmakers, counsellors, wedding celebrants, or diplomats. Others devote themselves to protecting places of beauty, preserving works of art, or opposing those who use cruelty and hatred to destroy the happiness of others.

Priests of Myhriss are charged with promoting love wherever they can. They bless lovers and perform marriage ceremonies, but their responsibilities extend beyond the wedding day. They counsel couples in difficulty, help families reconcile, and intervene when prejudice or social obligations threaten to separate people who sincerely wish to be together. Some act as advocates for lovers whose families oppose their unions; others help couples negotiate the practical difficulties of marriage, household, and community.

The priesthood does not regard every romance as wise, nor does it assume that every obstacle to a relationship is unjust. Its members are expected to distinguish between needless prejudice and legitimate concerns about a person's welfare. They may encourage, persuade, and counsel, but the use of enchantment to manufacture affection or override another person's wishes is a matter of serious ethical concern. A relationship obtained through coercion is not the fulfilment of Myhriss's ideals.

Temples and shrines may be found in towns and cities, particularly places known for art, music, gardens, courtship, and celebration. Their character varies according to local custom. Some are elegant sanctuaries filled with flowers, sculpture, and music; others are modest establishments where people may seek counsel, celebrate a marriage, or find refuge after a personal loss.

A temple of Myhriss is expected to offer sanctuary to lovers threatened with violence or persecution because of their relationships. Some maintain discreet quarters for those who must escape hostile families or powerful opponents. Priests may attempt to reconcile the parties involved, but the safety of those seeking sanctuary takes precedence over appeasing those who pursue them.

The faith does not require its priests to be beautiful by conventional standards, nor does it teach that physical attractiveness makes one virtuous. A priest is expected to recognise and celebrate beauty in its many forms, including qualities that may not be immediately apparent. The clergy are often charismatic, but compassion, sensitivity, and the ability to appreciate others matter more than appearance.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Myhriss is expected to:

- Celebrate love, affection, romance, and beauty wherever they are found.
    
- Bless lovers and solemnise marriages, respecting the customs and sincere commitments of those involved.
    
- Help reconcile estranged lovers, friends, families, and communities when reconciliation is possible and desired.
    
- Advocate for lovers separated by prejudice, unreasonable family demands, or social convention.
    
- Provide sanctuary to those threatened because of their relationships, protecting them while seeking a peaceful resolution where possible.
    
- Counsel couples experiencing distrust or disaffection, helping them rebuild their relationships when both are willing.
    
- Protect those whose relationships or happiness are threatened by cruelty, coercion, or deliberate humiliation.
    
- Create, preserve, or encourage works of beauty, including music, poetry, visual arts, and the cultivation of gardens.
    
- Seek out unfamiliar people, places, and artistic traditions, broadening their understanding of beauty and affection.
    
- Oppose needless destruction of beautiful places and things, while recognising that preservation must not take precedence over the lives and welfare of the innocent.
    
- Remember that love cannot be compelled, and that jealousy, possession, and domination are not substitutes for genuine affection.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Priests of Myhriss adventure to discover beauty, nurture affection, and oppose forces that make the world more hateful or desolate. They may travel in search of remarkable works of art or natural wonders, recover treasures of cultural importance, or protect artists and communities threatened by war. Some act as diplomats, using their charm and sensitivity to encourage negotiation between hostile parties. Others confront those who destroy beauty out of malice or exploit love to manipulate and control others.

A priest may become involved in a romance, a family dispute, or a conflict between communities because of the people affected rather than any immediate religious objective. Such involvement is entirely in keeping with the faith, provided the priest respects the wishes of those concerned. Myhriss's servants seek to encourage love, not to decide on another person's behalf whom they must love.

### Priest of Myhriss

**Ability Requirements:** Wisdom 10, Charisma 13. _(Proposed.)_

**Alignment:** Neutral Good.

**Weapons:** Shortbow, whip, club, lasso, mancatcher, and net. _(The shortbow and whip reflect Myhriss's traditional aspects; the remaining weapons are proposed from the generic love-priesthood model.)_

**Armour:** None; no shields. _(Proposed.)_

**Major Spheres:** All, Charm, Healing, Necromantic, Protection, Summoning.

**Minor Spheres:** Creation, Divination, Guardian, Plant, Sun, Weather.

**Granted Powers**

The precise granted powers of Myhriss's priesthood are not established by the sources currently available. The following are potential adaptations of the generic love-priesthood rules, subject to review against the reboot's existing spell and proficiency mechanics.

**1st Level — Eye for Beauty:** The priest has a practiced appreciation for artistic, natural, and personal beauty. Given time to observe or examine something, the priest may recognise exceptional craftsmanship, aesthetic qualities, or details that an untrained observer might overlook. This is a proficiency-like benefit rather than supernatural detection, and does not reveal hidden magic, moral character, or a person's feelings.

**3rd Level — Encouragement:** Once per day, the priest may offer words, music, or a gesture of sincere affection that helps restore another person's confidence. The recipient gains a +1 bonus to their next saving throw against fear or despair within the following turn. This ability cannot compel affection, erase justified fear, or remove the consequences of trauma.

**7th Level — Reconciliation:** Once per day, the priest may attempt to calm an emotionally charged disagreement between willing participants. Those involved receive a saving throw versus spell to resist the effect. On a failure, immediate hostility subsides for the duration of the encounter, allowing the parties to converse without escalating into violence solely from anger. The effect does not compel agreement, forgiveness, trust, or romantic attraction, and ends if a participant is attacked or placed in immediate danger.

**Turn Undead:** As a standard priest.

## Nerull

**Titles:** Foe of All Good, Hater of Life, Bringer of Darkness, Reaper of Flesh

**Alignment:** NE

**Portfolio:** Death, Murder, Darkness, Slaughter

**Symbol:** A rust-red or black skeletal hand

Nerull is the coldest and most openly hateful of the Flan powers. He is not a god of peaceful death, release, or the natural passing of mortal life. Nerull brings slaughter, delights in suffering, and seeks the destruction of all living things. He is a patron of murderers and those who pursue evil for pleasure or gain. He summons fiends, conjures darkness, and his touch is said to wither flesh and reduce the living to dust.

Nerull's worship is conducted in complete darkness. His litany recounts death and suffering, while bizarre and terrifying offerings are laid upon altars of rusty-red stone. His temples are hidden wherever his worshippers can establish themselves, particularly in evil lands. A few subterranean shrines are said to exist even beneath otherwise civilized regions.

Common folk do not ordinarily attempt to appease Nerull, even during war, plague, or other times of great death. To beseech the Reaper is thought to draw his attention to oneself, with potentially fatal consequences.

#### Dogma

Life is weakness, and death is its inevitable end. The living exist only to be destroyed. Mercy is weakness, compassion is contemptible, and suffering is to be embraced rather than relieved. Nerull's faithful are taught that death should not merely be accepted but actively brought to others.

Nerull's servants do not seek a natural balance between life and death. They seek the triumph of death over life.

### The Priesthood

Nerull's priests are murderous, psychopathic, cold, cruel, and utterly evil. They are necessarily secretive, for few communities would tolerate their open practices. Most priesthoods operate as isolated cells with little or no authority over one another, although established hierarchies may exist in lands where Nerull's worship is openly tolerated.

Priests are expected to cultivate an utter disregard for the lives of others. Murder, torture, and acts of calculated cruelty are not merely permitted but may be regarded as demonstrations of devotion. A priest who kills without purpose is no less faithful than one who carefully chooses a victim; what matters is that life is destroyed and Nerull's hatred of the living is advanced.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Nerull is expected to:

- Preserve the secrecy of Nerull's worship and protect the identities of fellow priests.
    
- Conduct rites and offerings in darkness upon a proper altar.
    
- Kill when opportunity and circumstance permit, particularly when doing so advances Nerull's interests.
    
- Show no mercy to those who have fallen into their power.
    
- Defile places associated with life, healing, or benevolence when doing so can be accomplished safely.
    
- Spread death and terror without needlessly exposing the priesthood.
    
- Never offer prayers or sacrifices to Nerull in the expectation of mercy or protection; Nerull's favour is earned through destruction.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Nerull's priests rarely adventure for the sake of exploration or personal glory. They travel to spread their master's influence, establish hidden cells, recover objects associated with death, eliminate enemies of the faith, or seek places where death can be inflicted on a significant scale. Some masquerade as ordinary adventurers until an opportunity arises to betray their companions.

### Priest of Nerull

**Ability Requirements:** Standard

**Alignment:** Any evil

**Weapons:** Dagger, knife, great sickle (treat as hookfauchard), staff, staff-sling

**Armour:** Any

**Major Spheres:** Astral, Combat, Guardian

**Minor Spheres:** Charm, Divination, Healing (reverse), Necromantic (reverse), Sun (reverse)

**Magical Items:** None

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Unsettling Presence:** Nerull's priests are so cold and disturbing that they are difficult to catch unprepared. They are surprised only on a roll of 1 on 1d6.

**6th Level — Evard's Black Tentacles:** At 6th level, the priest gains the ability to cast _Evard's black tentacles_ once per day, using the wizard spell's normal effects.

**12th Level — Destruction:** At 12th level, the priest gains the ability to cast _destruction_ once per week. This power may instead be used to cast the reversed form of _resurrection_, if applicable under the normal rules for reversed priest spells.

**Turn Undead:** Command

## Nola

**Titles:** The Sun Mother, The Life-Giver, She Who Reveals

**Alignment:** Neutral Good

**Portfolio:** Sun, Growth, Nurture, Revelation

**Symbol:** A golden sun surrounded by rays of flame

Nola was the first being created by Uvot. She represents the life-giving power of sunlight and its ability to illuminate what darkness conceals. She is a nurturing goddess who values growth and potential and abhors deadly cold and those who destroy living things before they have had the opportunity to mature.

Nola is depicted as a Touv woman of serene beauty, her head surrounded by a radiant corona of flame.

#### Dogma

Life needs warmth and time to grow. Nola teaches her followers to nurture what is young, protect what is vulnerable, and give people and living things the opportunity to develop before judging what they may become. Those who destroy something merely because it is small, immature, inconvenient, or imperfect deny the potential within it.

The sun reveals what darkness conceals. Nothing worthy of protection should depend upon secrecy or deception, and those who act in darkness should not assume that their deeds will remain hidden forever. Yet revelation should serve understanding rather than cruelty; exposing another's weakness merely for the pleasure of causing harm is not Nola's way.

Nola opposes needless destruction and the coldness of heart that permits it. Her followers are expected to be sources of warmth, encouragement, and protection to those around them.

### The Priesthood

Nola's priests and shamans concern themselves with the growth and development of living things, particularly children. They serve as teachers, caregivers, protectors, and counselors and are often deeply involved in the raising and education of young people.

Nola's priests encourage others to reach their potential rather than demanding that they conform to a predetermined ideal. They are patient with those still learning and are expected to protect the vulnerable from those who would exploit or destroy them.

Adventuring priests often regard their companions as a family placed in their care. They watch over the group's physical and emotional well-being, encourage inexperienced companions to mature, and may become distinctly parental toward younger or less experienced adventurers.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Nola is expected to:

- Protect children and other vulnerable people from harm.
    
- Encourage the growth and development of living things.
    
- Teach and nurture those who are young or inexperienced.
    
- Give people reasonable opportunities to learn from mistakes and mature.
    
- Expose dangerous deception and bring hidden wrongdoing into the light when doing so protects others.
    
- Provide warmth, food, shelter, and care to those in need.
    
- Oppose needless destruction and cruelty.
    
- Resist those who would extinguish a promising life or endeavor before it has had the opportunity to flourish.
    
- Serve as a source of hope and encouragement during hardship.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Nola's priests often adventure because they see an adventuring company as a collection of people with unrealized potential. They protect inexperienced companions, teach where they can, and encourage their companions to become better versions of themselves.

They are also drawn to places where life is threatened by unnatural cold, darkness, or destruction. A Nola priest may undertake an expedition to rescue children, protect a vulnerable community, restore a damaged settlement, or expose a hidden threat before it has the opportunity to destroy those under the priest's care.

### Priest of Nola

**Ability Requirements:** Charisma 13

**Alignment:** Neutral Good or Lawful Good

**Weapons:** Atlatl, dagger, hand axe, javelin, short bow, spear

**Armor:** Any

**Major Spheres:** All, Divination, Elemental (Fire, Air), Healing, Protection, Sun

**Minor Spheres:** Charm, Guardian, Necromantic, Weather

**Prohibited Spheres:**

**Magical Items:** A priest of Nola must wear a headdress and collar of copper and gold.

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Endure Heat:** The priest may endure extreme heat without discomfort and gains a natural resistance to heat and fire sufficient to withstand ordinary environmental extremes. The priest may also cast _endure heat_ once per day if the spell is distinct from this innate protection in the reboot's spell rules.

**3rd Level — Flame Blade:** The priest may cast _flame blade_ once per day.

**5th Level — Dispel Magic:** The priest may cast _dispel magic_ once per day.

**7th Level — Truth Revealed:** The priest may cast _detect lie_ once per day.

**10th Level — Flame Strike:** The priest may cast _flame strike_ once per day.

**13th Level — Animate Object:** The priest may cast _animate object_ once per day.

**Turn Undead:** A priest of Nola may turn undead normally.

## Norebo

**Titles:** The Gambler, The Risk-Taker, The Laughing Rogue

**Alignment:** Chaotic Neutral

**Pantheon:** Suel

**Portfolio:** Luck, gambling, risk, thieves

**Symbol:** A dagger crossed with a pair of dice
