Norebo is one of the most popular of the Suel gods. His temples, commonly called the **Churches of the Big Gamble**, can be found throughout the barbarian states as well as in more civilized lands. Norebo admires those willing to risk much for the chance of gaining much, and he is the patron of thieves and others who live by chance, daring, and opportunity. Though he chafes against the restrictions of law, he has been the lover of the lawful Wee Jas, on and off, for more than a thousand years.

Norebo is normally depicted as an unremarkable man of average height, weight, and appearance, although he is known to assume many different forms. He enjoys minor magic and clever tricks, but considers it unsporting to use magic to remove all risk from a theft or similar undertaking.

#### Dogma

Chance rewards the bold. A life without risk is a life without opportunity, and those who never wager anything can never win anything of consequence. Take risks knowingly, accept the consequences of failure, and never mistake certainty for courage. Gambling should be played fairly, but the stakes should be real.

Norebo's followers are expected to respect the unpredictable nature of fortune. They may seek to improve their chances, but should not attempt to eliminate chance altogether.

### The Priesthood

Norebo's priests are gamblers, thieves, wanderers, and professional risk-takers. Many operate or own gambling houses, while others travel from place to place deliberately introducing uncertainty and excitement into otherwise predictable lives. A priest of Norebo can be persuaded to wager on almost anything, provided the stakes are worth the risk.

Norebo's priests possess the practical abilities of thieves, gaining the abilities to hide in shadows, move silently, climb walls, and remove traps as a thief of their level. They use the standard thief percentages for their level.

Norebo's clergy frequently needle priests and followers of lawful or authoritarian faiths, particularly those of St. Cuthbert and Pholtus. They are not required to be thieves, but those who become thieves are regarded as particularly favored servants of the god.

Norebo permits the use of magic, but considers it contrary to the spirit of a proper gamble to use magic to make a theft completely one-sided. In particular, a thief who follows Norebo should not use spells to incapacitate or kill a target merely to guarantee the success of a theft, such as casting _hold person_ before making a backstab.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Norebo is expected to:

- Accept reasonable risks rather than allowing fear to dictate every decision.
    
- Honour wagers once they have been fairly made.
    
- Give others a genuine opportunity to win or lose when participating in a game of chance.
    
- Encourage cleverness, daring, and initiative.
    
- Oppose needless restrictions that prevent individuals from taking risks or choosing their own fortunes.
    
- Protect the traditions and reputation of the gambling houses and Churches of the Big Gamble.
    
- Avoid using magic to remove all meaningful risk from a theft or wager.
    
- Accept both good fortune and bad fortune without claiming that either was owed to them.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Norebo's priests adventure because adventuring is itself a gamble. They seek lost fortunes, unexplored places, dangerous wagers, unusual opportunities, and situations in which success is uncertain. Some travel to establish new gambling houses or test their luck against distant peoples; others simply believe that a settled life offers too little opportunity for fortune to change.

### Priest of Norebo

**Ability Requirements:** DEX 14

**Alignment:** Chaotic Neutral

**Weapons:** As thief

**Armor:** As thief, plus chain armour

**Major Spheres:** All, Chaos, Combat, Healing

**Minor Spheres:** Charm, Divination, Elemental (All), Guardian, Numbers, Protection, Sun (Reversed), Travelers

**Prohibited Spheres:** None

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Gambler's Hand:** The priest may make two attacks per round when armed with a dagger or sling. In addition, the priest may cast _knock_ once per day using the normal priest casting rules.

**3rd Level — The Changing Face:** The priest may cast _alter self_ or _wizard lock_ once per day.

**5th Level — Break the Odds:** The priest may cast _dispel magic_ or _phantom steed_ once per day.

**7th Level — Favoured by Fortune:** The priest's Dexterity increases by 1, to a maximum of 19. The priest may also cast _polymorph self_ once per day.

**10th Level — Threefold Gamble:** The priest may make three attacks per round when armed with a dagger or sling. The priest may also cast _seeming_ once per day.

**13th Level — Find the Path:** The priest may cast _find the path_ once per day.

**Turn Undead:** Norebo's priests cannot turn or command undead.

**Holy Vestments:** Norebo's priests wear brown or dark green robes when acting in their religious capacity. Individual priests often add dice, cards, daggers, or other symbols of gambling and thievery to their dress.

## Obad-Hai

**Titles:** The Shalm, Old Bubbling Brook, The Old Man of the Forests

**Alignment:** N

**Portfolio:** Nature, Wilderness, Druids, Natural Balance

**Symbol:** A wooden staff

Obad-Hai is an ancient Flan deity of nature and wilderness whose worship has spread throughout the Flanaess. He is a lover of the wild and a patron of those who live in harmony with their natural surroundings. More than any other Flan Power, he is associated with the druids who dwell far from settled lands.

Obad-Hai's neutrality is fundamental to his nature. He does not regard civilization as inherently evil, nor does he believe that the wilderness must always prevail over settled lands. Predators kill prey, storms destroy forests, and fire both consumes and renews the land. Good and evil are part of the mortal world, but neither defines nature itself.

This philosophy puts him at odds with Ehlonna. Where Ehlonna actively protects good woodland peoples and opposes those who despoil the forest, Obad-Hai is concerned first with the natural order itself. Their followers may cooperate when their interests coincide, but the two faiths are not interchangeable.

Obad-Hai is usually represented as a lean, weathered old man resembling a hermit, pilgrim, or simple rustic. He may also appear as a gnome, dwarf, or halfling, and is sometimes represented by woodland animals of many kinds. His shrines are generally simple wooden structures in rustic or wilderness settings.

Services involve the consecration of living flowers, earth, water, and fire, reaffirming the four elements and the living world they sustain.

#### Dogma

Nature does not require mortal approval. The wilderness is neither kind nor cruel; it simply is. Those who live within it must learn its ways rather than attempting to impose their own upon it.

Respect the land, take what is needed, waste nothing, and accept that life and death, growth and decay, predator and prey are all parts of the natural order. Do not destroy merely for pleasure, but neither assume that everything in nature must be preserved.

Civilization has its place, provided it does not consume the wilderness upon which it depends. The wild must have places where it remains wild.

### The Priesthood

Obad-Hai's specialty priests are, in essence, druids, except that they recognize Obad-Hai as the only and eternal Grand Druid. As each might individually commune with him, and none can replace him, they do not follow the formal hierarchy of the Old Faith druids, though age and experience are still respected, with younger druids commonly deferring to their elders.

Obad-Hai's druids are especially common in remote and sparsely populated regions, including barbarian lands and other places where wilderness remains largely undisturbed. They serve as guardians of natural places, interpreters of the land, and teachers of those who must survive beyond the reach of civilization.

Unlike Ehlonna's priests, Obad-Hai's druids are not primarily protectors of particular peoples. Their first loyalty is to the natural world itself.

#### Duties and Obligations

A druid of Obad-Hai is expected to:

- Preserve the natural balance of the wilderness.
    
- Protect wild places from needless or excessive exploitation.
    
- Teach those who live in remote regions how to survive without destroying their surroundings.
    
- Prevent unnatural disruptions to the natural order.
    
- Respect both predators and prey, recognising that neither is inherently good or evil.
    
- Take only what is needed and avoid waste.
    
- Maintain shrines and sacred wilderness sites.
    
- Conduct rites involving living plants, earth, water, and fire.
    
- Preserve knowledge of the natural world and pass it to younger druids.
    
- Avoid allowing personal morality to override the natural order.
    

### Druid of Obad-Hai

Obad-Hai's priesthood uses the normal rules, abilities, restrictions, and advancement of the Druid class, except as follows:

**Ability Requirements:** Wisdom 14

**Weapons:** As druids but must take staff at 1st level

**Major Spheres:** Animal, Elemental (all), Healing, Plant, Sun, Weather

**Minor Spheres:** Charm, Divination, Necromantic, Summoning

**Turn Undead:** None

**Granted Powers:** Obad-Hai's priests do not gain the followers or authority that druids normally gain from levels 12 through 15. They continue to gain new spells as normal after 15th level. At 16th level, they gain only the druid's immunity to natural poisons and vigorous health, and do not gain the class abilities that druids normally gain from levels 17 through 20.

## Oerth

Oeridian alias of Beory

## Olidammara

**Titles:** The Laughing Rogue

**Alignment:** CN

**Pantheon:** Oeridian

**Portfolio:** Music, Revelry, Wine, Roguery, Humour

**Symbol:** A laughing mask

Olidammara is the patron of many thieves and bards, a wandering minstrel and eternal prankster who delights in wine, women, song, and mischief. He is commonly depicted as a slim man with chestnut hair, a rakish beard, olive-tinted skin, sparkling emerald eyes, and green and gold clothing. A master of disguise, he delights in upsetting expectations and mocking those who take themselves too seriously.

His cult is widespread throughout the Flanaess, though his temples and formal priesthood are comparatively uncommon. Even those who do not worship the Laughing Rogue may welcome his clergy in troubled times, when laughter and diversion offer relief from despair. His services are light-hearted affairs filled with music, singing, chanting, feasting, and generous quantities of beer, ale, and wine.

#### Dogma

Life is too serious to be endured without laughter. Enjoy music, wine, good company, and the pleasures of the moment. Question those who demand unquestioning obedience, mock pomposity, and remind the powerful that dignity is often a mask for vanity. A clever jest can humble the mighty, while a well-told tale can give hope to the despairing.

Freedom and pleasure are worth defending, but neither should become an excuse for joyless cruelty. The finest trick is one that leaves its target wiser, the finest song one that brings strangers together, and the finest celebration one that people remember with delight.

### The Priesthood

Olidammara's priesthood consists of urban priests, commonly found in large cities, and wandering minstrel-priests who travel from settlement to settlement. They are generally welcomed by common folk seeking entertainment and relief from hardship, but their irreverence and fondness for unsettling established customs make them unpopular with many lawfully aligned clergy.

Olidammara's priests are expected to sing or play a musical instrument. They lead celebrations, perform in taverns and public squares, share stories, and use humour to challenge arrogance and expose hypocrisy. Some cultivate friendships among thieves, entertainers, and other people who live outside respectable society, while others make a point of bringing music and laughter to places afflicted by misery.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Olidammara is expected to:

- Sing or play a musical instrument, developing the ability to entertain and inspire others.
    
- Bring laughter, music, and diversion to those burdened by hardship or despair.
    
- Enjoy wine, feasting, song, and good company in the spirit of the Laughing Rogue.
    
- Challenge pomposity, hypocrisy, and excessive solemnity through humour and irreverence.
    
- Encourage freedom of expression and resist attempts to suppress harmless pleasures.
    
- Travel, perform, and share stories with people from different walks of life.
    
- Use disguise, cleverness, and misdirection to confound those who deserve to be humbled.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Adventuring priests of Olidammara often travel as minstrels, entertainers, storytellers, or vagabonds. They may accompany adventurers for the promise of new experiences, good company, and opportunities to test their wit against danger. Some use their talents to infiltrate places where conventional priests would be unwelcome; others simply believe that every expedition benefits from a song, a joke, and a bottle of wine.

### Priest of Olidammara

**Ability Requirements:** Dexterity 13 or Charisma 13

**Prime Requisites:** Wisdom

**Alignment:** Chaotic Neutral

**Weapons:** As thieves

**Armour:** As permitted by the reboot's general priest rules

**Major Spheres:** Charm, Creation, Healing, Protection

**Minor Spheres:** Chaos, Divination, Travellers

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Hide in Shadows:** The priest can hide in shadows as a thief, with a base chance of 5% per level.

**5th Level — Alter Self:** The priest can cast _alter self_ as a 2nd-level wizard spell.

**7th Level — Tasha's Hideous Uncontrollable Laughter:** The priest can cast _Tasha's hideous uncontrollable laughter_ as a 2nd-level wizard spell.

**10th Level — Confusion:** The priest can cast _confusion_.

**Turn Undead:** Nil.

## Osprem

**Titles:** Lady of the Sea, Guardian of Voyages, Mother of Sailors

**Alignment:** Lawful Neutral

**Pantheon:** Suel

**Portfolio:** Sea voyages, ships, sailors

**Symbol:** A whale, dolphin, or barracuda

More compassionate than her sometime-lover Xerbo, Osprem is the guardian of those who travel upon or through the sea. Sailors invoke her protection against storms, sea monsters, and the many other dangers of the open water. She is not, however, endlessly forgiving. Ships that offend her may find themselves pursued by storms or monsters, while coastal communities that violate her laws may suffer terrible weather, including destructive ice storms.

Osprem may appear as a whale, giant barracuda, or dolphin, or as a beautiful woman wearing a flowing gown resembling seaweed.

#### Dogma

The sea demands respect. Those who travel upon it must prepare themselves, maintain their vessels, respect their companions, and obey the laws that preserve the safety of all who depend upon the water. Courage is valuable, but recklessness that endangers others is not.

Osprem teaches that the sea connects distant peoples and communities. Ships and sailors therefore carry responsibilities beyond themselves: a vessel must be maintained, its crew must be protected, and those who depend upon the sea must not be abandoned through carelessness or selfishness.

### The Priesthood

Osprem's priests serve as guardians, advisers, and spiritual leaders for maritime communities. They bless ships before voyages, shepherd naval vessels through dangerous waters, and advise captains concerning the dangers of the sea. Many serve communities whose livelihoods depend upon fishing, shipping, naval activity, or coastal trade.

Osprem's priests generally maintain good relations with the priests of Xerbo, despite the differing temperaments and priorities of the two faiths. They are otherwise largely indifferent to the priests of other sea powers.

Her clergy are not confined to temples. Priests frequently travel aboard ships, establish shrines in ports, and serve as advisers to coastal rulers and naval commanders. Some deliberately seek positions of political authority, believing that control over maritime policy is an important means of protecting sailors and coastal communities.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Osprem is expected to:

- Protect sailors and others who depend upon the sea.
    
- Bless and properly prepare vessels before dangerous voyages.
    
- Encourage careful seamanship and responsible conduct aboard ship.
    
- Respect the sea and its dangers rather than treating it as an obstacle to be conquered.
    
- Aid communities whose survival depends upon the sea.
    
- Enforce Osprem's laws when dealing with those under the priest's authority.
    
- Punish serious offenses against Osprem when appropriate.
    
- Preserve ships, crews, and cargo whenever doing so does not violate Osprem's laws.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Osprem's priests commonly adventure aboard ships, accompanying expeditions into dangerous waters or travelling between coastal communities. Others venture inland to recover lost vessels, rescue sailors, investigate threats to maritime trade, or establish new shrines and ports. A priest of Osprem may also serve as a navigator, ship's chaplain, naval adviser, or captain.

### Priest of Osprem

**Ability Requirements:** CON 14

**Alignment:** Lawful Neutral

**Weapons:** Trident (required), plus any weapon commonly used by sailors

**Armor:** Any

**Major Spheres:** All, Animal (sea creatures only), Divination, Elemental (Water), Healing, Protection

**Minor Spheres:** Charm, Necromantic, Travelers

**Prohibited Spheres:** None

**Special Spell:** _Bless Vessel_

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Sea Friend:** The priest may cast _animal friendship_ once per day, but the spell may affect only aquatic animals or creatures normally found in or around the sea.

**3rd Level — Protection of the Sea:** While on or in the water, the priest gains a +2 bonus to Armor Class, equivalent to the protection granted by a _ring of protection +2_. This protection does not function while the priest is completely away from the water.

**5th Level — Form of the Sea:** The priest may cast _polymorph self_ once per day, but may assume only the form of a dolphin or giant barracuda.

**7th Level — Breath of the Sea:** The priest can breathe water at will. This ability affects the priest only.

**10th Level — Walk upon Water:** The priest can walk upon the surface of water at will, as though it were solid ground. This ability affects the priest only.

**13th Level — Form of the Great Whale:** Once per week, the priest may shapechange into a sperm whale of 16 Hit Dice. The transformation functions according to the normal rules for the _shapechange_ spell, except that only the specified form may be assumed.

**15th Level — Command the Waters:** Once per week, the priest may either summon a water elemental, using the normal rules for _summon earth elemental_, or dismiss a water elemental. A water elemental targeted by the dismissal receives no saving throw.

**Turn Undead:** Osprem's priests cannot turn or command undead.

**Bless Vessel**

_Bless vessel_ is a 4th-level priest spell in the Protection sphere. It may be cast upon a seagoing vessel before or during a voyage. For the duration of that voyage, from one port to another, the vessel is half as likely to suffer a maritime calamity. Such calamities include capsizing, men going overboard, broken rigging, torn sails, and similar accidents.

The reverse of the spell, _curse vessel_, doubles the normal likelihood of such a calamity occurring. The reversed spell is normally used against vessels that have offended Osprem or deliberately violated her laws.

**Holy Vestments:** Osprem's priests traditionally wear blue robes or brass armour decorated with symbols of ships and sea creatures, particularly dolphins, barracuda, and whales.

## Pelor

**Titles:** The Sun Father, the Shining One, the Great Sun God

**Alignment:** NG

**Pantheon:** Flan

**Portfolio:** Sun, Light, Strength, Healing, Vitality

**Symbol:** A golden sun

Pelor is the great Sun God, regarded as the creator and sustainer of much that is good. He represents strength, light, warmth, healing, and the vitality of life. Pelor is said to fly upon a great ki-rin, summon flights of eagles, and cast down the forces of darkness with bolts of brilliant sunlight.

The character of Pelor's worship has changed during and since the wars. His older aspect as a peaceful and gentle god concerned chiefly with suffering remains strong among his priests, but among the wider population it has increasingly been overshadowed by his martial aspect as a wrathful Power who drives evil from the world. This change parallels the rise of Mayaheine, Pelor's martial servitor.

Pelor's priests continue to strengthen and heal those beset by evil, and warriors increasingly turn to his faith for courage, protection, and aid. His worship therefore encompasses both the quiet work of tending the suffering and the active struggle against darkness.

Services involve hymn singing, communal prayer, and the dispensing of alms to the needy. The well-to-do are expected to contribute generously to these works.

#### Dogma

Light exists to drive back darkness, and strength exists to protect those who cannot protect themselves. Pelor's servants are expected to preserve life, relieve suffering, oppose cruelty, and give aid without regard for wealth or status.

Mercy is not weakness, but neither is mercy an excuse for allowing evil to prey upon the helpless. When evil threatens the innocent, Pelor's faithful are expected to stand against it. The gentle hand that heals the wounded may also wield the weapon that protects them.

### The Priesthood

Pelor's priests have long worked among the poor and sick, providing healing, food, shelter, and practical aid. These responsibilities remain central to the priesthood even as Pelor's public image has become more martial.

Most Pelorian priests are quiet, kindly, and compassionate. They are not pacifists, however, and the faith increasingly expects them to possess the courage and resolve necessary to defend those under their care. A Pelorian temple commonly serves simultaneously as a place of worship, a source of healing, and a refuge for the needy.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Pelor is expected to:

- Aid the sick, injured, poor, and otherwise helpless.
    
- Provide healing and practical assistance where the temple has the means to do so.
    
- Collect alms from those able to give and distribute them to those in need.
    
- Conduct communal prayers, hymns, and other observances of the faith.
    
- Protect those entrusted to the priest's care from violence, oppression, and supernatural evil.
    
- Oppose undead, fiends, and other forces that corrupt or destroy life.
    
- Show compassion to those who genuinely seek aid, while refusing to allow mercy to endanger the innocent.
    
- Maintain the temple as a place of light, healing, and refuge.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Pelor's priests adventure to bring aid where it is needed, oppose supernatural evil, recover holy objects, and protect communities threatened by darkness. Some accompany bands of warriors specifically to provide healing and protection. Others leave their temples to seek out sources of evil before they can threaten the innocent.

### Priest of Pelor

**Ability Requirements:** Standard

**Alignment:** Any good

**Weapons:** Flail, mace, morning star, staff, staff-sling

**Armour:** Leather or chain

**Major Spheres:** Charm, Creation, Elemental (air), Guardian, Healing, Necromantic, Protection, Sun

**Minor Spheres:** Summoning, Wards

**Magical Items:** None

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Healing Touch:** All healing spells cast by a Pelorian priest restore at least the median amount of hit points possible. Thus, _cure light wounds_ restores at least 5 hit points, _cure serious wounds_ at least 9 hit points, and _cure critical wounds_ at least 16 hit points. _Heal_ cures all but 1–2 hit points of damage, as normal for the spell.

**5th Level — Eyes of the Sun:** The priest automatically succeeds on saving throws against spells and magical effects that deprive the priest of sight, including _darkness_ and _blindness_.

**9th Level — Flight:** At 9th level, the priest gains the ability to cast _fly_ once per day, using the wizard spell's normal effects.

**Turn Undead:** Turn at +1 level.

## Phaulkon

**Titles:** The Archer, Lord of the Open Sky, Master of the Winds

**Alignment:** Chaotic Good

**Portfolio:** Air, wind, clouds, birds, archery

**Symbol:** A winged arrow

Phaulkon is an active deity who sends omens to the peoples of the Flanaess. He concerns himself with all things that occur beneath the open sky, from the flight of birds to the movement of clouds and the changing of the winds. He possesses considerable knowledge of magical artifacts and relics, including methods by which their powers may temporarily be suppressed.

Phaulkon can see through the eyes of birds and maintains close ties with Aerdrie Faenya, the elven goddess of air and weather. He is second only to Kord among the Suel gods in fighting ability and is particularly favored by military leaders who rely upon archers and other ranged troops. He and Jascar are friendly allies, often working together in the advancement of good.

Phaulkon appears as a clean-shaven Suel man, usually bare-chested, with wind-tousled hair. He is occasionally depicted with wings.

#### Dogma

The open sky belongs to all who live beneath it. Observe the winds, clouds, and birds, for they carry knowledge to those willing to watch. Freedom, mobility, and the ability to act from a distance are valuable advantages, but they carry responsibilities.

The bow is both a hunting tool and a weapon of war. It should be taught to those who need it, and used with discipline rather than cruelty. Phaulkon's followers are also expected to protect flying creatures and preserve the freedom of the skies.

Dangerous magic must not be ignored. When a powerful artifact threatens the innocent, knowledge of its nature and means of suppressing it can be as important as the strength to destroy it.

### The Priesthood

Phaulkon's priests are archers, hunters, scouts, weather-watchers, military instructors, and guardians of the open sky. They study clouds and winds for omens, observe the movements of birds, and protect flying animals much as druids protect other forms of wildlife.

The clergy teach archery and bowmaking to common people so that they may hunt for food and defend themselves. They also instruct soldiers in the effective use of ranged weapons. When rumors arise of a dangerous artifact or relic, Phaulkon's priests may investigate, particularly when the object threatens the innocent or cannot safely be destroyed.

All priests of Phaulkon receive the Bowyer/Fletcher nonweapon proficiency without spending a proficiency slot.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Phaulkon is expected to:

- Observe the sky, winds, clouds, and birds for signs and omens.
    
- Protect birds and other flying creatures from needless harm.
    
- Teach archery and bowmaking to those who can benefit from them.
    
- Help communities acquire food through hunting when necessary.
    
- Teach soldiers the responsible and effective use of ranged weapons.
    
- Maintain and improve bows, arrows, and other ranged equipment.
    
- Investigate credible reports of dangerous magical artifacts.
    
- Seek means of suppressing or neutralizing dangerous magic when destruction is impossible or unwise.
    
- Preserve freedom of movement and oppose needless restrictions upon the open sky.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Phaulkon's priests adventure as scouts, hunters, military advisers, explorers, and investigators of magical relics. They are particularly likely to become involved when a journey crosses dangerous wilderness, when flying creatures are threatened, or when an artifact of uncertain power has been discovered. Many travel with military expeditions or adventuring companies as archers and scouts.

### Priest of Phaulkon

**Ability Requirements:** STR 10, DEX 13

**Alignment:** Chaotic Good

**Weapons:** Dagger (required), longbow (required), short bow, short sword, long sword

**Armor:** Any up to chain armour

**Major Spheres:** All, Animal (flying creatures only), Divination, Elemental (Air), Healing, Summoning, Travelers, Weather

**Minor Spheres:** Combat, Protection

**Prohibited Spheres:** None

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Featherfall:** The priest may cast _feather fall_ once per day.

**3rd Level — Wind-Borne Arrow:** The priest may cast _Melf's acid arrow_ once per day. The priest may choose to describe the spell's manifestation as an arrow or bolt of magical wind, but its game effects remain unchanged.

**5th Level — Voice of the Birds:** The priest can communicate with normal birds at will. This functions as _speak with animals_, but only normal birds may be addressed.

**7th Level — Take to the Sky:** The priest may cast _fly_ once per day.

**10th Level — Master of the Winds:** The priest may cast _control winds_ once per day.

**13th Level — Summon the Wind:** Once per week, the priest may conjure an air elemental, using the normal rules for _conjure earth elemental_.

**Turn Undead:** Phaulkon's priests cannot turn or command undead.

**Holy Vestments:** Phaulkon's priests wear blue clothing in ordinary religious service. During formal ceremonies they wear white clothing and feather headdresses.

## Pholtus

**Titles:** The Blinding Light, the One True Path, the Silvery Sun

**Alignment:** Lawful Good; lawful neutral in the Theocracy of the Pale

**Pantheon:** Oeridian

**Portfolio:** Light, Law, Order, Resolution, Righteousness

**Symbol:** The Silvery Sun, an electrum disc atop an ivory staff

Pholtus is the power of unwavering resolve, absolute certainty, and the One True Path. He is the stern guardian of unbending law and a defender of rightful order. His teachings promise certainty to those who follow them, but permit no deviation from the path he reveals. He is depicted as a tall, slender man with pale skin, flowing white hair, and bright blue eyes that shine with the inner fire of devotion. He wears a white silk robe and carries an ivory staff shod with silver and topped by the Silvery Sun.

Pholtus's worshippers are often rigid and uncompromising, finding comfort in a faith that offers clear answers and unquestionable principles. His following is particularly strong in the Theocracy of the Pale, where the faith emphasises his lawful-neutral aspect. This interpretation sometimes conflicts with that of worshippers who regard Pholtus primarily as a champion of good. Many paladins and some warriors revere him, while his priesthood seeks to bring all peoples under the authority of the One True Path.

#### Dogma

There is one right path, one proper order, and one truth by which all things should be judged. Doubt invites error, deviation breeds disorder, and compromise with falsehood endangers the righteous. The laws of Pholtus must be upheld without wavering, and those who have strayed must be shown the error of their ways.

The faithful are expected to live with discipline, observe stringent moral standards, and bring the light of truth to those who remain in darkness. Good intentions do not excuse disobedience, nor does personal conviction make a false path correct. The faithful must be prepared to endure hardship rather than abandon their principles. How the One True Path is interpreted varies among Pholtus's followers, however, particularly over whether law serves righteousness or is itself the highest expression of righteousness.

### The Priesthood

Pholtus's priesthood is strongly organised and hierarchical, with a particular presence in urban communities. Its members actively seek to reveal the light of Pholtus to unbelievers, often with little patience for argument or dissent. Their certainty and insistence on conformity make them unpopular with many other priesthoods, especially those of St. Cuthbert and deities of chaos.

Priests are expected to maintain strict standards of morality and personal conduct. The hierarchy distinguishes three ranks by experience: **Glimmering Priests** of 1st–4th level, **Gleaming Priests** of 5th–8th level, and **Shining Priests** of 9th level and above. Their vestments reflect these ranks: Glimmering Priests wear white robes, Gleaming Priests add silver, and Shining Priests are adorned with gold.

Services take place in consecrated buildings decorated in white. They feature candlelight, lengthy sermons, and the singing of the faith's anthem, _O Blinding Light_. Temples serve as centres of worship, instruction, and the propagation of Pholtus's laws.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Pholtus is expected to:

- Uphold the laws and teachings of Pholtus without wavering.
    
- Maintain strict personal standards of morality, discipline, and righteousness.
    
- Teach the One True Path and seek to bring unbelievers into the faith.
    
- Oppose practices and doctrines regarded as contrary to Pholtus's teachings.
    
- Preserve the authority and proper functioning of the priesthood's hierarchy.
    
- Conduct services, deliver sermons, and lead the faithful in prayer and the anthem _O Blinding Light_.
    
- Promote lawful order and oppose disorder, corruption, and wilful defiance of legitimate authority.
    
- Maintain the sanctity and proper observances of Pholtus's consecrated buildings.
    
- Support the faithful who seek guidance in matters of law, duty, and moral conduct.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Adventuring priests of Pholtus travel to spread the One True Path, confront perceived heresy, and restore lawful order where they believe it has been undermined. Some accompany paladins and lawful rulers, while others investigate corruption, suppress cults, or seek to convert communities beyond the reach of established temples. Their conviction can make them steadfast allies in the face of evil and disorder, but their unwillingness to tolerate dissent may bring them into conflict with companions whose methods or beliefs differ from their own.

## Priest of Pholtus

**Ability Requirements:** Wisdom 14 or Charisma 13

**Prime Requisites:** Wisdom, Charisma

**Alignment:** Lawful good; lawful neutral only in the Theocracy of the Pale

**Weapons:** Flail, mace, morningstar, scourge, staff, staff-sling, sling, warhammer

**Armour:** Any

**Major Spheres:** Combat, Divination, Guardian, Healing, Law, Necromantic, Protection, Sun, War

**Minor Spheres:** Charm*, Summoning*, Wards*

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Dispel Darkness:** The priest can dispel magical darkness, as the granted power of that name.

**5th Level — Glow:** The priest can produce the effect known as _glow_, as described in the relevant spell or power entry.

**9th Level — Reflect:** The priest gains the ability to use _reflect_, as described in the relevant spell or power entry.

**12th Level — Holy Word:** The priest can cast _holy word_ once per day.

**Turn Undead:** Turns undead.

## Phyton

**Titles:** Lord of the Wild, Keeper of the Green, The Earth-Father

**Alignment:** Chaotic Good

**Portfolio:** Nature, natural beauty, farming

**Symbol:** A green leaf upon brown earth

Phyton is the protector of unspoiled wilderness, admirer of natural beauty, and cultivator of the rich earth. He rewards those who work the land without exhausting it and those who recognize and preserve the beauty found in the natural world. He is especially concerned with the balance between cultivation and preservation: fields may be planted, forests may be harvested, and animals may be hunted, but none should be treated as inexhaustible.

Phyton is particularly beloved in rural communities, where farmers pray to him during lean years and thank him for abundant harvests. He appears as a youthful Suel man and may assume the form of any creature normally found in a forest.

#### Dogma

The natural world is both provider and masterpiece. Take what is needed, but do not destroy what cannot be replaced. Good stewardship allows the earth to provide for generations rather than merely satisfying the needs of the present.

Phyton teaches that wilderness and agriculture need not be enemies. A well-tended field, healthy forest, fertile pasture, and thriving population of animals can all form part of a living landscape. Those who work the land must therefore learn its limits and respect its needs.

### The Priesthood

Phyton's priests are stewards of the land, farmers, foresters, healers, and protectors of wilderness. Each priest chooses a region to watch over, normally an area of roughly 300 square miles or a circle approximately one day's walk in diameter. Within this region, the priest is responsible for maintaining the balance between settlement, cultivation, wildlife, and wilderness.

Phyton's priests work closely with druids, and some druids worship Phyton directly. They use their magic to aid people and animals, improve crops, restore damaged land, and prevent famine. They do not oppose cultivation simply because it alters the wilderness; rather, they oppose wasteful or destructive use of the land.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Phyton is expected to:

- Protect unspoiled wilderness and important natural habitats.
    
- Help farmers cultivate the land without exhausting it.
    
- Aid people and animals during famine, drought, and other periods of scarcity.
    
- Preserve forests, waterways, and fertile soil from needless destruction.
    
- Protect wildlife from needless slaughter.
    
- Encourage sustainable hunting, farming, forestry, and grazing.
    
- Recognize and preserve places of exceptional natural beauty.
    
- Watch over and defend the priest's chosen region.
    
- Teach others to understand the needs and limits of the land.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Phyton's priests adventure when threats extend beyond their chosen region or when their skills are needed elsewhere. They may seek sources of famine, investigate blighted lands, protect threatened wilderness, recover lost agricultural knowledge, or aid communities suffering from natural disaster. Some travel specifically to study unfamiliar ecosystems before returning to their own lands.

### Priest of Phyton

**Ability Requirements:** INT 11, WIS 12

**Alignment:** Chaotic Good

**Weapons:** Any

**Armor:** Any

**Major Spheres:** All, Elemental, Healing, Plant, Sun, Weather

**Minor Spheres:** Animal, Charm, Divination

**Prohibited Spheres:** None

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Green Sight:** The priest may cast _locate animals or plants_ once per day. The priest may also cast _shocking grasp_ once per day.

**3rd Level — Bounty of the Earth:** The priest may cast _goodberry_ once per day and gains a +2 bonus to all saving throws against poisons derived from plants.

**5th Level — Plant Growth:** The priest may cast _plant growth_ once per day.

**7th Level — Shaping Wood:** The priest may cast _warp wood_ three times per day and may use _animal summoning_ once per day.

**10th Level — Become the Tree:** The priest may cast _tree_ three times per day and may cast _commune with nature_ once per day.

**13th Level — Door Through the Wood:** The priest may cast _plant door_ three times per day and may cast _liveoak_ once per day.

**Keeper of the Land:** Upon attaining 1st level, the priest chooses a region to watch over, normally approximately 300 square miles or a circle one day's walk in diameter. While within that region, or while in a forest, the priest gains a +2 bonus to all saving throws.

**Turn Undead:** Phyton's priests cannot turn or command undead.

**Holy Vestments:** Phyton's priests wear brown or green robes. Lighter shades are traditionally worn during autumn, reflecting the changing colours of the natural world.

## Procan

**Titles:** The Storm Lord, Lord of the Seas

**Alignment:** Chaotic Neutral

**Pantheon:** Oeridian

**Portfolio:** Seas, Oceans, Weather, Navigation

**Symbol:** A great spear hung with fronds of seaweed

Procan is the power of the seas and oceans, their weather, and the navigation of their waters. He is depicted as a large, muscular man with blue-green skin and hair and golden eyes, armed with a great spear from which hang fronds of seaweed. Stormy and impulsive, Procan is prone to fits of temper. He is also greedy for treasure, hoarding what sinks to the sea bed and jealously guarding the pearls and wonders of his domain.

Fisherfolk and sailors seek to placate Procan, and small shrines to him can be found in ports of almost any size. His worshippers are relatively few and are concentrated among those who spend most of their lives at sea, though some sentient aquatic peoples also honour him.

#### Dogma

The sea provides wealth and sustenance, but it is never a possession to be taken for granted. Its bounty must be respected, its dangers acknowledged, and its lord treated with due caution. Procan is neither a kindly protector nor a consistent enemy: the waters may be calm one day and murderous the next, and those who sail them must be prepared for either. Offerings and prayers may win his favour, but no sailor should presume that such gestures guarantee a safe passage.

### The Priesthood

Procan’s priests are practical, unpretentious, and attentive to the lives of those who earn their living on the sea. They eschew elaborate ceremonies and dress simply, preferring to minister to sailors and fisherfolk in the course of their daily work. Their services take place on or beside the water and commonly include offerings of gold or pearls cast into the sea, a meal of fish and bread, and prayers for safe passage. Having a priest of Procan aboard is considered good fortune by many seafarers.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Procan is expected to:

- Attend to the spiritual needs of sailors, fisherfolk, and others whose livelihoods depend upon the sea.
    
- Offer prayers for safe voyages and conduct appropriate observances before or during sea travel.
    
- Make offerings to Procan, particularly when seeking his favour or giving thanks for a successful voyage.
    
- Respect the sea's dangers and discourage reckless confidence in the face of storms or other hazards.
    
- Preserve the customary practices of seafarers and maintain shrines in ports and coastal communities.
    
- Treat the sea's bounty with respect, recognising that its riches are not theirs to claim without regard for Procan.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Priests of Procan may accompany ships as chaplains, navigators, or trusted members of the crew. Others travel to remote coasts, explore uncharted waters, seek lost treasures, or investigate disturbances in the sea. Their calling is particularly suited to those who live and work aboard vessels, though Procan's unpredictable nature means that his priests are not always content to remain in familiar waters.

### Priest of Procan

**Ability Requirements:** Constitution 14

**Prime Requisites:** Constitution

**Alignment:** Chaotic neutral

**Weapons:** Flail, mace, morning star, net, staff, staff-sling, spear (1st level), trident

**Armour:** Leather

**Major Spheres:** Divination, Elemental (Air, Water), Healing, Protection, Summoning (aquatic creatures only), Weather

**Minor Spheres:** Animal, Chaos, Charm, Combat, Plant

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Swimming:** The priest gains the Swimming proficiency. If the reboot's proficiency rules do not permit a granted proficiency to be acquired in this manner, the character instead gains its benefits without spending a proficiency slot.

**3rd Level — Water Walk:** The priest can walk upon the surface of water as though it were solid ground. The effect otherwise follows the appropriate spell description, if available in the reboot.

**6th Level — Water Breathing:** The priest can breathe water as though under the effect of the wizard spell _water breathing_. The source identifies this as a 4th-level wizard spell; it does not specify a frequency for this power.

**8th Level — Airy Water:** The priest can create an area of water in which air-breathing creatures can breathe normally, as though under the effect of the wizard spell _airy water_. The source identifies this as a 5th-level wizard spell; it does not specify a frequency for this power.

**Turn Undead:** Cannot turn undead.

## Pyremius

**Blazing Killer, Demon of Venom, Hideous Assassin**

**Alignment:** Neutral Evil

**Portfolio:** Fire, poison, murder

**Symbol:** A fiendish head with winglike ears

Pyremius is the Suel god of fire, poison, and murder. He is depicted as a grotesque man with a head resembling that of a jermlaine, wearing great brass bracers and bearing a sword and whip. According to Suel tradition, Pyremius was once a lesser god of poison and murder. During the height of the Suel Empire, he poisoned Ranet, the goddess of fire, and seized her weakened portfolio. Since then, fire has been associated with Pyremius not as a source of warmth or life, but as an instrument of destruction.

Pyremius is worshipped by assassins, arsonists, poisoners, and those who delight in destructive acts. His faith is also found among certain monstrous peoples, particularly those accustomed to volcanic or subterranean environments. He teaches that fire destroys without remorse, poison defeats those who believe themselves secure, and every enemy possesses a weakness that can be exploited.

Pyremius is distrustful of other gods and rarely forms lasting alliances. He remembers that his own rise to power was accomplished through betrayal and assumes that others would treat him similarly if given the opportunity. His strongest divine association is with Syrul, whose appreciation for deceit and malice makes her one of the few powers he considers worthy of trust.

#### Dogma

The strong survive by recognizing weakness and destroying those who threaten them. Fire consumes what cannot be defended; poison defeats those who believe themselves beyond harm; murder removes obstacles that cannot otherwise be overcome.

Never assume that an enemy is harmless simply because they are stronger, more numerous, or better protected. Study those who oppose you and discover where they are vulnerable. A guarded enemy still sleeps, eats, drinks, and trusts others. Use every weakness available.

Those who allow themselves to be defeated through carelessness, misplaced trust, or failure to prepare have earned their fate. Mercy toward a dangerous enemy is weakness; mercy toward an enemy who might later become useful is merely another form of calculation.

### The Priesthood

Priests of Pyremius, known as **deathseekers**, cultivate secrecy, observation, poisoncraft, and the controlled use of fire. They study people not to understand them for their own sake, but to identify their weaknesses and determine how those weaknesses might be exploited.

Temples are built of mortared stone, often using volcanic rock, and are designed with numerous alcoves, blind corners, and concealed passages. Fires burn continuously in the principal chambers. Red and orange patterns are commonly enameled upon the walls, while brass is favored for religious implements and decoration.

The priesthood is deliberately suspicious of its own members. Advancement is based upon demonstrated usefulness and survival, and priests rarely advertise their rank unnecessarily. Assassins are welcomed among the faithful but seldom rise to the highest ranks of the priesthood, where mastery of Pyremius's fire magic is considered essential.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Pyremius is expected to:

- cultivate knowledge of fire, poisons, and dangerous substances;
    
- study the weaknesses of potential enemies;
    
- maintain secrecy concerning the affairs of the faith;
    
- never place complete trust in another when precautions are possible;
    
- destroy threats before they become greater dangers;
    
- exploit opportunities rather than allowing them to pass unused;
    
- maintain useful knowledge of poisons, combustion, and other destructive techniques;
    
- punish betrayal when doing so serves the interests of the faith.
    

Pyremius does not require his priests to murder indiscriminately. A needless killing that gains nothing is wasteful. Murder is a tool, and a tool should be used when it accomplishes something that cannot be accomplished more effectively by other means.

#### Adventuring Priests

Pyremius's priests adventure to acquire forbidden knowledge, discover new poisons and combustible materials, eliminate enemies of the faith, recover valuable artifacts, or establish themselves in places where the worship of Pyremius can grow. They are particularly interested in dangerous ruins, volcanic regions, alchemical laboratories, and other places where fire and poisonous substances can be studied.

### Priest of Pyremius

**Ability Requirements:** Constitution 13, Wisdom 9

**Alignment:** Neutral Evil

**Weapons:** Any

**Armor:** Leather or lighter

**Major Spheres:** All, Combat, Creation, Elemental (Fire), Guardian, Summoning

**Minor Spheres:** Divination, Healing, Necromantic, Plant, Protection

**Prohibited:** Spells that use cold

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Fire and Cold Resistance:** The priest gains a +1 bonus to saving throws against fire and suffers a −1 penalty to saving throws against cold.

**3rd Level — Poisoner:** The priest can detect poison by tasting a substance and may cast _poison_ once per week.

**5th Level — Improved Resistance:** The priest's bonus to saving throws against fire increases to +2 and the penalty against cold increases to −2. The priest also gains the ability to backstab as a thief, inflicting ×2 normal backstab damage.

**7th Level — Venomous Faith:** Yugoloths react one category more favorably toward the priest than they normally would. Saving throws against poisons created or delivered by the priest suffer a −1 penalty.

**9th Level — Pyrotechnics:** The priest may cast _pyrotechnics_ once per day. Their backstab multiplier increases to ×3.

**11th Level — Burning Blood:** The priest suffers a −3 penalty to saving throws against cold and takes 1 additional point of damage per die from cold-based attacks, up to the normal maximum damage of the attack. The priest may also cast _fire shield_ using its warm-flame form once per day.

**16th Level — Master of Flame:** The priest suffers a −4 penalty to saving throws against cold. One hit point lost from each cold-based attack is permanently lost until restored by _restoration_ or _wish_. The priest may cast a 10d6 _fireball_ once per day.

## Quetzalcoatl

**Titles:** The Feathered Serpent

**Alignment:** LN

**Pantheon:** Olman

**Portfolio:** Air, Birds, Snakes, Leadership, Arts, Metallurgy

**Symbol:** A feathered serpent

Quetzalcoatl is the enigmatic greater god who has spent more time leading the Olman gods than any other power. He most frequently manifests as a couatl, but may appear in any number of bizarre and monstrous forms. He is a patron of the arts and metallurgy and is the least warlike of the major Olman powers. His authority is expressed through leadership, organization, creation, and the ordering of society rather than through battle.

#### Dogma

Quetzalcoatl teaches that civilization requires capable leadership and that knowledge, craft, and administration are as important to a people as warriors. Art and metalworking are not merely trades but means by which mortals impose skill and order upon the world.

Those entrusted with authority are expected to exercise it. Quetzalcoatl's priests therefore regard obedience to legitimate authority as a natural part of an ordered society, while expecting themselves to provide competent leadership rather than merely claim power.

### The Priesthood

Quetzalcoatl's priests are leaders, administrators, artisans, and political authorities. They promote the god through political means, organizing communities and institutions in his name. A senior priest expects orders to be obeyed by non-priests and by younger priests of other gods, and the priesthood readily assumes administrative authority when circumstances permit.

Many priests are accomplished sculptors, metalworkers, or both. Temples commonly contain elaborate carvings and finely fabricated metalwork produced by the priesthood itself. Quetzalcoatl's priests may cast any priest spell that creates or improves metal items, regardless of the sphere to which the spell belongs.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Quetzalcoatl is expected to:

- Exercise authority competently and maintain order within the community.
    
- Promote Quetzalcoatl through political and administrative influence.
    
- Obey legitimate orders from senior priests and enforce the authority of the priesthood.
    
- Develop and preserve the arts, particularly sculpture and metalworking.
    
- Produce or commission elaborate works of art and craftsmanship.
    
- Teach useful crafts and administrative knowledge to those capable of learning them.
    
- Maintain temples, workshops, records, and other institutions entrusted to the priesthood.
    
- Use diplomacy, organization, and political influence in preference to unnecessary warfare.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Quetzalcoatl's priests adventure to establish or strengthen the god's influence, recover lost works of art and metalworking, investigate ancient sites, and acquire knowledge useful to their communities. They may also act as emissaries, administrators, or political agents, seeking to bring distant settlements under orderly government and the influence of the priesthood.

### Priest of Quetzalcoatl

**Ability Requirements:** Intelligence 13, Charisma 14

**Alignment:** Any Lawful

**Weapons:** Dagger, javelin, short bow, staff

**Armour:** Any

**Major Spheres:** All, Animal, Astral, Charm, Divination, Elemental (Air), Guardian, Healing, Law, Protection, Thought

**Minor Spheres:** Necromantic, Sun, Weather

**Prohibited Spheres:**

**Magical Items:** A priest of Quetzalcoatl may use any priest spell that creates or improves metal items, regardless of its sphere.

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Feathered Authority:** The priest may cast _invisibility to animals_ or _detect poison_ once per day. In addition, the priest gains a +2 bonus to proficiency checks involving the arts or metalworking.

**3rd Level — Serpent's Guile:** The priest may cast _snake charm_ or _invisibility_ once per day.

**5th Level — Transformation of the Serpent:** The priest may cast _fly_ or _wraithform_ once per day.

**7th Level — Ordering the Elements:** The priest may cast _snakes to sticks_ (the reverse of _sticks to snakes_) or _control temperature, 10' radius_ once per day.

**10th Level — Mastery of Air and Beast:** The priest may cast _control winds_ or _animal summoning II_, calling only birds or snakes, once per day.

**13th Level — Form of the Couatl:** The priest may polymorph into a couatl. While in couatl form, the priest may cast their own spells normally.

**15th Level — Wind Walk:** The priest may cast _wind walk_ once per day.

**Turn Undead:** Nil

## Ralishaz

**Titles:** The Unlooked-for, The Lord of Chance

**Alignment:** CN

**Portfolio:** Chance, Misfortune, Risk, Gambling

**Symbol:** A six-sided die showing no consistent number

Ralishaz is the Power of chance, ill-luck, and unexpected misfortune. He is also patron of gamblers and those willing to take unusual risks. He seldom rewards those who risk everything, but when he does, the reward may be extraordinary. His favour is as unpredictable as his displeasure, and neither good fortune nor disaster is proof that a mortal has earned either.

Ralishaz is said to appear in whatever form best suits the moment: an idiot or dolt, a hideously wrinkled old man, a scabby beggar, or a beautiful maid. He favours wooden weapons, usually a staff, and is a formidable wielder of curses and magical aging. His gaze can send a victim into enchanted sleep.

#### Dogma

Ralishaz teaches that certainty is an illusion. Fortune turns without warning, and no mortal can know whether the next choice will bring triumph or disaster. Those who understand this do not expect the world to be fair, nor do they assume that prudence will always be rewarded.

Risk is neither inherently wise nor foolish. A great opportunity may demand great danger, while an apparently safe choice may lead to ruin. Gamblers and adventurers therefore invoke Ralishaz before risking themselves, but never assume that doing so guarantees success. Misfortune should be endured without self-pity, while unexpected good fortune should be accepted without assuming it will last.

### The Priesthood

Priests of Ralishaz are said to suffer ordinary misfortunes only rarely, but when misfortune does find them, it is often grave. They alternate between stoicism and periods of wild endeavour, sometimes embracing risks that more cautious people would consider foolish. Augury and other attempts to discern what fortune may bring occupy an important place in their lives.

Ralishaz's priests are not necessarily dishonest, but many are calculating, opportunistic, or mean-minded. They are comfortable with uncertainty and often enjoy exploiting situations in which others cannot predict what will happen next. Their temples and shrines favour unpredictable rites, including semi-random musical performances, babbled paeans, auguries, and sudden changes between light and darkness, heat and cold, noise and silence.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Ralishaz is expected to:

- Accept both good and ill fortune without assuming either is deserved.
    
- Invoke Ralishaz before undertaking unusual or dangerous risks.
    
- Perform auguries and interpret omens, while remembering that no omen guarantees an outcome.
    
- Offer aid or counsel to those struck by sudden misfortune.
    
- Maintain shrines and perform rites intended to placate Ralishaz after serious reversals of fortune.
    
- Never promise another person that Ralishaz will bring success.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Priests of Ralishaz are naturally drawn to gambling, exploration, speculation, and ventures whose outcome cannot be reliably predicted. Some deliberately seek dangerous opportunities, believing that fortune favours those willing to place something at risk. Others adventure because they regard uncertainty itself as an expression of their faith. Regardless of their temperament, few priests of Ralishaz are comfortable with a life in which nothing unexpected can happen.

### Priest of Ralishaz

**Ability Requirements:** None

**Alignment:** Chaotic Neutral or Chaotic Evil

**Weapons:** Wooden weapons only; must take staff at 1st level

**Armour:** Leather

**Major Spheres:** Chaos, Charm, Divination, Guardian, Numbers

**Minor Spheres:** Combat, Healing, Summoning, Thought

**Granted Powers**

**3rd Level — Ralishaz's Favour:** The priest may put an enemy to sleep with a gaze. The target must be within 30 yards and must be able to meet the priest's gaze. A successful saving throw vs. spells negates the effect. A creature affected sleeps for 3 turns.

**5th Level — Fortune's Protection:** Once per encounter, the priest may reduce the damage suffered from a single melee attack to one-half the damage rolled.

**7th Level — Fumble:** The priest may cast _fumble_ as a 4th-level wizard.

**9th Level — Defiance of Fate:** The priest gains a saving throw with a base value of 18 against spells and magical effects that normally allow no saving throw. Apply the priest's normal saving-throw modifiers to this base value.

**Turn Undead:** None

## Rao

**Titles:** The Serene One, the Mediator, the God of Reason

**Alignment:** LG

**Portfolio:** Reason, Intellect, Peace, Wisdom, Diplomacy

**Symbol:** A serene, open hand

Rao is the serene and detached god of reason, intellect, and peace. He does not ordinarily intervene directly in the mortal world, preferring that mortals resolve their conflicts through wisdom and understanding. His influence is nevertheless felt through the powerful magical artefacts associated with him, many of which are especially potent against evil. The most famous is the Crook of Rao, which may have been created with the assistance of Boccob.

Rao is traditionally depicted as a dark-skinned, white-haired old man with bright dark-brown eyes and long, slender hands. He is always smiling and serene.

Rao's faith has never been particularly popular among commonfolk. His followers are more often rulers, diplomats, sages, scholars, philosophers, and others whose duties require patience and reason. Those seeking powerful magic to aid the cause of good may make offerings to Rao and meditate upon his sacred texts. Services involve theological discussion, philosophical debate, and group meditation.

#### Dogma

Reason is the proper guide to action. Anger, fear, pride, and haste obscure judgement, while patience and understanding reveal the course most likely to preserve peace.

Peace is not merely the absence of violence. It is the resolution of conflict through wisdom, justice, and mutual understanding. Violence may sometimes be necessary to defend the innocent or prevent a greater evil, but it should never be the first resort of the wise.

Rao's most important lesson is that thought must precede action. His priests therefore teach: **There is a time to think, and more rarely to act; but in that time, action is wisdom.**

### The Priesthood

Rao's priests are mediators and negotiators, generally wise, quiet, and intensely studious. They are frequently called upon to settle disputes between individuals, communities, rulers, and even rival faiths. The priesthood has historically attracted more men than women, although this is a matter of tradition rather than doctrine.

Rao's church has relatively few followers compared with the larger Flan faiths, but its influence can be disproportionate to its size. Rulers seek its counsel, diplomats rely upon its mediators, and scholars preserve its teachings. The High Patriarch in Greyhawk played an important role in the diplomacy that ended the wars.

Despite their commitment to peace, Rao's priests are not wholly pacifistic. When reason demonstrates that action is necessary to prevent greater suffering or defend the innocent, refusing to act is itself considered a failure of wisdom.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Rao is expected to:

- Seek peaceful resolutions to disputes whenever such resolutions remain possible.
    
- Mediate honestly between opposing parties, without allowing personal loyalty to corrupt judgement.
    
- Study theology, philosophy, history, and other disciplines that improve reason and understanding.
    
- Preserve and teach Rao's sacred texts and traditions.
    
- Counsel rulers and other leaders when their decisions threaten peace or justice.
    
- Resist hasty decisions, emotional manipulation, and deliberate deception.
    
- Defend the innocent when peaceful means have failed.
    
- Promote understanding between communities and faiths.
    
- Use violence only when reason demonstrates that inaction would cause greater harm.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Rao's priests adventure to recover lost knowledge, protect artefacts of great power, mediate disputes beyond the reach of ordinary diplomats, and oppose those whose actions threaten peace. Some accompany adventurers because a dangerous evil cannot be reasoned with and must be stopped. Others seek ancient texts, philosophical works, or magical knowledge that may strengthen the cause of good.

### Priest of Rao

**Ability Requirements:** Wisdom 16, Charisma 13

**Alignment:** Lawful good

**Weapons:** Flail, mace, morning star, staff, staff-sling

**Armour:** None or magical chain

**Major Spheres:** Astral, Charm, Divination, Guardian, Healing, Law, Numbers, Thought

**Minor Spheres:** Necromantic, Protection, Wards

**Magical Items:** Rao's priests may be entrusted with sacred magical artefacts created in service to Rao.

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Friends:** The priest may cast _friends_ once per day, using the wizard spell's normal effects.

**4th Level — Clear Mind:** The priest gains a +2 bonus to all saving throws against illusions and mind-affecting spells.

**7th Level — Calm:** The priest may cast _emotion (calm)_ once per day, using the wizard spell's normal effects.

**9th Level — True Sight:** The priest gains the ability to cast _true seeing_ once per day.

**Turn Undead:** Turn at –4 levels.

## Rudd

**Titles:** Luckbringer, Lady Luck, The Laughing Goddess, Smiley, Queen of Cards

**Alignment:** CN (CG)

**Pantheon:** Oeridian

**Portfolio:** Chance, Good Luck, Skill

**Symbol:** Open hand of five cards

Rudd is a young goddess, born in the Flanaess less than a millennium ago. Said to have originated in Bissel, she wandered the world and discovered her love of games that combine chance with skill. A daughter of Norebo, she shares his love of gambling but favours games in which judgement and practice can influence the outcome. Cards and backgammon are among her favourites; games determined entirely by luck bore her, while cerebral games offer too little uncertainty.

Rudd loves mortals and delights in testing those who consider themselves unbeatable. She tolerates cheating only when it is executed with exceptional skill and the cheater escapes detection—though she invariably catches anyone who tries to cheat her. She rejects the idea of a fixed fate or destiny, putting her at odds with Istus.

#### Dogma

Make your own luck. Skill is as important as good fortune and, in the long run, more readily acquired. Practise until you excel, then continue practising. Seek challenges that test both ability and adaptability, and never mistake a lucky outcome for proof of mastery.

Rudd's faithful condemn cheating in principle, yet some teach that a successful cheat who escapes detection has merely made their own luck. Such lessons are delivered with a smile, but those who are caught may discover that Lady Luck has little sympathy for them.

### The Priesthood

Rudd's clergy are few and loosely organized, consisting of clerics, specialty priests known as Luckdealers, and bards. Most have Oeridian or mixed Oeridian ancestry, though the goddess welcomes people of any ancestry. Her worshippers are found in Bissel, the Wild Coast, the Bandit Kingdoms, Ull, Ekbir, and other lands where games of skill and chance are popular. Small shrines are sometimes maintained beside gambling houses, but there are no great temples dedicated to her.

The clergy wander in search of worthy opponents, new games, and opportunities to test their skills. Many frequent gambling houses, where they earn money for themselves and the church. Others teach games for a modest fee, expose dishonest gamblers, or introduce people to contests of skill such as bowling and horseshoes. Whatever their chosen pursuit, Rudd's priests practise it with almost fanatical dedication.

Her church works closely with that of Norebo and the luckholders associated with his temples.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Rudd is expected to:

- Develop and continually refine at least one skill in which they aspire to excellence.
    
- Teach others the value of skill, practice, and sound judgement.
    
- Seek worthy challenges and games that combine chance with personal ability.
    
- Make opportunities for themselves rather than relying entirely on fortune.
    
- Oppose crude or obvious cheating, while recognizing that the goddess admires a clever deception that goes undetected.
    
- Earn money for the church through games, instruction, or other demonstrations of skill.
    
- Remember that luck is fickle, and never mistake a winning streak for invincibility.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Adventuring priests of Rudd seek new experiences, worthy opponents, and opportunities to test their skills under unfamiliar conditions. They may travel as gamblers, performers, scouts, swashbucklers, or wandering teachers. Some investigate cheating and protect honest players; others make their living at the gaming table. Many believe that adventuring itself is a game in which preparation and ability create opportunities for good fortune.

The largest shrine to Rudd is housed in the Crossed Blades Gambling Hall in Peacekeep, on Bissel's southern border. The gambling hall is operated by her clergy, and its shrine attracts patrons hoping to improve their fortunes. The church's position has become precarious under Ket's invasion and subjugation of Bissel.

Some priests celebrate the tenth day of Planting as Rudd's birthday, though this is a local custom rather than an official holy day.

### Priest of Rudd

**Ability Requirements:** Charisma 14

**Prime Requisites:** Wisdom, Charisma

**Alignment:** Chaotic Neutral, Chaotic Good

**Weapons:** Any weapon other than very large weapons; rapier and stiletto are the preferred starting weapons

**Armour:** Any; leather is preferred

**Major Spheres:** All, Chaos, Charm, Combat, Numbers, Thought, Time

**Minor Spheres:** Divination

**Granted Powers**

**Weapon Training:** Luckdealers may choose the Swashbuckler kit if they qualify for it as a fighter or thief. A Luckdealer who specializes as a swashbuckler receives two bonus weapon-proficiency slots, which must be spent on the stiletto, main-gauche, rapier, or sabre. Throughout their career, half of the Luckdealer's weapon-proficiency slots must be devoted to these four weapons. Once proficient with all four, the Luckdealer may allocate further slots freely. These rules follow the specialization options described in _The Complete Fighter's Handbook_.

**4th Level — Nahal's Reckless Dweomer:** The Luckdealer can cast _Nahal's reckless dweomer_ once per day.

**8th Level — Luck:** The Luckdealer can cast _luck_, as described in the _Tome of Magic_ as the opposite of _unluck_, once per day.

**Turn Undead:** Luckdealers cannot turn or command undead.

## Solan

Oeridian alias of Pelor

## Sotillon

**Titles:** Lady Harvest, Queen of Ease

**Alignment:** Chaotic Good, Chaotic Neutral

**Pantheon:** Oeridian

**Portfolio:** Summer, Harvest, Ease, Comfort, Satisfaction

**Symbol:** A scythe cutting wheat

Sotillon is the daughter of Velnius and the twin sister of Atroa. Although she appears older than her sister, Sotillon is actually the younger of the two. Where Atroa plants, Sotillon brings the harvest. Legend claims that when she was first born, she persuaded Kurell to do the harvesting on her behalf. The sisters rarely agree, but neither bears the other any lasting hostility.

Sotillon is the goddess of summer, harvest, comfort, and satisfaction. She delights in warmth, leisure, music, and the simple pleasures of a life free from needless hardship. Some regard her as the embodiment of laziness, especially when comparing her with Atroa's industrious clergy, but Sotillon and her followers see little reason to be ashamed of their preference for ease. Comfort is not merely a luxury: it is a condition worth preserving, and sometimes worth fighting for.

Her worship is found throughout the Flanaess, particularly along the Oeridian migration routes and in Keoland, Ket, Bissel, and parts of the former Great Kingdom. Inns sometimes maintain small shrines or statues to Sotillon, marking establishments where travellers may expect a particularly comfortable welcome. Her temples are famously plush, furnished with padded chairs, divans, thick carpets, and other comforts. Many have wide windows to admit summer breezes and fireplaces to keep the worshippers warm through winter.

#### Dogma

Seek comfort in all things, and offer comfort to those around you. Life is difficult enough without needless hardship, and no virtue is gained merely by suffering when a more pleasant way is available. Help others where you can, but do not destroy your own peace in the process. Find ways to make work less unpleasant, and ensure that those who labour for the common good are rewarded with rest and enjoyment.

Summer is the season of abundance, and the harvest is its greatest gift. When the crops ripen, celebrate the bounty and help ensure that the common folk who bring it in are fed, entertained, and comfortable. When danger threatens the ease and security of a community, it must be addressed before it destroys the conditions in which a pleasant life can flourish. Even Sotillon's followers will endure temporary discomfort when doing so protects their lasting comfort.

### The Priesthood

Sotillon's clergy includes clerics, specialty priests known as **Luxuriates**, and bards. There are no formal sects, as the church considers such divisions needless complications that create more discomfort than they resolve.

The priesthood favours relaxation, music, philosophy, poetry, and quiet contemplation. Many priests compose songs, poems, or plays; others entertain worshippers and visitors or use their magic to secure money and resources for the church. Their apparent idleness should not be mistaken for indifference. A threatened harvest, an approaching frost, or an invading army can rouse even the most indolent priest to action. They may be reluctant to inconvenience themselves, but they understand that a threat left unattended can make life far less comfortable later.

Services are generally short and peaceful, held every other Godsday and consisting of light singing or stringed music. A worshipper falling asleep during a service is considered to have paid the priesthood a great compliment. Harvest time in Reaping and the festival of Richfest are sacred periods, though celebration usually receives more attention than labour in the fields. Bedtime is regarded as the holiest time of day.

The Cathedral of Delight in Niole Dra, in the Kingdom of Keoland, is the greatest temple of Sotillon. Once built by followers of Telchur, the structure was purchased and refurbished by Sotillon's clergy. Its great octagonal worship hall, beautiful stained glass, thick carpets, and comfortable furnishings allow hundreds of worshippers to attend ceremonies in exceptional comfort. A fraternity of bards called the **Soothers** is loosely affiliated with the church. They offer music and song to those who struggle to sleep and sometimes perform during services.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Sotillon is expected to:

- Seek comfort and satisfaction in daily life, avoiding needless hardship.
    
- Offer hospitality and comfort to those in need, within reasonable limits.
    
- Find practical ways to make work less unpleasant for themselves and others.
    
- Encourage celebration during summer and harvest, ensuring that those who labour are not forgotten.
    
- Use music, song, dance, poetry, or other arts to bring pleasure and relaxation to the faithful.
    
- Preserve the comforts and security of their community against threats such as famine, frost, or invasion.
    
- Avoid creating needless divisions or burdens within the church.
    
- Recognise when temporary exertion is necessary to prevent greater suffering or discomfort later.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Priests of Sotillon usually prefer comfortable surroundings and agreeable companions, but they may adventure when danger threatens their community, livelihood, or way of life. Some seek magical means of improving daily life, securing abundant harvests, or making shelter more comfortable. Others accompany travellers to ensure that their journeys are as pleasant and safe as circumstances permit. A priest who sees a future threat to the community's comfort may act decisively, even if doing so requires an unwelcome period of hardship.

### Priest of Sotillon

**Ability Requirements:** Charisma 15

**Prime Requisites:** Wisdom, Charisma

**Alignment:** Chaotic good or chaotic neutral

**Weapons:** Blunt weapons, dagger, sickle, and other small, easily learned weapons, such as the light crossbow

**Armour:** None

**Required Proficiencies:** Etiquette

**Bonus Proficiencies:** Dancing or Singing (choose one)

**Recommended Proficiencies:** Cooking, Musical Instrument, Poetry, Artistic Ability, Brewing, Herbalism

**Major Spheres:** All, Chaos, Charm, Creation, Healing, Plant, Protection

**Minor Spheres:** Time

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Restful Sleep:** The Luxuriate sleeps soundly and requires only six hours of sleep to gain the benefits of a full night's rest. The priest may sleep longer if desired.

**3rd Level — Sleep:** The Luxuriate can cast _sleep_ once per day.

**6th Level — Leomund's Tiny Hut:** The Luxuriate can cast _Leomund's tiny hut_ once per day.

**8th Level — Leomund's Secure Shelter:** The Luxuriate can cast _Leomund's secure shelter_ once per day.

**15th Level — Mordenkainen's Magnificent Mansion:** The Luxuriate can cast _Mordenkainen's magnificent mansion_ once per day.

**Turn Undead:** Cannot turn undead.

**Special Restrictions:** Any nonhuman race may join Sotillon's church, though dwarves rarely do. Halflings and elves are particularly drawn to the faith.

## Syrul

**The Forked Tongue, The Night Hag, Oathbreaker**

**Alignment:** Neutral Evil

**Pantheon:** Suel

**Portfolio:** Lies, deceit, treachery, false promises

**Symbol:** A forked tongue

Syrul is the Suel goddess of deception and betrayal, and every successful lie, broken promise, and act of treachery is said to strengthen her. She is commonly depicted as a dirty old hag in ragged clothing, though this appearance is believed to be an illusion; beneath it, Syrul is said to be utterly nondescript.

Syrul teaches that deception is a weapon and trust a weakness to be exploited. She has little interest in lies for their own sake. A truth honestly spoken may be useful when it establishes credibility, while a promise sincerely kept may make a later betrayal more effective. Her worshippers therefore cultivate reputations for honesty when convenient, only to turn those reputations against those who trust them.

Syrul is suspicious of the other Suel powers, believing that they conceal truths from her. Although she can supposedly recognize deception instantly, she does not know whether this gift functions against the gods. She consequently keeps most of the pantheon at a distance. Pyremius is her notable exception, and the two have collaborated on numerous schemes.

#### Dogma

Protect what you know by concealing it behind deception. Your tongue is a weapon more dangerous than any blade. A carefully chosen lie can topple the mighty as easily as the weak. Give your word when doing so serves you, and break it when the promise has ceased to be useful. Trust is a weakness; betraying one who trusts you is both a victory and a lesson.

### The Priesthood

Syrul's priesthood is secretive and adaptable, and its members cultivate deception as both a practical skill and a religious discipline. Priests serve as negotiators, spies, actors, confidence artists, intermediaries, and advisers to those willing to employ their talents. Many become skilled at manipulating conversations without making an outright false statement, while others specialize in elaborate fabrications.

Temples are uncommon and deliberately difficult to recognize. Those that exist are usually hidden in cities, particularly among thieves and other criminal communities.
