**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Command:** The priest may cast _command_ once per day. In addition, the priest gains a +1 bonus to all saving throws against fire.

**3rd Level — Enchantment:** The priest may choose either _charm person or mammal_ or _heat metal_ as a granted spell.

**5th Level — Magical Vestment:** The priest may cast _magical vestment_ as a priest of the appropriate level.

**7th Level — Lightning Immunity:** The priest becomes immune to lightning and electrical damage.

**10th Level — Charm Monster:** The priest may cast _charm monster_ twice per day.

**13th Level — Symbol of Persuasion:** The priest may cast _symbol of persuasion_ as a priest of the appropriate level.

**Turn Undead:** Turn as a priest of one level lower than the character's actual level.

## Xerbo

**Titles:** Lord of the Sea, Master of Commerce, Keeper of the Deep

**Alignment:** Neutral

**Portfolio:** Sea, sailing, money, business

**Symbol:** A dragon turtle shell

Xerbo is an indifferent power who regards the sea according to a simple principle: the **law of the seas**. No creature of the sea should be favored over another merely because it is useful, intelligent, or pleasing to land-dwellers. The sea belongs to those who live within it, and Xerbo expects his followers to respect that distinction.

This attitude makes him unpopular with many who make their living from the sea, but his mercantile aspect has earned him many other followers. Merchants, sailors dissatisfied with more prosperous gods of commerce, and those who have little patience for idealized notions of business often turn to Xerbo. Cynical older sailors also favor him, seeing the sea as something to be feared and respected rather than conquered or exploited. He is particularly popular among the seafaring barbarians.

Xerbo appears as a large, moustached man with kelp-like hair, clad in armour made from the shell of a dragon turtle.

#### Dogma

The sea follows its own laws. No creature has a natural claim to dominate another simply because it is stronger, more intelligent, or more valuable to those who live upon the land. Respect the sea, understand its dangers, and take what it offers without assuming that it exists to serve you.

In commerce, value must be recognized and obligations honoured. Wealth is neither inherently virtuous nor shameful; it is a measure of what one possesses and what one is capable of exchanging. A sensible merchant neither wastes resources nor mistakes generosity for an obligation.

### The Priesthood

Xerbo's priests are sailors, merchants, shipmasters, maritime traders, and guardians of the sea. They are expected to concern themselves primarily with the sea and its creatures. Extended stays on land are discouraged, particularly when undertaken for pursuits Xerbo considers frivolous, such as exploring ruins or dungeons.

Most priests serve aboard merchant vessels, oversee trade routes, or facilitate commerce through naval and maritime ports. They are practical advisers on shipping, trade, and maritime law, but are not expected to concern themselves with the problems of land-dwellers unless those problems affect the sea.

Xerbo's priests may aid intelligent land creatures when doing so protects the sea or its creatures. They otherwise have no obligation to assist them.

The clergy never cast spells that create or manipulate fire. Spells that protect against fire, or otherwise defend against it without creating or controlling it, are permitted. Beneficial spells cast by Xerbo's priests affect sea creatures as readily as they affect humans.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Xerbo is expected to:

- Protect the sea and its natural inhabitants.
    
- Respect the law of the seas and avoid favouring one sea creature over another without cause.
    
- Remain primarily engaged in maritime activities and avoid unnecessary time ashore.
    
- Aid sailors and merchants whose activities serve legitimate maritime commerce.
    
- Protect merchant vessels and facilitate trade through ports.
    
- Treat wealth and commerce as practical matters rather than moral absolutes.
    
- Refuse to restore those killed in or while crossing the sea unless a non-sea creature was responsible for their death.
    
- Avoid spells that create or manipulate fire.
    
- Aid land-dwelling creatures only when doing so protects the sea or its inhabitants.
    
- Respect the dangers of the sea rather than treating it as a resource without limits.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Xerbo's priests rarely seek adventure for its own sake. They may accompany merchant expeditions, investigate threats to sea routes, recover lost cargo, negotiate maritime disputes, or pursue enemies who threaten the sea. They generally have little interest in inland ruins, dungeons, or other adventures that do not serve a maritime or commercial purpose.

A priest who finds an inland expedition unavoidable is expected to complete the necessary task and return to the sea rather than allowing temporary circumstances to become a new way of life.

### Priest of Xerbo

**Ability Requirements:** INT 13, WIS 12

**Alignment:** Neutral

**Weapons:** Trident (required), plus any weapon used by sailors or suitable for underwater use

**Armor:** Any nonmetal armour

**Major Spheres:** All, Animal (sea creatures only), Divination, Elemental (Water), Healing

**Minor Spheres:** Combat, Creation, Necromantic, Numbers, Plant (sea plants only), Protection

**Prohibited Spheres:** —

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Tongue of the Sea:** The priest may cast _comprehend languages_ or _detect magic_ once per day.

**3rd Level — Voice Beneath the Waves:** The priest may communicate with sea creatures as though using _speak with animals_. The ability may be used at will, but affects only creatures that normally live in the sea.

**5th Level — Breath of the Deep:** The priest can breathe water at will and suffers no penalty for speaking or casting spells while submerged.

**7th Level — Master of the Tide:** The priest may cast _lower water_ or _raise water_ once per day.

**10th Level — Master of the Winds:** The priest may cast _control winds_ once per day.

**13th Level — Call of the Deep:** Once per week, the priest may conjure a water elemental, using the normal rules for _conjure fire elemental_.

**Turn Undead:** Xerbo's priests cannot turn or command undead.

**Holy Vestments:** Xerbo's priests wear blue and green vestments designed to resemble waving sea vegetation. Decorations commonly incorporate shells, scales, kelp, and other objects associated with the sea.

## Zagyg

**Titles:** The Mad Arch-Mage, The Mad  
**Alignment:** Chaotic Neutral  
**Pantheon:** Oeridian  
**Portfolio:** Humour, Eccentricity, Occult Lore, Unpredictability  
**Symbol:** The Rune of Insanity, two parallel zigzag lines

Zagyg, once known as Zagig Yragerne, is the Mad Arch-Mage, a demipower whose interests encompass arcane mysteries, eccentricity, and the unexpected. His appearance varies, though he is often depicted as a balding, heavyset man with a broad smile and wild eyes, dressed in dark blue and silver. He is associated with Boccob, whose sponsorship helped him attain divinity.

Zagyg delights in humour that leaves its victims wondering whether they have been mocked, enlightened, or both. He pursues obscure magical knowledge and believes that predictability stifles creativity. His jokes and experiments may be elaborate, bewildering, or seemingly pointless, but he does not consider a puzzle worthwhile unless it has a solution. His interest in the occult is genuine, even when his methods appear nonsensical.

### Dogma

The world is stranger than most people imagine, and those who insist upon seeing it as orderly and predictable have already closed their minds. Seek obscure knowledge, question accepted wisdom, and never assume that the obvious explanation is the correct one. Humour is a weapon against complacency, while eccentricity is a declaration of intellectual freedom. Surprise others, but do not mistake mere cruelty for wit. Every worthwhile puzzle has a solution, however improbable it may seem.

### The Priesthood

Zagyg's priesthood is exceedingly small and poorly organized. Some accounts deny that he has priests at all, while others describe scattered individuals who claim his patronage. There is no universally recognized hierarchy, and different priests may disagree about nearly every aspect of their deity's intentions.

Those who serve Zagyg are often eccentric scholars, wandering performers, collectors of occult curiosities, or adventurers searching for lost magical knowledge. Some are genuinely learned; others appear to have misunderstood their calling entirely. It is not always possible to distinguish the two.

Priests favour humour, riddles, elaborate practical jokes, and unusual methods of teaching. They may conceal useful knowledge inside an absurd story or challenge a student with a puzzle whose solution seems unrelated to the question. They are expected to pursue arcane lore, preserve discoveries that might otherwise be lost, and resist the temptation to accept conventional explanations without examination.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Zagyg is expected to:

- Pursue unusual, obscure, or neglected knowledge, particularly concerning magic and the occult.
    
- Challenge complacency and encourage others to question assumptions.
    
- Employ humour, puzzles, and eccentricity to expose folly, though not simply to inflict suffering.
    
- Preserve interesting discoveries and make them available to suitably curious minds.
    
- Avoid becoming predictable in habits, methods, or outlook.
    
- Remember that apparent absurdity is not proof of wisdom, and that a puzzle without a solution is merely a waste of time.
    

### Adventuring Priests

Zagyg's priests adventure in search of forgotten spells, peculiar artefacts, lost libraries, and mysteries that other scholars consider beneath their attention. Some accompany adventurers because the party presents an interesting experiment in human behaviour. Others are following a clue that makes sense only to them.

Their unpredictable behaviour can make them entertaining companions and frustrating allies. A priest may possess a remarkable piece of obscure knowledge but be unable to explain why it matters. Another may spend an hour devising a complicated trick when a simple answer would suffice. Their powers, like their judgement, are rarely dependable.

### Priest of Zagyg

The following specialty priesthood is a **Reboot creation**, not a recovered AD&D 2e write-up. Its deliberately limited and unpredictable abilities reflect the obscurity of Zagyg's clergy. Characters should choose this priesthood for its roleplaying possibilities, not for mechanical advantage.

**Ability Requirements:** Intelligence 13, Wisdom 12, Charisma 12

**Prime Requisites:** Intelligence, Wisdom

**Alignment:** Chaotic Neutral

**Weapons:** Club (favoured), dagger, staff, or any weapon appropriate to the priest's eccentric pursuits

**Armour:** Any

**Major Spheres:** All, Chaos, Divination

**Minor Spheres:** Charm, Creation, Guardian, Numbers, Thought

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Odd Blessing:** Each morning, the priest must roll 1d8 on the Odd Blessings table. The result is the only blessing available to the priest that day. These effects last until the following morning unless otherwise stated. The priest cannot choose or reroll the result, even if it proves inconvenient.

|   |   |   |
|---|---|---|
  
|1d8|Odd Blessing|Effect|
|1|**Inappropriate Echo**|The priest's voice echoes conspicuously, even in places where an echo should be impossible. This has no mechanical effect, but makes quiet conversation difficult.|
|2|**Chromatic Misfortune**|The priest's hair, skin, and clothing acquire garish, shifting colours. The effect is harmless but cannot be concealed by ordinary clothing.|
|3|**Unhelpful Insight**|Once that day, the DM may provide the priest with a true but irrelevant fact about an object, person, or location currently in view.|
|4|**Misplaced Scent**|The priest smells strongly of a randomly determined substance, such as roses, brimstone, wet wool, or old cheese. The scent cannot be washed away that day.|
|5|**Errant Spark**|Whenever the priest attempts a mundane task requiring concentration, there is a 1-in-6 chance of a harmless flash of coloured light appearing nearby. The flash does not disrupt the task.|
|6|**Backward Reflection**|The priest's reflection moves a moment out of synchrony. It cannot communicate, reveal information, or act independently.|
|7|**Unbidden Rhyme**|The priest involuntarily rhymes the final word of every third sentence. This does not force the priest to speak, but cannot be suppressed while speaking.|
|8|**Nothing at All**|Zagyg provides no discernible blessing that day. The priest may suspect that this is itself a lesson.|

These effects are intentionally cosmetic or inconvenient. They cannot be used to gain a meaningful combat advantage, bypass an obstacle, or substitute for a proficiency.

**4th Level — Uncertain Wonder:** Once per day, the priest may attempt to invoke a small supernatural oddity. Roll 1d6; the priest cannot choose the result.

|   |   |
|---|---|
 
|1d6|Effect|
|1|A nearby unattended object makes a loud, ridiculous noise.|
|2|A random object within 30 feet briefly glows in an unusual colour.|
|3|The priest's voice seems to come from a random point within 30 feet for one round.|
|4|A random creature within 30 feet sneezes, hiccups, or experiences another harmless bodily interruption.|
|5|A small, harmless illusion appears for one round, depicting an arbitrary object or creature chosen by the DM.|
|6|Nothing happens. The priest is left with the uncomfortable impression that something should have happened.|

The effect cannot inflict damage, impose a meaningful penalty, compel behaviour, or provide reliable information. It is not a substitute for a spell and cannot be used to reproduce a spell's effects.

**7th Level — Occult Coincidence:** Once per tenday, the priest may ask Zagyg for a clue concerning an occult mystery, magical phenomenon, or piece of lost lore currently under investigation. The DM determines whether any clue is received. If one is provided, it must be truthful but may be symbolic, obscure, incomplete, or difficult to interpret. The power cannot reveal information Zagyg does not know or guarantee the solution to a mystery.

**10th Level — The Joke Is on You:** Once per month, the priest may attempt to turn an otherwise ordinary occurrence into a supernatural practical joke. The player describes the intended joke, and the DM determines whether Zagyg takes an interest. If he does, the effect must be harmless, brief, and incapable of causing significant material loss or injury. Zagyg may instead choose a different joke, or decline to respond at all.

This power is not an attack, a means of compelling another creature, or a reliable way to create a distraction. Its principal value is narrative.

**Turn Undead:** As a priest of the same level.

**Special Restriction:** Zagyg's specialty priests cannot exchange or substitute their granted powers for other abilities. They receive no bonus spell slots, no additional spells beyond their normal sphere access, and no general bonus to spellcasting or magical-item use. Their abilities are gifts of eccentricity, not evidence that they possess the power of their divine patron.

## Zilchus

**Titles:** The Money God, the Power of Prestige and Influence

**Alignment:** Lawful Neutral

**Pantheon:** Oeridian

**Portfolio:** Money, Business, Trade, Prestige, Influence

**Symbol:** A purse of gold

Zilchus oversees money and business, but his influence extends beyond commerce to prestige, social standing, and temporal power. He is revered by those who conduct their affairs fairly, scrupulously, and honestly; merchants who depend upon deceit and exploitation do not look to him for favour.

Zilchus appears as a middle-aged, smiling man with thick, curly brown hair, brown eyes, and tanned skin. His manner is dignified, and his rich clothing displays wealth without ostentation. He carries a purse full of gold and a flail with which to chastise the dishonest.

His faith has long been widespread throughout the central Flanaess. Although the rise of martial deities has somewhat diminished his standing as a power of prestige and influence, rulers continue to value his priests for their honesty, diplomatic skill, and ability to mediate disputes. Zilchus is particularly revered among the well-to-do and the nobility, rather than among common folk.

#### Dogma

Wealth, influence, and prestige are earned through honest dealings, sound judgement, and the fulfilment of obligations. A bargain is only as valuable as the trust on which it rests, and a reputation for integrity is worth more than a short-lived profit gained through deceit. Those who possess wealth or influence should exercise them responsibly, honour their agreements, and recognise that their standing depends upon the confidence of others. Dishonesty undermines both commerce and the social order that sustains it.

### The Priesthood

Priests of Zilchus often occupy positions of temporal influence. Many are merchants, nobles, financiers, or officials, and their worldly success is generally regarded as evidence of their competence and worth. They cultivate diplomacy and mediation, seeking to resolve disputes through negotiation and mutually acceptable agreements rather than force. Their reputation for honesty allows them to serve as advisers to rulers and arbiters in commercial and political affairs.

The priesthood generally maintains cordial relations with the followers of Rao, whose emphasis on peace and spiritual reflection sometimes leads them to chide Zilchus's clergy for their worldliness. Services to Zilchus commonly include the burning of incense, small offerings of goods, sermons, and homilies on honest conduct and responsible dealings.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Zilchus is expected to:

- Conduct personal and professional affairs honestly, honouring agreements and obligations.
    
- Promote fair dealing and discourage fraud, deceit, and dishonest commerce.
    
- Encourage sound judgement in financial matters and the responsible use of wealth.
    
- Mediate disputes and pursue diplomatic solutions to commercial, political, and personal conflicts.
    
- Preserve their own reputation for integrity, recognising that their conduct reflects upon the faith.
    
- Advise merchants, nobles, and rulers on matters of trade, influence, and negotiation.
    
- Respect legitimate contracts and agreements, while opposing those founded upon fraud or coercion.
    
- Use wealth and influence responsibly, without mistaking social prestige for proof of moral worth.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Priests of Zilchus may adventure as merchants, diplomats, financial agents, negotiators, or advisers to powerful patrons. Some travel to establish trade agreements, investigate commercial disputes, protect legitimate enterprises, or recover goods and debts unlawfully taken. Others accompany expeditions to manage finances and negotiations, or seek to restore the reputation of a merchant house or ruler whose standing has been damaged by corruption. They generally prefer persuasion and lawful settlement to violence, but may act decisively when dishonest dealings threaten their interests or those under their protection.

### Priest of Zilchus

**Ability Requirements:** Wisdom 13, Charisma 13

**Prime Requisites:** Wisdom, Charisma

**Alignment:** Lawful neutral

**Weapons:** Any blunt weapon; flail (1st level). Alternatively, short sword.

**Armour:** Chain

**Major Spheres:** Charm, Divination, Guardian, Law

**Minor Spheres:** Healing, Necromantic, Protection, Summoning, Travellers

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Mental Resistance:** The priest gains a +2 bonus to saving throws against mind-controlling or mind-reading spells, including _ESP_, _hypnotism_, _magic jar_, and _magic mirror_.

**3rd Level — Appraisal:** The priest can determine the value of goods to within 5% of their actual value. This ability does not apply to rarities, unique objects, or items for which no reliable market value exists.

**7th Level — Increased Charisma:** The priest's Charisma score increases by 1, subject to the reboot's general rules for ability-score limits and derived statistics.

**9th Level — True Seeing:** The priest gains access to _true seeing_, subject to the reboot's general rules for granted powers.

**Turn Undead:** Cannot turn undead.

## Zodal

**Titles:** Bestower of Hope, The Gray Son, The Gentle Hand

**Alignment:** Neutral Good

**Portfolio:** Mercy, Hope, Benevolence, Compassion

**Symbol:** A bright yellow stylized butterfly; also depicted as a man's hand partially wrapped in gray cloth.

Zodal is the god of mercy, hope, and benevolence, a kindly but stern power who seeks to alleviate suffering and turn anger away from vengeance. A servant of Rao, he believes that even those consumed by hatred and cruelty may yet change if shown sufficient patience and compassion. He does not mistake kindness for weakness, nor does he expect the suffering to endure without aid while others are protected from consequence. Rather, he seeks to end suffering without creating more of it.

Zodal is commonly depicted as a careworn man in simple grey robes, with large, gentle hands. His worship is widespread throughout the Flanaess, particularly among those who minister to the wounded, aid the destitute, reconcile enemies, or seek to reform those who have done wrong.

#### Dogma

Only through experiencing kindness and mercy can the cruel be turned from their path. Do not allow anger, hatred, or despair to master you, even when you have every reason to feel them. You are responsible for your own conduct, and by choosing compassion over vengeance, you demonstrate that another way is possible.

The world is full of suffering, but this is no reason to abandon hope. Every kindness may ease a burden; every act of mercy may prevent another wrong; every person treated with dignity may discover a reason to change. Persist even when your efforts appear fruitless, for the measure of a good deed is not solely whether it succeeds.

Mercy does not require allowing the cruel to continue harming others. Restrain the violent, protect the vulnerable, and seek to reform those who have done wrong. If an enemy asks for mercy, it must be given, but kindness need not mean surrendering the innocent to further injury. Take life only when every other course has failed or when the immediate defence of innocent life leaves no alternative.

### The Priesthood

Zodal's priesthood is found throughout most of the Flanaess, though it is absent or severely restricted in lands hostile to its teachings, particularly territories controlled by Iuz and the Scarlet Brotherhood and the northern barbarian lands. His priests live simply, often owning little more than they can carry. Those responsible for temples and permanent charitable works may maintain the possessions necessary to fulfil those duties.

Priests of Zodal serve as healers, counsellors, mediators, teachers, and protectors. They minister to the wounded in war, comfort those who have suffered loss, aid the poor, and seek to persuade wrongdoers to abandon cruelty. Their work takes them into places where hatred and despair are most deeply entrenched. They may accompany armies to tend the injured, enter troubled communities to reconcile feuding groups, or confront those whose actions threaten the innocent.

A priest of Zodal is trained to end violence without unnecessary injury. Priests may not initiate a fight. When confronted by violence, they should first seek to defuse the situation, then disarm or incapacitate an aggressor with the least possible pain. They are taught the Butterfly Style of martial arts, along with the use of quarterstaffs, sword-breakers, nets, and other weapons suited to restraint and subdual. They are expected to accept some risk to themselves when doing so will spare others greater suffering, but reckless self-sacrifice that accomplishes nothing is not inherently virtuous.

Zodal's teachings forbid the taking of life unless every other option has been exhausted or the life of the priest or another innocent is in immediate danger. An enemy who asks for mercy must receive it. This does not mean that a priest must trust a dangerous adversary without precautions, nor that an aggressor must be permitted to resume harming others. Priests may restrain, imprison, disarm, or otherwise prevent further wrongdoing while seeking a path to reform.

Some communities interpret these teachings with extreme selflessness, placing themselves between attackers and their victims even when they could defend themselves. Others adopt a more confrontational approach, deliberately placing themselves in a position where aggressors strike first before restraining them. Such practices are controversial even among Zodal's faithful, and the goddess's tolerance of a particular practice should not be taken as universal approval.

Zodal's temples are generally modest establishments offering aid, shelter, counsel, and instruction. His priests also wander widely, carrying their ministry to those who would not otherwise seek it. Their services often resemble rhythmic festivals of singing and dancing, followed by sermons on hope, generosity, kindness, and the obligations people owe one another. Their colourful clothing and musical talents sometimes cause them to be mistaken for entertainers or bards.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Zodal is expected to:

- Relieve suffering wherever practical, offering aid to the wounded, poor, sick, and distressed without regard to their station or past deeds.
    
- Refrain from initiating violence and seek to resolve disputes through persuasion, mediation, and peaceful means.
    
- Restrain aggressors with the least necessary force, favouring disarmament and subdual over injury or death.
    
- Grant mercy to an enemy who sincerely asks for it, while continuing to protect those threatened by that enemy.
    
- Seek the reform of wrongdoers through instruction, restitution, and constructive service rather than vengeance.
    
- Preserve hope in the face of cruelty and despair, demonstrating through personal conduct that kindness need not be weakness.
    
- Live simply and avoid the accumulation of personal wealth, except where possessions are necessary to maintain a temple or fulfil charitable responsibilities.
    
- Use the Butterfly Style and other appropriate techniques to protect others while minimizing harm.
    
- Never take a life unless every other course has failed or immediate danger to innocent life leaves no alternative.
    

#### Adventuring Priests

Priests of Zodal adventure to relieve suffering, protect the vulnerable, and bring their teachings to places where hatred and violence threaten to overwhelm hope. They may accompany armies to tend the wounded, intervene in feuds, protect refugees, or attempt to reform a notorious criminal or cruel ruler. Some seek artefacts of good, such as the Crook of Rao, while others seek to destroy objects that perpetuate misery, corruption, or needless suffering.

A priest may be required to confront an enemy who cannot be reasoned with, and must learn to distinguish perseverance from futility. Zodal does not demand that his followers allow preventable harm to continue. When peaceful persuasion fails, restraint and decisive action may be necessary; the priest's duty is to accomplish what protection requires without allowing hatred to dictate the means.

### Priest of Zodal

**Ability Requirements:** STR 12, DEX 12, WIS 9, CHA 13.

**Alignment:** Neutral Good.

**Weapons:** Butterfly Style, sword-breaker, net, quarterstaff, lasso, and other weapons primarily intended for subdual.

**Armour:** None.

**Major Spheres:** All, Astral, Charm, Divination, Healing, Protection, Wards.

**Minor Spheres:** Creation, Guardian, Law, Summoning.

**Additional Spells:** _Light, Continual Light._

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Uplifting Song:** A priest can raise, but never lower, the morale of a crowd through singing and instrumental performance, duplicating the relevant bard ability. The priest receives one Musical Instrument proficiency without spending a proficiency slot.

**1st Level — Butterfly Style:** The priest receives one bonus weapon proficiency slot every three levels, beginning at 1st level, which must be spent on the Butterfly Style. These bonus slots are in addition to the priest's normal weapon proficiency slots.

The priest also gains additional attacks when using the Butterfly Style according to the following progression:

- **Levels 1–5:** Two attacks.
    
- **Levels 6–9:** Three attacks.
    
- **Levels 10–13:** Four attacks.
    
- **Level 14 and above:** Five attacks.
    

These additional attacks apply only when using the Butterfly Style. The style represents a specialised system of unarmed techniques and controlled movements intended to disarm, redirect, and incapacitate an opponent rather than inflict lethal injury.

**1st Level — Suggestion:** The priest may cast _Suggestion_ (as the 3rd-level wizard spell) twice per day. The casting time is 1, and the components are verbal and the priest's holy symbol.

**5th Level — Emotion Control:** The priest may cast _Emotion Control_ twice per day, but only to produce the Hope effect.

**7th Level — Hold Person:** The priest may cast _Hold Person_ twice per day, with a casting time of 1. The priest's holy symbol is the only material component required. Otherwise, the spell functions as the 2nd-level priest spell. Any additional _Hold Person_ spells the priest prepares are cast normally.

**11th Level — Quest:** The priest may cast _Quest_ once per week. Permission from a senior priest should generally be obtained before using this power. Quests imposed through this ability must serve Zodal's principles and must never deliberately place the subject in harm's way. Appropriate quests might require a wrongdoer to raise money legally for the poor, provide food to the hungry, or undertake a sustained effort to treat others with kindness and respect.

**14th Level — Mass Suggestion:** The priest may cast _Mass Suggestion_ (as the wizard spell) once per week. This is a powerful ability entrusted to the priest for exceptional circumstances. Using it to manipulate others for personal gain, compel cruelty, or undermine the principles of mercy and benevolence is a serious violation of Zodal's teachings.

**Turn Undead:** As a priest of three levels lower than the character's actual level.

## Zuoken

**Master of the Fist, Perfected One, Lord of Mind and Body**

**Alignment:** Neutral

**Portfolio:** Martial arts, physical and mental mastery, discipline, psionics

**Symbol:** A striking fist

**Favored Weapon:** Nunchaku

Zuoken is the Baklunish demigod of physical and mental mastery. Once a mortal martial artist, he attained such perfection of body and mind that Xan Yae raised him into godhood. He remains closely associated with his former teacher and is regarded by her followers as the living proof that the principles of Internal Peace, Perpetual Harmony, and mastery of the self can be carried beyond mortal limitations.

Unlike gods who grant strength to their followers, Zuoken teaches his worshippers to **develop their own strength**. His priests are expected to cultivate their bodies through exercise and combat, their minds through meditation and study, and their wills through deliberate exposure to hardship. Physical ability without mental discipline is regarded as incomplete, while intellect without mastery of the body is similarly deficient.

Zuoken is particularly concerned with creatures possessing psionic or other unusual mental abilities. Such powers are neither inherently good nor evil, but uncontrolled mental power can harm its wielder and those around them. His priests therefore seek out psionicists, wild talents, mystics, and others whose minds possess extraordinary abilities, offering training where possible and protection when necessary.

Zuoken is not a god of conquest. Combat is a means of testing and refining the self, not an excuse for cruelty or domination. A victory achieved through discipline and precision is worth more than one achieved through overwhelming force.

#### Dogma

Master yourself.

The body must be trained until it responds without hesitation. The mind must be disciplined until fear, anger, desire, and pain no longer dictate its actions. The will must be strengthened until hardship cannot easily break it.

Do not seek strength merely to possess it. Strength exists to overcome weakness, protect those who cannot protect themselves, and continue the work of self-perfection.

Accept hardship as a teacher. Do not deliberately seek suffering for its own sake, but neither should you flee from difficulty merely because it is uncomfortable.

Combat reveals the truth about the self. When confronted with danger, observe your own reactions. Fear reveals weakness. Anger reveals a lack of control. Pride reveals attachment to the self. Master these things rather than allowing them to master you.

The greatest opponent is the self that you were yesterday.

### The Priesthood

Zuoken's priests, commonly called **Fists of Zuoken**, are martial ascetics who divide their lives between physical training, meditation, instruction, and service. Many live in monasteries associated with the Way of the Lotus, while others wander from community to community seeking worthy teachers and students.

The priesthood has no requirement that its members avoid violence. Indeed, martial training is central to the faith. A priest is expected to be capable of defending themselves and others, but violence performed out of anger, vanity, or cruelty is considered a failure of discipline.

Fists of Zuoken frequently train warriors, monks, and other martial artists. They may also act as teachers and guardians of people with psionic abilities, particularly those who have difficulty controlling their powers.

Zuoken's priests are expected to maintain their physical conditioning throughout their lives. A priest who deliberately allows themselves to become weak through neglect has violated the fundamental principles of the faith, although illness, injury, age, and other circumstances beyond the priest's control are not considered failures.

#### Duties and Obligations

A priest of Zuoken is expected to:

- train the body regularly;
    
- cultivate discipline, patience, and mental control;
    
- practice meditation and other exercises of concentration;
    
- continually seek to overcome personal weaknesses;
    
- teach useful martial and mental disciplines to worthy students;
    
- protect those whose unusual mental abilities place them in danger;
    
- use physical force with precision rather than anger or cruelty;
    
- accept legitimate challenges to their abilities;
    
- endure reasonable hardship without surrendering to fear or despair;
    
- avoid boasting about accomplishments that should speak for themselves.
    

A priest who loses a contest or suffers defeat is expected to examine the experience rather than respond with resentment. Failure is not dishonor if it becomes a lesson; refusing to learn from failure is.

#### Adventuring Priests

Fists of Zuoken adventure to test themselves, protect students and travelers, investigate unusual manifestations of psionic power, recover lost martial teachings, and confront opponents whose abilities provide a meaningful challenge.

Some deliberately travel without possessions beyond what they can carry, regarding dependence upon wealth and equipment as another potential weakness. Others accompany adventuring groups because experience in unfamiliar circumstances provides challenges that cannot be reproduced within a monastery.

A Fist of Zuoken is particularly likely to intervene when someone with dangerous mental powers is being exploited, persecuted, or allowed to harm others through lack of control. The priest's preferred solution is education and discipline, but they will restrain a dangerous individual when necessary.

### Priest of Zuoken

**Ability Requirements:** Strength 11, Dexterity 12, Wisdom 13

**Alignment:** Neutral, Lawful Neutral, or Neutral Good

**Weapons:** Nunchaku, quarterstaff, dart, dagger, unarmed combat

**Armor:** None

**Major Spheres:** All, Combat, Healing, Protection, Thought

**Minor Spheres:** Divination, Time, Travelers

**Prohibited Spheres:** None

**Granted Powers**

**1st Level — Unarmored Defense:** A priest of Zuoken gains a +1 bonus to Armor Class for every three levels of experience attained, rounded down. This bonus applies only while the priest wears no armor and carries no shield.

**1st Level — Martial Discipline:** The priest may use unarmed combat according to the martial arts rules. In addition, the priest may use the nunchaku as a favored weapon.

**1st Level — Mind Over Matter:** Once per day, the priest may enter a state of complete mental concentration and immediately end one effect causing physical or mental incapacitation, including magical sleep, paralysis, stunning, or similar effects. This ability does not restore hit points or remove an underlying disease or injury.

**5th Level — Disciplined Mind:** The priest gains a +2 bonus to saving throws against effects that directly influence, read, or control the mind.

If the campaign uses psionics, the priest may instead attempt to acquire a Wild Talent when the character is created, gaining a +2 bonus to the appropriate Wild Talent check. This does not grant a Wild Talent automatically.

**7th Level — Physical Perfection:** Once per day, the priest may enter a state of heightened physical control for 1 round per level. During this period, the priest gains a +2 bonus to Strength and Dexterity, subject to the normal ability-score maximums.

**10th Level — Purity of Body:** The priest becomes immune to all nonmagical diseases and gains a +2 bonus to saving throws against magical diseases, poisons, and effects that directly weaken or incapacitate the body.

**13th Level — Perfected Strike:** Once per day, the priest may make a single unarmed or nunchaku attack with perfect concentration. The attack gains a +4 bonus to the attack roll and inflicts maximum normal damage. A successful hit also forces the target to save vs. paralyzation or be stunned for 1 round.

### Holy Days and Ceremonies

Zuoken's worship has few conventional holy days. His followers instead observe **Days of Discipline**, during which ordinary activities are reduced to the minimum necessary while the faithful devote themselves to meditation, physical training, and study.

The most important observance occurs when a student completes a significant stage of training. Rather than receiving a ceremonial title or material reward, the student is expected to demonstrate the discipline they have acquired before their teachers and fellow practitioners.

The faithful commonly pray at dawn, before beginning physical training, and at dusk, when training gives way to meditation.

### Relationship with Xan Yae

Zuoken's relationship with Xan Yae is that of **student and teacher, disciple and exemplar**. Xan Yae represents the principles of harmony and mastery; Zuoken demonstrates what those principles can produce when pursued to their ultimate conclusion.

Her priests generally regard Zuoken with considerable respect, while his Fists remain students of Xan Yae's deeper teachings. The two faiths therefore overlap heavily in monasteries and martial traditions, but their emphasis differs: **Xan Yae teaches the harmony of mind, body, and world; Zuoken teaches the perfection of the individual who seeks that harmony.**

# Chapter 3:The Old Faith

The Old Faith is among the oldest religious traditions of the Flanaess, predating the arrival and expansion of the peoples whose gods now dominate much of the region. Its origins are closely associated with the Flan, among whom reverence for the natural world developed into a body of customs, observances, and teachings concerning the relationship between living things and the world they inhabit. Although its influence has diminished in some regions, the Old Faith remains deeply rooted in rural communities, ancient woodlands, and places where the rhythms of nature continue to shape mortal life.

The Old Faith is not a pantheon in the usual sense, nor is it simply the worship of gods associated with nature. **Its central belief is that Nature exists as a power and order in its own right, distinct from the deities who shaped the world or exercise influence over its various parts.** The natural world is not merely the property of the gods, nor does it require the constant intervention of divine patrons to sustain it. It possesses its own cycles, relationships, and means of renewal, which together form what adherents call the Balance.

The Balance encompasses the interdependence of living things, the changing seasons, the renewal of the land, and the continuing existence of the world itself. It does not promise peace, comfort, or fairness to individual creatures. Predators kill prey, storms destroy settlements, droughts bring famine, and old growth gives way to new. Such events may cause suffering, but suffering alone does not establish that the Balance has been disturbed. The question is whether the relationships and processes that sustain the natural world can continue.

The Old Faith therefore does not divide the world neatly into benevolent and malevolent forces, nor does it regard every natural process as something to be preserved unchanged. Death makes room for life, destruction can permit renewal, and intervention may be necessary to prevent a local disturbance from spreading. The adherent's task is to understand these relationships and act with due regard for their consequences.

**The Balance is not a commandment imposed by a god. It is the condition that allows Nature to endure, and its preservation is the responsibility of those who understand it.**

#### The Balance and the Living World

The Old Faith teaches that mortals are part of Nature, not creatures standing apart from it. Human communities, their farms and settlements, and even their conflicts and ambitions belong to the living world. The cultivation of land, the harvesting of timber, and the hunting of animals are not inherently violations of the Balance. Such activities become objectionable when they disregard necessity, destroy the conditions upon which life depends, or pursue immediate gain at the expense of continued renewal.

The Balance is understood through relationships rather than universal prohibitions. A forest may need protection from excessive logging, while a community may need timber to survive the winter. A predator may threaten livestock, yet its destruction may create another imbalance. A marsh may be valuable habitat, but a settlement may reasonably drain part of it to prevent disease or provide arable land. The Old Faith offers no simple rule that settles every such question.

Instead, its adherents are expected to consider the needs of the whole: the creatures and communities involved, the resources available, the likely consequences of intervention, and the capacity of the land to recover. The right course may require restraint, active intervention, or the acceptance of a loss that cannot be prevented without causing greater harm.

This understanding extends beyond individual ecosystems. The Balance encompasses the changing seasons, the movements of the heavens, and the larger cycles that govern life on Oerth. The motions of the sun and moons, the appearance of the stars, and the turning of the seasons are therefore of particular religious importance. These events provide both a means of understanding the natural order and occasions for ritual observance.

#### Worship and Observance

The Old Faith has no single body of scripture, universal creed, or central authority governing all its adherents. Its teachings are preserved through oral tradition, customary rites, the study of Nature, and the accumulated knowledge of those who maintain its practices. Customs vary by region, reflecting the character of the local environment and the communities that have lived alongside it.

Many adherents honour sacred groves, springs, ancient trees, standing stones, stone circles, and other places where the natural world is held to possess particular significance. Such sites may serve as places of assembly, instruction, seasonal observance, or the marking of celestial events. Some are maintained with care; others remain largely untouched, their significance known only to those who understand the traditions associated with them.

The changing seasons provide the framework for many observances. Planting and harvest, the turning of winter and summer, and significant astronomical events are occasions for gatherings, ceremonies, and customary offerings. These observances do not necessarily seek favour from a divine patron. They may instead mark the passage of time, acknowledge the gifts and dangers of a season, or reaffirm a community's responsibilities to the land upon which it depends.

Certain plants, particularly mistletoe, oak, and holly, hold special significance in druidic practice. Their importance is not merely symbolic: particular rites and traditions associate them with the gathering and use of natural energies. Druids preserve the methods by which such materials are obtained and prepared, treating the knowledge as a sacred responsibility rather than a collection of interchangeable rituals.

Not all adherents observe every custom, and not every community follows the same calendar or recognizes the same sacred places. **The Old Faith is a living tradition of practice and understanding, not a uniform church whose authority depends upon strict adherence to a single set of rites.**

#### Druids of the Old Faith

Druids are the principal custodians and interpreters of the Old Faith, but they are not its only adherents. Rural communities may maintain its seasonal observances without possessing a druid of their own. Farmers, hunters, herbalists, wise folk, and others whose lives depend upon knowledge of the natural world may preserve its customs and teachings. Some recognize particular nature deities as patrons, while others participate in the Old Faith without any exclusive devotion to a god.

What distinguishes a druid is not simply reverence for Nature, but a formal commitment to understanding and maintaining the Balance. Druids study the relationships that sustain the natural world, preserve the traditions through which that knowledge is transmitted, and accept responsibility for acting upon what they learn. Their authority arises from their knowledge, experience, and position within the druidic tradition rather than from membership in a universal priesthood of nature gods.

The responsibilities of a druid extend beyond the protection of wilderness. A druid may guide a rural community through famine, advise farmers on the use of land, mediate disputes over hunting grounds, protect a watershed, or oppose practices that threaten the long-term survival of an ecosystem. In another circumstance, the same druid may conclude that a settlement must be relocated, a herd reduced, or a forest deliberately cleared to prevent a greater disaster.

Druids are expected to consider their responsibilities at several levels. They must first govern their own conduct, taking from Nature what they need without needless exploitation. They must then consider the survival and welfare of their community, followed by the health of the local environment and the wider region. Beyond these lie the larger concerns of the world and, for the most accomplished druids, the planes beyond it.

These responsibilities may conflict. Protecting a community can harm a local ecosystem, while preserving a habitat may impose severe costs upon those who depend upon it. A druid cannot always satisfy every obligation simultaneously. The task is to understand the consequences, make a considered judgement, and prevent a local necessity from becoming a greater and more enduring disturbance.

The Old Faith's emphasis upon neutrality follows from this understanding. Neutrality does not require indifference to suffering, nor does it forbid acts of courage, compassion, or sacrifice. A druid may defend the weak, oppose wanton destruction, and work tirelessly to preserve a community. Such conduct does not make the druid inherently good, just as the acceptance of necessary loss does not make the druid evil. The measure of the action is its place within the Balance, not its conformity to a single moral ideal.

#### The Old Faith and the Gods

The Old Faith recognizes the existence and power of the gods. It does not require its adherents to deny them, nor does it assume that every deity's interests are opposed to those of Nature. Many gods are associated with the land, the seasons, plants, animals, weather, or the cycles of life and death. Some actively protect aspects of the natural world; others exercise authority over particular forces without necessarily concerning themselves with the Balance as a whole.

A druid may honour a deity, participate in its rites, or even serve within its clergy when the deity's teachings and obligations are compatible with the druid's understanding of the Balance. Beory and Obad-hai are particularly closely associated with the Old Faith, but they do not own Nature, nor are they the exclusive source of druidic authority. Other deities may also be recognized or served, depending upon their interests and the commitments of the individual druid.

This distinction is fundamental. **A druid's power derives from Nature and the druid's attunement to its forces, not from the favour of a patron deity.** A druid who serves a god may understand that deity as a guardian, embodiment, or advocate of some part of the natural order, but the druid's responsibility to the Balance remains independent of that relationship.

The distinction also separates the Old Faith from ordinary deity-centred priesthoods. A priest of a nature deity serves that deity's interests and observes the obligations of its faith. A druid serves the Balance, even when doing so requires disagreement with a god or its followers. A deity's command does not automatically justify an action that would cause a greater disturbance, just as the refusal to follow a particular divine instruction does not necessarily constitute impiety in the eyes of the Old Faith.

The relationship between druids and other religions is consequently determined by compatibility and circumstance rather than by a universal rule. Druids may cooperate with temples whose work supports the Balance, maintain cordial relations with communities that honour different gods, or oppose religious institutions whose activities threaten the natural order. They need not reject the gods to recognize that divine authority and the Balance are not the same thing.

#### Dogma

Nature is greater than any one creature, community, or god's concern for a particular part of the world. All living things are bound together, and the continuance of life depends upon relationships that no single creature can command or fully control.

Understand the Balance before you presume to defend it. Take what you need, but do not destroy without necessity. Preserve what must endure, allow what must change, and recognize that loss and renewal are inseparable parts of the natural order.

Protect your community, but do not mistake its immediate desires for the needs of the world. Defend the land upon which life depends, but do not assume that every change is a corruption or every untouched wilderness a sacred necessity. Consider the consequences of action and inaction alike.

Respect the gods and understand their interests, but do not mistake their authority for the Balance itself. A deity may guard a part of Nature without encompassing the whole, and even a well-intentioned command may have consequences beyond its patron's concern.

Above all, remember that **the Balance is not maintained by wishing the world to remain unchanged, but by understanding what must endure, what must change, and what must be allowed to pass.**

#### Duties and Obligations

An adherent of the Old Faith is expected to:

- learn and preserve the customs, teachings, and seasonal observances of their local tradition;
    
- respect the natural relationships upon which their own survival and that of their community depend;
    
- take from Nature according to need and avoid needless waste or destruction;
    
- consider the long-term consequences of hunting, farming, settlement, resource extraction, and other activities that affect the living world;
    
- recognize that communities are part of Nature and that their survival may be a legitimate concern of the Balance;
    
- preserve sacred sites and the knowledge associated with them;
    
- observe significant seasonal and astronomical events according to local custom;
    
- respect those who preserve knowledge of plants, animals, weather, and the natural cycles;
    
- support efforts to correct disturbances that threaten the continued health of a local ecosystem or the wider natural order;
    
- recognize when intervention is necessary and when restraint is the wiser course;
    
- cooperate with other adherents and compatible religious traditions where doing so serves the Balance;
    
- respect the gods without assuming that their individual interests supersede the needs of Nature as a whole;
    
- pass on the knowledge and practices of the Old Faith to future generations.
    

These duties do not establish a universal code of conduct for every circumstance. The Balance must be understood in context, and the proper response to a particular problem may differ according to the environment, the needs of the community, and the likely consequences of intervention.

#### Adventuring Druids and Adherents

Adherents of the Old Faith may travel beyond their home communities for many reasons. Some seek knowledge of unfamiliar environments, investigate disturbances in the natural order, recover lost lore, or visit sacred sites. Others accompany travellers to protect vulnerable lands, mediate disputes over resources, or prevent the actions of a settlement or powerful individual from causing lasting damage.

Druids may also adventure because the Balance cannot be maintained from a single grove or village. A blight may spread across several regions, a river may carry pollution far beyond its source, or an unnatural force may threaten the natural cycles of an entire land. Such problems require knowledge, cooperation, and action beyond the limits of any one community.

An adventuring druid is not required to oppose civilization, nor must every journey be justified by the defence of wilderness. The acquisition of knowledge, the protection of a threatened people, and the correction of a local disturbance may all serve the Balance. Equally, a druid may have to oppose companions whose actions threaten an ecosystem or accept that the needs of a community sometimes conflict with the preservation of a particular place.

Other adherents may travel for less formal reasons: to learn from distant communities, preserve old customs, discover unfamiliar plants and animals, or maintain relationships between settlements and the lands upon which they depend. Their understanding of the Old Faith may be less comprehensive than that of a druid, but their knowledge and work can still be valuable.

**An adherent of the Old Faith does not travel to impose a universal prohibition upon change, but to understand the world, recognize the relationships that sustain it, and act when those relationships are threatened.**
